**2.17.3**  Entering the first courtyard, in the second he saw aged Brāhmaṇas learned in the Vedas, honored by the king.

**2.17.4**  Rāma bowed to those elders, and in the third courtyard he saw women — both old and young — intent on guarding the door.

**2.17.5**  Those delighted women greeted him and then swiftly entered the house and announced to Rāma's mother the joy of his arrival.

**2.17.6**  Queen Kausalyā too, having kept vigil through the night in concentrated prayer, at dawn performed worship of Viṣṇu, she who desired her son's welfare.

**2.17.7**  She, clad in a silk garment, joyful, ever devoted to vows, performed oblations into the sacred fire with mantras, having made all auspicious rites.

**2.17.8**  Then Rāma, entering his mother's auspicious inner apartments, saw his mother there engaged in making offerings to the sacred fire.

**2.17.9**  She, seeing her son who had long been awaited, the delight of his mother, now arrived, rushed forward overjoyed, as a mare rushes toward her young colt.

**2.17.10**  Kausalyā, out of maternal love for her son, spoke to that Rāghava, her invincible child, these words that were both affectionate and beneficial.

**2.17.11**  "May you attain the long life, glory, and dharma proper to the lineage of the noble, dharma-disciplined, great-souled royal sages."

**2.17.12**  "Behold, O Rāghava, your father the king, true to his pledges — this very day the righteous-souled one shall consecrate you as crown prince."

**2.17.13**  Rāghava, extending his joined palms toward his mother, spoke to her — he who was naturally humble and now bent down in reverence.

**2.17.14**  "O queen, you surely do not know that a great peril is at hand — one that will bring sorrow to you, to Vaidehī, and to Lakṣmaṇa."

**2.17.15**  "For fourteen years I shall dwell in the lonely forest, living on honey, roots, and fruits, having forsaken meat in the manner of a sage."

**2.17.16**  "The great king grants the crown-princeship to Bharata, and banishes me — as an ascetic — to the Daṇḍaka forest."

**2.17.17**  Seeing her — unaccustomed to sorrow — fallen like a plantain tree, insensible, Rāma raised his mother up.

**2.17.18**  Having turned and raised her up — wretched as an overburdened mare — her every limb covered with dust, he stroked her with his hand.

**2.17.19**  She, afflicted with sorrow, she who was accustomed to happiness, spoke to Rāghava who sat beside her — that tiger among men — while Lakṣmaṇa listened.

**2.17.20**  "O Rāghava, my son, if you had not been born for my grief, then I, being childless, would have seen no greater sorrow than that."

**2.17.21**  "For a barren woman has only one human sorrow — the grief of 'I am without a child' — truly there is no other sorrow, my son."

**2.17.22**  "It was with the hope that I might behold good fortune or happiness through a husband's valor or through a son that I persevered, placing my faith in Rāma."

**2.17.23**  "I, though the chief among co-wives, shall be forced to hear many unpleasant, heart-piercing words from those junior co-wives — what greater sorrow can there be for any woman?"

**2.17.24**  "Even with you present near me, I was thus rejected and ignored — what then when you are gone, my dear? Death alone is certain for me."

**2.17.25**  "Whoever serves me or follows my wishes, upon looking toward Kaikeyī's son, that person does not address me."

**2.17.26**  "Seventeen years since your birth have passed, O Rāghava, while I waited in expectation for the end of my sorrows."

**2.17.27**  "Through fastings, austerities, and many hardships — reared with difficulty you have been raised in vain by me in my misfortune."

**2.17.28**  "Firm indeed I deem my heart, that it does not break apart, like the bank of a great river touched by the new flood-waters of the rains."

**2.17.29**  "Surely death does not exist for me, nor is there room for me in Yama's realm, since Death does not this very day carry me off by force, as a lion carries off a weeping doe."

**2.17.30**  "My heart is surely firm as iron, that it does not shatter, that upon this earth it does not split asunder with this body given over to such grief — for truly, untimely death does not exist."

**2.17.31**  "This is my sorrow: that my vows, gifts, self-restraints, and austerities practiced for the sake of offspring are utterly fruitless — like good seed sown in barren soil."

**2.17.32**  "If one could attain untimely death by one's own will when worn away by great sorrow, I would have gone this very day to the assembly of the dead, like a cow bereft of her own calf."

**2.17.33**  Deeply unhappy, overcome with indignation, she lamented much, gazing upon Rāghava, perceiving that great calamity — like a kiṃnarī looking upon her son bound in chains.

**2.18.1**  To Kausalyā, Rāma's mother, lamenting thus, Lakṣmaṇa, stricken with sorrow, spoke words befitting that moment.

**2.18.2**  "It does not please me either, O noble lady, that Rāghava should go to the forest, abandoning royal splendor, having been subjected to the words of a woman."

**2.18.3**  "A king who has changed in character, who is old, and who has been overpowered by the senses — what might such a one not say when provoked while in the grip of passion?"

**2.18.4**  "I see no offense in him, nor any fault of that kind, on account of which Rāghava should be banished from the kingdom to dwell in the forest."

**2.18.5**  "I see no one in the world — not even speaking behind his back — no man, enemy or estranged, who would name a fault in him."

**2.18.6**  "Who, bearing in mind dharma, would abandon without cause a son who is godlike, upright, self-restrained, tender even toward his enemies?"

**2.18.7**  "What son, keeping in mind the conduct proper to kings, would take to heart this word of a king who has reverted to childhood?"

**2.18.8**  "As long as no person knows of this matter, until then, establish your authority while it rests in yourself — act jointly with me."

**2.18.9**  "With me standing at your side bearing my bow, protecting you, O Rāghava, who is capable of doing you harm — as if standing against Death himself?"

**2.18.10**  "I shall empty this entire Ayodhyā of men with sharp arrows if she persists in this wrong, O bull among men."

**2.18.11**  "Whoever sides with Bharata or wishes his good — all of them I shall slay; for the gentle are always overpowered."

**2.18.12**  "With both you and me as his unsurpassed enemies, who has the power to give sovereignty to Bharata, O chastiser of foes?"

**2.18.13**  "I am genuinely devoted with my whole being to my brother, O queen — I swear to you by truth, by my bow, and by the sacrificial offerings I have made."

**2.18.14**  "If Rāma enters a blazing fire or the wilderness, know me, O queen, as having already entered there before him."

**2.18.15**  "I shall remove your sorrow by my valor, as the risen sun disperses darkness — let the queen behold my prowess, and let Rāghava behold it."

**2.18.16**  Having heard these words of the great-souled Lakṣmaṇa, Kausalyā, weeping and distracted with grief, spoke to Rāma.

**2.18.17**  "My son, you have heard what your brother Lakṣmaṇa has said — do accordingly whatever follows from that, if it pleases you."

**2.18.18**  "And you ought not to depart from here, leaving me scorched with grief, after hearing the unrighteous words spoken by my co-wife."

**2.18.19**  "O knower of dharma, if you are righteous and wish to practice dharma, then serve me, remaining here — practice the highest dharma."

**2.18.20**  "Serving his mother, my son, Kaśyapa, dwelling restrained in his own home, endowed with the highest austerity, attained the three heavens."

**2.18.21**  "Just as the king is to be honored by you with reverence, so too am I — I do not give you permission; you must not go to the forest from here."

**2.18.22**  "Without you, life holds no purpose for me, nor happiness — with you beside me, even eating grass is my greater good."

**2.18.23**  "If you go to the forest leaving me here consumed with grief, I shall sit here in a fast unto death — I shall not be able to live."

**2.18.24**  "Then you, my son, shall attain the hell renowned in the world, just as the lord of rivers, the ocean, receives the sin of brahminicide through unrighteousness."

**2.18.25**  Then the righteous-souled Rāma spoke words suffused with dharma to his mother Kausalyā, lamenting thus in her misery.

**2.18.26**  "I have no power to transgress my father's word — I bow my head and beseech you: I wish to go to the forest."

**2.18.27**  "Even the learned sage Kaṇḍu, practitioner of vows, while carrying out his father's command — knowing dharma — slew a cow."

**2.18.28**  "And in our own lineage of old, by the Sāgaras who dug at their father Sagara's command, a most great destruction was accomplished."

**2.18.29**  "By Rāma Jāmadagnya, carrying out his father's command, his own mother Reṇukā was herself slain with an axe in the forest."

**2.18.30**  "This adherence to a father's command is not being done by me alone — this path, intended by the ancients, is being followed in their footsteps."

**2.18.31**  "This therefore must be done by me on earth, and no otherwise — for one who carries out his father's word never comes to diminishment."

**2.18.32**  Having thus addressed his mother, he spoke again to Lakṣmaṇa: "I know, Lakṣmaṇa, your unsurpassed love for me, though the purport of truth and of tranquility has not been understood."

**2.18.33**  "For dharma is supreme in the world; truth is grounded in dharma; and this highest word of my father rests upon dharma."

**2.18.34**  "Having pledged to a father's word, or a mother's, or a Brāhmaṇa's, one standing firm in dharma must not make it vain, O hero."

**2.18.35**  "I am not able to transgress my father's command — for it is by my father's word, O hero, that Kaikeyī has sent me forth."

**2.18.36**  "Therefore abandon this ignoble resolve rooted in the dharma of warriors, take refuge in dharma, set aside harshness — let my counsel be followed."

**2.18.37**  Having thus spoken out of affection to his brother, the elder brother of Lakṣmaṇa spoke again to Kausalyā, with joined palms and bowed head.

**2.18.38**  "Grant me permission, O queen, as I go to the forest from here — I swear to you by my life: perform the auspicious rites for me; having fulfilled my vow, I shall return again from the forest to the city."

**2.18.39**  "For I am not able to bring about great prosperity behind the back of renown merely for the sake of the kingdom in a short while — nor, O queen, do I today choose a lesser earth through unrighteousness over life."

**2.18.40**  That bull among men, still intent on going to the Daṇḍaka forest by his own valor, propitiating his mother, and then firmly instructing his younger brother, made a final appearance, circumambulated his mother in his heart, and took his leave.

**2.19.1**  Then, seeing him afflicted with anguish, intensely indignant, breathing like a mighty serpent, his eyes dilated with fury —

**2.19.2**  — Rāma, self-possessed, steadying his composure with fortitude, approached Saumitri, that dear friend and beloved brother, and spoke these words:

**2.19.3**  "O Saumitri, let the bustle of preparations that was made for my consecration now serve instead for the reversal of that consecration.

**2.19.4**  She whose heart is being tormented on account of my consecration — that mother of ours — act in such a way that she may have no further cause for anxiety.

**2.19.5**  I cannot bear, O Saumitri, to disregard even for a moment the grief born of apprehension that has arisen in her heart.

**2.19.6**  I do not recall having ever done, intentionally or unwittingly, even the slightest thing displeasing to my mothers or to my father.

**2.19.7**  My father, who is truthful, steadfast in his truth-pledges, ever of true valor — may he who fears the sin of falsehood in the next world be freed from fear.

**2.19.8**  For if this act is not carried through, his heart would burn with the torment that his pledge of truth was not honored — and his torment would in turn torment me.

**2.19.9**  Therefore, O Lakṣmaṇa, having set aside the rite of consecration, I wish straightaway to depart to the forest from this very place.

**2.19.10**  Once I am banished today, that king's daughter will have achieved her purpose, and then she will consecrate her son Bharata without impediment.

**2.19.11**  When I have gone to the forest clad in bark garments and deerskin, wearing matted locks, Kaikeyī's heart will be at peace.

**2.19.12**  That by which this resolve has been directed and the mind fully composed — that I ought not to trouble; I shall go forth without delay.

**2.19.13**  In my banishment and in the return of the kingdom that was granted to me — fate alone, O Saumitri, is to be seen at work.

**2.19.14**  For how could Kaikeyī have come to act in such a way as to cause me harm, were this not the disposition of fate, ordained by the god of death?

**2.19.15**  For you know, O gentle one, that there was never any distinction in my regard for my mothers, nor any special difference in hers toward me, her child.

**2.19.16**  Therefore, that she should have spoken to me those harsh, bitter words aimed at annulling the consecration and at banishment — I attribute this to nothing other than fate.

**2.19.17**  How could a princess of such natural nobility, possessed of such qualities, have spoken in the presence of her husband of my humiliation, as though she were a common woman?

**2.19.18**  That fate which is inscrutable cannot be prevented even among living beings — clearly, a reversal has befallen both her and me.

**2.19.19**  Who, O Saumitri, can venture to fight against fate, whose workings in any action can be grasped by nothing other than itself?

**2.19.20**  Pleasure and pain, fear and anger, gain and loss, existence and non-existence — whatever is thus constituted, is that not indeed the work of fate?

**2.19.21**  Even though my consecration has been thwarted, I feel no grief. Therefore, free from grief yourself and following my lead, quickly withdraw the preparations for the consecration rite.

**2.19.22**  O Lakṣmaṇa, in this obstruction of my kingship, the younger mother is not to be overly blamed — for those overtaken by fate speak of unwished things; and you know well the power of fate to work thus.

**2.20.1**  While Rāma was speaking thus, Lakṣmaṇa repeatedly hung his head low; hearing these words, he felt himself pulled between grief and fierce resolve.

**2.20.2**  Then, O bull among men, he knitted his brows together, and breathed hard — like a great serpent coiled in its den, enraged.

**2.20.3**  His face, set with that fearsome frown, was then unbearable to behold — resembling the visage of a lion roused to wrath.

**2.20.4**  He who was unconstrained, shaking like an elephant shaking its own trunk, let his neck drop sideways and upward across his body —

**2.20.5**  — and looking at his brother askance from the corners of his eyes, he said: "This very great agitation of yours has arisen in the wrong place.

**2.20.6**  How could a man of your stature speak such things composedly, when the world would not so readily suspect fault in dharma?

**2.20.7**  Why do you, a bold bull among kṣatriyas, praise fate as though it were cowardly? Why do you invoke pitiful, impotent fate?

**2.20.8**  O righteous one, how is it that you harbor no suspicion of those two sinful ones? There are subtle tests of dharma — do you not perceive them?

**2.20.9**  The consecration of another in your place — which the world would hate — is what has led your mind to this confusion, O lord of the earth. That very dharma by whose invocation you are bewildered is hateful to me.

**2.20.10**  Even if your resolve is of divine origin and their opinion carries weight, still, the fact that you would overlook this — that does not please me either.

**2.20.11**  One who is faint-hearted and bereft of valor submits to fate; heroes who hold their souls in high esteem do not merely worship fate.

**2.20.12**  A man who has the power to overcome fate by his own exertion does not sink down when his purpose is ruined by fate.

**2.20.13**  Today they shall see the power of fate and the power of a man; today the distinction between the divine and the human will be made manifest.

**2.20.14**  Today people shall see fate struck down by my valor, just as today we have seen your royal consecration struck down by fate.

**2.20.15**  Just as a maddened elephant running rampant beyond the goad is turned back, so I shall turn back fate by my own exertion.

**2.20.16**  Not all the guardians of the worlds together could obstruct Rāma's consecration today — nor could all three worlds — much less a father alone.

**2.20.17**  Those who have secretly conspired for your exile to the forest, O king — they shall dwell in the forest likewise for fourteen years.

**2.20.18**  I shall cut off that hope of your father and of her — the hope that works through obstructing your consecration so that a son may become king.

**2.20.19**  Faced with my power in opposition, the force of fate shall not be able to prevail to cause harm in the way my own fierce exertion shall.

**2.20.20**  After a thousand years, once the duty of ruling over the people is fulfilled, the noble-born sons shall perform the forest-dwelling themselves — after you have already gone.

**2.20.21**  For according to the way of former royal seers, forest-dwelling is prescribed — entrusting the people to one's sons to be cared for as children.

**2.20.22**  O king, if out of fear that the kingship might be disrupted by a divided mind you do not wish to hold the kingdom in yourself, O Rāma, O righteous one —

**2.20.23**  — then I pledge to you, O hero — may I forfeit my place in the world of heroes — I shall guard your kingdom as the shore guards the ocean.

**2.20.24**  Be consecrated with auspicious rites; be engaged in that alone. I alone am capable of holding back by force all the kings of the earth.

**2.20.25**  These two arms of mine are not for adornment; the bow is not worn by me for decoration; the sword is not bound on for show; the arrows are not for display.

**2.20.26**  All these four — arms, bow, sword, and arrows — serve me for the crushing of enemies; nor do I have excessive desire for what I regard as hostile.

**2.20.27**  With my sharp-edged sword, blazing like leaping lightning, I count as no foe even the wielder of the thunderbolt himself.

**2.20.28**  The ground before me, dense and hard to traverse, shall be strewn with the trunks, thighs, and heads of elephants, horses, and men — crushed and pressed by my sword's blows.

**2.20.29**  Today, struck by my sword's edge, elephants shall fall to earth blazing like mountains — like clouds falling with their lightning.

**2.20.30**  With my leather finger-guard strapped on and my bow taken up, how could anyone count himself a true man among men while I yet stand?

**2.20.31**  Surpassing many enemies as one, and many as if they were one, I shall release my arrows into the vital points of men, horses, and elephants.

**2.20.32**  Today the power of my weapons shall make itself felt — to strip the king of his sovereignty and to establish your sovereignty, O master.

**2.20.33**  Today — for the sake of sandalwood paste and the loosening of armlets, for the bestowal of wealth, and for the protection of friends —

**2.20.34**  — these two arms of mine shall do fitting work, O Rāma, in warding off those who are responsible for the obstruction of your consecration.

**2.20.35**  Tell me — who is your enemy, that he may this very day be separated from me, from life and glory? Command me as you see fit so that this earth may come under your sway; I am your servant.

**2.20.36**  Wiping away his tears and consoling Lakṣmaṇa again and again, he who was the enhancer of the Rāghava line said: "Know me as one firmly set in our father's command — this, O gentle Saumitri, is the true path."

**2.21.1**  Seeing him steadfastly intent on fulfilling his father's decree, Kausalyā, choking with tears, spoke righteous words.

**2.21.2**  "One who has never known sorrow, righteous in soul, beloved in speech to all living beings — born of Daśaratha and of me — how shall he live by gleaning?

**2.21.3**  He whose own servants and slaves eat the finest prepared food — how shall that lord subsist in the forest on roots and fruits?

**2.21.4**  Who would believe this, hearing it? And for whom would fear not arise — that Rāghava, virtuous and beloved of the king, is being banished?

**2.21.5**  Bereft of you, the immeasurable great fire of grief shall consume me, as the bright-flamed fire consumes dry grass at the end of winter.

**2.21.6**  For how could a cow not follow her own calf when it departs? I shall follow you, O son, wherever you go.

**2.21.7**  Having heard those words spoken thus by his mother, Rāma, that bull among men, spoke in reply to his deeply grieved mother:

**2.21.8**  "Deceived by Kaikeyī, and with me gone to the forest, and abandoned by you as well, the king surely will not survive.

**2.21.9**  The abandonment of one's husband is said to be a cruelty peculiar to a woman — that you must not do, condemned even in thought.

**2.21.10**  As long as my father, the Kākutstha, lord of the earth, still lives — serve him; that is the eternal dharma.

**2.21.11**  Thus addressed by Rāma, the fair-visaged Kausalyā replied "So be it" with full willingness to Rāma the tireless in action.

**2.21.12**  Thus addressed, Rāma, foremost among the upholders of dharma, once more spoke these words to his deeply grieved mother:

**2.21.13**  "Both you and I must do our father's bidding — the king is husband, teacher, most excellent, the lord and master of all.

**2.21.14**  Having passed these fourteen years in the great forest, I shall return in supreme gladness and shall abide by your word.

**2.21.15**  Thus addressed, her face filled with tears, her heart consumed with anguish, Kausalyā, that devoted mother, then spoke to her beloved son:

**2.21.16**  "It is not possible for me to dwell among these co-wives, O Rāma. Take me too, O Kākutstha, to the forest, like a doe of the wild — if your mind is resolved on going, in deference to your father.

**2.21.17**  To her weeping thus, Rāma, himself in tears, replied: "For a living woman, her husband is the deity and the master — today the king is master, lord over both you and me.

**2.21.18**  And Bharata too, righteous in soul, dear in speech to all creatures, will attend to you — for he is always devoted to dharma.

**2.21.19**  Act vigilantly so that, after my departure, the king may not suffer even the slightest affliction through grief for his son.

**2.21.20**  A woman, however supremely excellent, who is devoted to vows and fasts but does not follow her husband's path — even she incurs a sinful fate.

**2.21.21**  She should render service alone, devoted to the beloved welfare of her husband — this is the dharma seen of old in the world, heard in the Veda, and remembered in the Smṛtis.

**2.21.22**  O queen, on my account you should honor the brāhmaṇas and those of excellent vows — and thus wait out the time, longing for my return.

**2.21.23**  You shall attain your supreme wish when I return — if the foremost of those who uphold dharma, the king, shall hold on to life.

**2.21.24**  Thus addressed by Rāma, her eyes clouded with a rush of tears, Kausalyā, afflicted by the sorrow for her son, spoke to Rāma: "Go, my son, single-minded; may you ever be blessed, O lord."

**2.21.25**  Thus indeed, seeing Rāma resolved upon forest-dwelling, the queen with her highest mind spoke words to Rāma of auspicious marks, and she became one who longed to perform rites of benediction over him.

**2.22.1**  Having performed her ablutions and touched the pure water, putting aside that distress, Rāma's wise and noble mother performed the auspicious rites.

**2.22.2**  May the Sādhyas, the Viśvas, the Maruts, and the great seers bestow well-being; may Dhātṛ, Vidhātṛ, Pūṣan, Bhaga, and Aryaman bestow well-being.

**2.22.3**  May the seasons, the fortnights, the months, the years, the nights, the days, and the hours always bring you well-being.

**2.22.4**  May Memory, Fortitude, and Dharma protect you on all sides, my son; and may the divine lord Skanda, Soma, and Bṛhaspati protect you.

**2.22.5**  May the Seven Seers and Nārada protect you on all sides; may all the lunar mansions and the planets together with their presiding deities protect you, the wise one clad in an ascetic's garb as you roam the great forests.

**2.22.6**  May jumping creatures, scorpions, biting insects, mosquitoes, reptiles, and all manner of bugs in the dense forest cause you no harm.

**2.22.7**  May the great elephants, lions, tigers, bears with their fangs, and the fierce horned buffalo do you no harm, my dear son.

**2.22.8**  May the terrible beings who feed on human flesh, and whatever other kinds of creatures there may be, do you no injury, my son — for I have duly propitiated them here.

**2.22.9**  May your journeys be auspicious; may your feats of valor be accomplished; may all prosperity attend you, Rāma. Go in safety, my son.

**2.22.10**  May there be well-being for you again and again from the celestial regions and from the earthly regions; from all the gods, and from those who obstruct your path.

**2.22.11**  May Brahmā, the lord of all worlds, the sustainer of all beings, along with the sages and the remaining gods, protect you as you dwell in the forest.

**2.22.12**  With these words, that illustrious wide-eyed woman honored the hosts of gods with garlands, perfumes, and fitting hymns of praise.

**2.22.13**  Whatever auspiciousness arose for the thousand-eyed Indra, saluted by all gods, at the slaying of Vṛtra — may that same auspiciousness be yours.

**2.22.14**  Whatever auspiciousness Vinatā fashioned long ago for Suparṇa — Garuḍa — when he was seeking the nectar of immortality, may that auspiciousness be yours.

**2.22.15**  Kausalyā also fashioned a protective charm using the efficacious and auspicious herb Viśalyakaraṇī, and muttered the appropriate sacred formulas over it.

**2.22.16**  Bowing, smelling his head, and embracing him, that illustrious woman said: "Go comfortably, Rāma, my son, your purposes fulfilled."

**2.22.17**  "May I see you again, my dear child, returned to Ayodhyā healthy, all your aims accomplished, dwelling happily and well established in the royal palace."

**2.22.18**  "The bands of gods headed by Śiva, the great seers, the beings, great asuras, and serpents that I have honored — may they and all the directions long desire your welfare, O Rāghava, as you depart for the forest."

**2.22.19**  Having spoken thus with eyes brimming with tears, and having duly completed the benedictory rites, she circumambulated the Rāghava and again and again pressed him tightly in her embrace.

**2.22.20**  Having thus been circumambulated by the queen, and having pressed his mother's feet again and again, that great-glorious Rāghava, blazing with his own splendor, departed for the dwelling of Sītā.

**2.23.1**  Having reverenced Kausalyā, Rāma set out for the forest, blessed with the auspicious rites performed by his mother, steadfast on the path of righteousness.

**2.23.2**  The prince, illuminating the royal road crowded with people, seemed to churn the hearts of the assembled folk by the sheer power of his virtues.

**2.23.3**  The afflicted Vaidehī had heard none of all this, for in her heart there was only the expectation of Rāma's consecration as crown prince.

**2.23.4**  Having performed her worship of the gods, that grateful and joyful woman, well versed in the duties of royalty, awaited the prince.

**2.23.5**  Then Rāma entered his own beautifully adorned dwelling, filled with joyful attendants, his face cast slightly downward out of shame.

**2.23.6**  Then Sītā sprang up and beheld her husband trembling — scorched by grief, his senses disturbed by anxious thoughts.

**2.23.7**  Seeing his face pale and streaming with perspiration, beside himself with agitation, and herself scorched by grief, she said: "What is this now, my lord?"

**2.23.8**  "Today, O Rāghava, the auspicious Puṣya asterism is in conjunction with Bṛhaspati — so declare the learned Brāhmaṇas. Why then are you downcast?"

**2.23.9**  "Your lovely face is not graced, as it should be, by the royal parasol with its hundred ribs, white as the foam of water, held above it."

**2.23.10**  "Your face, whose eyes resemble the lotus, is not being fanned by the two principal fly-whisks, white as the moon and the swan."

**2.23.11**  "O bull among men, the eloquent bards and panegyrists — the Sūtas and the Māgadhas — are not seen today joyfully singing your praises with auspicious hymns."

**2.23.12**  "The Brāhmaṇas who are masters of the Veda are not placing honey and curds upon your head in the prescribed manner, as upon one who has had the consecration poured over him."

**2.23.13**  "All the ministers of state, adorned with ornaments, and the heads of the guilds, together with the townspeople and the country folk, do not seem to wish to accompany you."

**2.23.14**  "Why does the chief ceremonial chariot, yoked to four swift horses adorned with golden ornaments, not go before you?"

**2.23.15**  "Nor is the auspicious, mark-honored elephant, dark as a rain cloud or a mountain, seen going before you in the procession, O hero."

**2.23.16**  "Nor do I see the golden, ornately decorated throne being carried ahead of you, O fair to behold, with heroes marching before it."

**2.23.17**  "When the consecration was ready, what has now happened to you? Your complexion is unprecedented, and no exhilaration is visible in you."

**2.23.18**  To her lamenting thus, the delight of the Raghu clan spoke: "Sītā, the revered lord — my father — is sending me into exile in the forest."

**2.23.19**  "O Jānakī, born of a great lineage, one who knows righteousness and walks in its ways, listen to how this has come about step by step."

**2.23.20**  "My father King Daśaratha, a man of true vows, once gladly granted two great boons to Kaikeyī."

**2.23.21**  "Now, today, when the king had made ready my consecration, she has called in that old debt; that appointed time has been invoked and compelled by dharma."

**2.23.22**  "I must dwell in the Daṇḍaka forest for fourteen years; my father has also installed Bharata as crown prince. I have come to see you before I depart for the uninhabited forest."

**2.23.23**  "You should never speak of me in Bharata's presence. Men who have attained power cannot endure praise of a rival; therefore my virtues should not be spoken of before Bharata."

**2.23.24**  "Nor should you ever be treated by him as a special charge; you can comport yourself near him only with compliance and agreeableness."

**2.23.25**  "I too, honoring that promise made to my elder, shall depart for the forest this very day. Be steadfast, O wise one."

**2.23.26**  "And when I have gone to the forest frequented by sages, O auspicious one, O sinless one, you must be devoted to vows and fasts."

**2.23.27**  "Rising early in the morning and performing the worship of the gods in the prescribed manner, you must pay reverence to Daśaratha, my father, the lord of men."

**2.23.28**  "And my mother Kausalyā, aged and worn thin by grief, deserves your respect, placing dharma above all else."

**2.23.29**  "You must always revere my other mothers as well, for in terms of affection, tenderness, and the enjoyment of my company, they are all equal to my mother."

**2.23.30**  "And Lakṣmaṇa and Śatrughna, who are dearer to me than my own life, are to be regarded by you especially as sons and brothers alike."

**2.23.31**  "And you must never do anything disagreeable to Bharata, for he is now the king and lord both of the realm and of the family."

**2.23.32**  "Kings who are gratified by good conduct and attended upon with effort become gracious; in the contrary case they grow wrathful."

**2.23.33**  "Kings abandon even their own natural sons if they act against their interests, and embrace even ordinary people who serve them capably."

**2.23.34**  "I shall depart for the great forest, my dear; you must remain here, O beautiful one. So that you do no wrong to anyone, act in accordance with these words of mine."

**2.24.1**  Thus addressed, Vaidehī — who deserved only tender words and spoke only tender words — became indignant out of deep love, and spoke these words to her husband.

**2.24.2**  "O noble son, a father, a mother, a brother, a son — and likewise a daughter-in-law — each enjoys the fruits of their own merit and tends to their own separate destiny."

**2.24.3**  "But a wife, O bull among men, partakes solely of her husband's fortune. Thus it is I who have been destined to dwell in the forest as well."

**2.24.4**  "Neither a father, nor a son, nor one's own self, nor a mother, nor a circle of friends — in this world and after death, the husband is ever the sole refuge of women."

**2.24.5**  "If you depart today for that inaccessible forest, O Rāghava, I shall go before you, crushing underfoot the sharp kuśa grass and thorns."

**2.24.6**  "Setting aside jealousy and anger as one sets aside water left over after a meal, take me with you without apprehension, O hero — no sin resides in me."

**2.24.7**  "Whatever the condition — whether in palace towers, or aerial mansions, or traveling through the sky — the shadow of a husband's feet excels them all."

**2.24.8**  "I have been instructed by my mother and my father in conduct befitting all manner of circumstances; I need not now be told how I should comport myself."

**2.24.9**  "I shall dwell happily in the forest just as in my father's house, giving no thought to the three worlds, but thinking only of my vow to my husband."

**2.24.10**  "Ever attending upon you, self-disciplined, and observing the vow of chastity, I shall take delight with you, O hero, in the honey-fragrant forests."

**2.24.11**  "For you, Rāma, are able to protect in the forest the people of others — how much more so me, O giver of honor."

**2.24.12**  "I shall subsist always on fruits and roots, without doubt. Living with you, I shall cause you no hardship."

**2.24.13**  "I long to see the rivers, the mountains, the lakes, and the forests everywhere, fearless in every place, with you, my wise lord, as my protector."

**2.24.14**  "I would wish, made happy by union with you, O hero, to behold the lotus pools thronged with geese and kāraṇḍava ducks, their lotuses in full and beautiful bloom."

**2.24.15**  "With you, O large-eyed one, I shall take the greatest delight — and thus I could dwell with you for a hundred thousand years."

**2.24.16**  "And if my abode, O Rāghava, O tiger among men, were to be even in heaven but without you, I should not desire that either."

**2.24.17**  "I shall go to the forest most difficult to traverse, filled with deer, inhabited by monkeys and elephants; I shall dwell in the forest as in my father's house, clasping your very feet, honored by you."

**2.24.18**  "Take me — whose mind is set on none other, whose heart cleaves to you alone, who am resolved on death if separated from you. Do this kindness, grant my entreaty; no burden shall thereby fall upon you from me."

**2.24.19**  Yet even as she spoke thus, that best of men, attached to righteousness, did not intend to take Sītā; and he spoke to her at length, on the subject of dissuasion, and concerning the hardships of dwelling in the forest, addressing her in her distress.

**2.25.1**  As Sītā spoke thus, that knower of dharma, devoted to righteousness, that righteous soul, spoke these words to turn her back.

**2.25.2**  "Sītā, you are born of a great lineage and are ever devoted to dharma. Practise your own duty here — do not act merely as your heart desires."

**2.25.3**  "Sītā, you must act as I shall tell you, O gentle one. The forest holds many hardships; hear me speak of them."

**2.25.4**  "Sītā, abandon this resolve to dwell in the forest. What is called the wilderness is full of many dangers."

**2.25.5**  "I say this out of concern for your welfare: the forest knows no happiness — it is nothing but misery at every turn."

**2.25.6**  "The roars of lions dwelling in mountain caves, rising from mountain torrents — these are dreadful to hear. For this reason, the forest is suffering."

**2.25.7**  "One must sleep on leaf-beds, broken and spread on the bare ground, weary from exertion through the nights. For this reason, the forest is yet harder to bear."

**2.25.8**  "Fasts must be kept, O Maithilī, as one's strength allows; matted locks must be worn, and garments of bark must be borne."

**2.25.9**  "There the wind is fierce, the darkness deep, and hunger constant; great terrors abound — for this reason, the forest is harder still."

**2.25.10**  "Many reptiles of many forms, O passionate one, crawl boldly over the ground. For this reason, the forest is very hard to endure."

**2.25.11**  "Serpents that dwell in rivers, winding through their bends, lie coiled across the paths. For this reason, the forest is harder to bear."

**2.25.12**  "Locusts, scorpions, worms, horse-flies and mosquitoes together torment one ceaselessly, O gentle one. All of it is suffering — therefore the forest is misery."

**2.25.13**  "Thorny trees, and kusa grass and kāśa reeds, with their branch-tips tangled in the forest, O passionate one — for this reason the forest is harder to endure."

**2.25.14**  "Therefore, enough of going to the forest. The forest is not fit for you. Considering it carefully, I see the forest as full of many faults indeed."

**2.25.15**  But even after the great-souled Rāma resolved not to take her to the forest, Sītā paid no heed to his words. Then, deeply distressed, she spoke to Rāma thus.

**2.26.1**  Hearing Rāma's words, Sītā, stricken with grief, her face streaming with tears, spoke these words slowly.

**2.26.2**  "The hardships you have named regarding life in the forest — know that I regard all of them as virtues, offered forth by your love for me."

**2.26.3**  "I must go with you — such is the command of my elders. Without you, Rāma, I must give up this life here."

**2.26.4**  "Standing at your side, O Rāghava, not even Śakra, lord of the gods, could overpower me by his might."

**2.26.5**  "A woman bereft of her husband cannot live — such is the very truth, Rāma, that you have yourself shown me."

**2.26.6**  "And besides, O greatly wise one, I once heard from brāhmaṇas, truly, in my father's house, that I am destined to dwell in the forest."

**2.26.7**  "Hearing the words of those auspicious twice-born sages at home, O mighty one, I have ever since felt a keenness for living in the forest."

**2.26.8**  "That sojourn in the forest, so foretold, must come to me — and there, beloved, I shall go with you alone, no other way."

**2.26.9**  "The prophecy shall be fulfilled — I shall go with you. Now the time has come; let the brāhmaṇa's word prove true."

**2.26.10**  "I know well that in forest-life, many kinds of suffering are surely encountered by men of undisciplined spirit, O hero."

**2.26.11**  "And as a girl in my father's house, I heard of forest-dwelling from a wandering ascetic woman of virtuous conduct, here in the presence of my mother."

**2.26.12**  "You have been entreated in many ways before, O lord — for the journey of forest-dwelling together with you is what I have longed for."

**2.26.13**  "I am ready and resolved for the journey, O Rāghava — bless you! The wandering life of the forest, proper to a hero, is pleasing to me."

**2.26.14**  "O pure-souled one, following my husband out of love, I shall be free of all sin — for a husband is my very deity."

**2.26.15**  "Even in the life after death, union with you is auspicious for me — so runs the holy scripture that is heard from the illustrious brāhmaṇas."

**2.26.16**  "O greatly wise one, a woman who has been given by her parents to a man here in this world with the rite of water and in accord with dharma — she belongs to him even in the afterlife."

**2.26.17**  "Given all this, for what reason do you not wish to take me — your own wife, of good conduct and devoted to her husband?"

**2.26.18**  "You ought to take me, O Kākutstha — devoted, faithful to my vow, helpless, equal in joy and in sorrow — one who shares equally in happiness and grief."

**2.26.19**  "If you do not wish to take me, thus wretched, to the forest, then I shall resort to poison, fire, or water as the cause of my death."

**2.26.20**  Even as she pleaded with him in many ways for permission to go, the mighty-armed Rāma did not consent to taking her into the desolate forest.

**2.26.21**  Spoken to in this way, Maithilī fell into deep anguish, and the hot tears flowing from her eyes seemed to drench the earth.

**2.26.22**  As she brooded thus, the self-composed Kākutstha consoled Vaidehī at length, even as she was overcome with anger, trying to turn her away from her resolve.

**2.27.1**  But even while being consoled by Rāma, Maithilī, daughter of Janaka, spoke these words to her husband in order to go to the forest.

**2.27.2**  Deeply agitated, Sītā embraced broad-chested Rāghava out of both affection and wounded pride.

**2.27.3**  "What must my father, Vaideha, lord of Mithilā, have thought of you, Rāma — a son-in-law who has a woman's form in the shape of a man?"

**2.27.4**  "False and ignorant is what the world will say — that there is no supreme valour in Rāma — when he blazes like the sun in the sky."

**2.27.5**  "What have you done to make you downcast? Or whence comes the fear that makes you wish to abandon me who have no refuge but you?"

**2.27.6**  "Know me, O hero, to be like Sāvitrī who was devoted to Satyavat, son of Dyumatsena — wholly devoted to you, under your authority alone."

**2.27.7**  "I have never looked upon another man, even in my mind, O sinless one. How could I go without you, O Rāghava, like a woman who dishonours her family?"

**2.27.8**  "And yet, Rāma, you yourself wish to hand me over to others — your wife, taken in youth, long cohabited with, chaste — as a stage-dancer might his own."

**2.27.9**  "Without taking me, you should not set out for the forest. Whether it be penance, wilderness, or heaven — all is to be with you."

**2.27.10**  "There will not be any hardship for me on the path, following behind you, whether in the places of wandering or in sleeping."

**2.27.11**  "The kuśa grass, kāśa reeds, śara rushes, and thorny trees along the way — to me, travelling with you, their touch will be as soft as cotton or hide."

**2.27.12**  "The dust that a great wind will scatter upon me, O beloved, I shall regard as the most precious sandalwood paste."

**2.27.13**  "Resting on the grassy meadows at the forest's edge, familiar with the forest, on beds spread with kuthā grass — what could be more pleasant than that?"

**2.27.14**  "Whatever leaf, root, or fruit you yourself gather and give me — whether little or much — that will be like the taste of nectar to me."

**2.27.15**  "There I shall not think of my mother, nor my father, nor the house, as I enjoy the seasonal flowers and fruits."

**2.27.16**  "There you shall not see anything unpleasant on my account; there will be no sorrow for you because of me, nor shall I be a burden to you."

**2.27.17**  "Where you are is heaven; where you are not is hell — knowing this supreme love of mine, go, Rāma, with me."

**2.27.18**  "But if you will not take me — thus calm and resolved — to the forest, I shall this very day drink poison. I will not fall under the power of your enemies."

**2.27.19**  "Even thereafter, life abandoned by you in sorrow is no life at all, O lord — death at that very moment is better."

**2.27.20**  "This grief I cannot bear even for a moment — how much less, wretched, for thirteen years?"

**2.27.21**  Thus she, scorched by grief, having lamented piteously and at length, clung tightly to her husband, weeping aloud in her anguish.

**2.27.22**  Pierced by many sharp words as a she-elephant by poisoned arrows, she released the tears long suppressed, like fire released from the araṇi wood.

**2.27.23**  Water clear as crystal, born of her anguish, flowed from her two eyes as water flows from two lotuses.

**2.27.24**  Embracing her in his arms — she who was grief-stricken and as if unconscious — Rāma then spoke words of reassurance.

**2.27.25**  "O goddess, I do not desire even heaven purchased by your suffering. I have no fear from any quarter, like Svayambhū himself."

**2.27.26**  "Without knowing your full mind, O fair-faced one, I did not wish to dwell in the forest — even though I am capable of protecting you."

**2.27.27**  "Since you were created to share forest-dwelling with me, O Maithilī, you cannot be forsaken by me — as a self-respecting man cannot forsake his honour."

**2.27.28**  "The dharma practised of old by the good — that I follow today, O elephant-gaited one, as Suvarcalā follows the sun."

**2.27.29**  "This is dharma, O fair-hipped one — to be subject to one's father and mother. Having transgressed their command, I cannot bear to live."

**2.27.30**  "I wish to conduct myself as my father instructs me, he who stands firm on the path of truth and righteousness — for that is eternal dharma. Follow me, O timid one; be my companion in dharma."

**2.27.31**  "Give jewels to the brāhmaṇas and food to the mendicants; give to those who seek — and make haste, do not delay."

**2.27.32**  Learning that her husband's departure and her own journey were now agreed, the goddess Sītā, joyful, swiftly set about giving gifts.

**2.27.33**  Then she, elated and with heart full of joy, the illustrious and noble-minded woman, heeding her husband's words, set about giving wealth and jewels to those who uphold dharma.

**2.28.1**  Then the great-spirited Rāma spoke to Lakṣmaṇa, who stood before him ready to go forward, a hero entreating with palms joined together.

**2.28.2**  "O Saumitri, when you depart with me today into that forest, who will support the illustrious Kausalyā and Sumitrā?"

**2.28.3**  "The great-spirited king, who showered upon them their desires even as rain-clouds shower upon the earth, is now bound fast in the noose of passion."

**2.28.4**  "For she, the daughter of King Aśvapati, having now obtained this kingdom, will not deal kindly with her co-wives who are in distress."

**2.28.5**  Having been addressed thus by Rāma with gentle words, Lakṣmaṇa — skilled in speech — then replied to that master of eloquence.

**2.28.6**  "By your very glory alone, O hero, Bharata will with devotion honour both Kausalyā and Sumitrā — of this there is no doubt."

**2.28.7**  "The noble Kausalyā could sustain even a thousand men like me, for she has the revenue of a thousand villages to live upon."

**2.28.8**  "Bearing bow and arrows, carrying a spade and a basket, I shall go ahead of you, pointing out the path."

**2.28.9**  "I shall constantly gather for you roots and fruits, and whatever other forest foods are the natural sustenance of ascetics."

**2.28.10**  "While you take delight with the Vaidehi on mountain slopes, I shall do everything for you, whether you are awake or asleep."

**2.28.11**  Rāma, greatly pleased by these words, replied to him: "Go take leave, O Saumitri, of all our dear friends."

**2.28.12**  "And the two divine bows of fearful aspect which the great-souled Varuṇa himself gave to King Janaka at the great sacrifice —"

**2.28.13**  "— the two divine, impenetrable coats of armour, two quivers with inexhaustible arrows, and two swords pure as the sun and adorned with gold —"

**2.28.14**  "— all this was reverently deposited in the preceptor's house. Go quickly, Lakṣmaṇa, and bring those weapons here."

**2.28.15**  He, resolute for forest life, took leave of his dear ones and of the preceptor of the Ikṣvāku line, and then took up those supreme weapons.

**2.28.16**  Saumitri, the tiger among princes, displayed all those divine, duly honoured, garland-adorned weapons to Rāma.

**2.28.17**  The self-controlled Rāma, with affection, spoke to Lakṣmaṇa who had arrived: "You have come at the right time, O gentle Lakṣmaṇa, when I was longing for you."

**2.28.18**  "O scorcher of foes, I wish to distribute this wealth of mine — together with you — to the brāhmaṇas and ascetics."

**2.28.19**  "The most excellent twice-born who dwell here with steadfast devotion serving the elders — and especially to all who depend on me for their livelihood."

**2.28.20**  "Quickly bring here the noble Suyajña, son of Vasiṣṭha, foremost among the twice-born; for I shall depart to the forest after honouring all the other venerable brāhmaṇas as well."

**2.29.1**  Then, having learned his brother's most auspicious and beloved command, he went quickly and entered the dwelling of Suyajña.

**2.29.2**  Saluting that brāhmaṇa who was standing by the sacred fire, Lakṣmaṇa said: "Come, dear friend; behold the home of him who performs difficult deeds."

**2.29.3**  Then, after quickly completing the twilight devotions, he went together with Saumitri and entered that charming abode of Rāma, graced with prosperity.

**2.29.4**  With palms joined together, Rāghava, together with Sītā, approached the arrived Suyajña — knower of the Vedas — as one approaches a consecrated fire.

**2.29.5**  With splendid bracelets and earrings of pure gold, together with golden thread-necklaces, jewels, armlets, and bangles,

**2.29.6**  — and with many other gems — the Kākutstha honoured Suyajña; and then Rāma, prompted by Sītā, spoke to him.

**2.29.7**  "Convey, O gentle one, this necklace and golden thread to your wife; and now Sītā wishes to give you, dear friend, this girdle as well."

**2.29.8**  "The Vaidehi also desires to bestow upon you this couch with its excellent coverlet adorned with manifold gems."

**2.29.9**  "The elephant named Śatruṃjaya which my maternal uncle gave me — that elephant, worth a thousand common elephants, I give to you, O bull among the twice-born."

**2.29.10**  Thus addressed by Rāma, Suyajña accepted all that and pronounced auspicious blessings upon Rāma, Lakṣmaṇa, and Sītā.

**2.29.11**  Then Rāma, sweet of speech, spoke these words to his dear and composed brother Saumitri, even as Brahmā might address Indra, lord of the thirty gods.

**2.29.12**  "Invite both Agastya and Kauśika, those two most excellent brāhmaṇas, O Saumitri, and honour them with jewels as rain bestows blessings on crops."

**2.29.13**  "And the one who devotedly attends on Kausalyā with blessings — the teacher of the Taittirīyas, handsome and learned in the Vedas —"

**2.29.14**  "— give to him, O Saumitri, a carriage, female servants, and silk garments — as much as may satisfy that brāhmaṇa."

**2.29.15**  "The noble charioteer Citraratha, a trusted minister of long standing — gratify him too with precious gems, garments, and wealth."

**2.29.16**  "And prepare also, O Saumitri, a thousand loads of fine rice, two hundred auspicious ones, and a thousand cows to be used for relish."

**2.29.17**  Then that tiger among men, Lakṣmaṇa, himself distributed that wealth to the foremost of brāhmaṇas just as instructed, even as Kubera the wealth-giver.

**2.29.18**  Then, after bestowing abundant goods upon each of his dependants who stood there with tears in their eyes, he spoke to them.

**2.29.19**  "Both Lakṣmaṇa's house and this house of mine are to be kept each one occupied and not left empty, until my return."

**2.29.20**  Having spoken thus to all those grieving dependants, he said to the treasurer: "Let the wealth be brought here." Thereupon all his dependants brought all his wealth.

**2.29.21**  Then that tiger among men, together with Lakṣmaṇa, caused that wealth to be distributed to the twice-born, to children and the aged, and to the destitute.

**2.29.22**  There was among them a tawny-eyed brāhmaṇa of the Gārgya lineage named Trijaṭa; no one barred him up to the fifth enclosure.

**2.29.23**  Approaching the prince, Trijaṭa spoke these words: "O prince of great renown, I am without wealth and have many sons; I live by gleaning grain in the forest — please look after me."

**2.29.24**  Then Rāma spoke to him with gentle humour: "Not even one of a thousand cows has yet been distributed by me; however far you throw your staff, that far shall you receive."

**2.29.25**  He hastily and excitedly wrapped his cloth tightly round his waist, then whirling his staff hurled it with all the force of his might.

**2.29.26**  Then Rāma spoke, reassuring that scion of the Gārgyas: "Do not be angry — for this was but a jest of mine."

**2.29.27**  Then the great sage Trijaṭa, together with his wife, joyfully received the herd of cattle and in return offered to the great-souled one blessings that swelled with fame, strength, joy, and happiness.

**2.30.1**  Having distributed abundant wealth to the brāhmaṇas together with the Vaidehi, the two Rāghavas set out together with Sītā to see their father.

**2.30.2**  Then, with those terrible-to-behold weapons taken up and adorned by Sītā with garlands and strings of flowers, the two shone resplendent.

**2.30.3**  Then the prosperous people, climbing the palaces, mansions, and the pinnacles of towers, looked on with sorrowful detachment.

**2.30.4**  For the streets thronged with so many people that it was impossible to pass through them; hence they climbed the palaces and in grief watched Rāghava.

**2.30.5**  Seeing Rāma proceeding on foot without his royal umbrella, the people, their hearts stricken with grief, uttered many diverse words.

**2.30.6**  "He whom a great four-division army used to follow when he went forth — now only Lakṣmaṇa follows that one alone, together with Sītā."

**2.30.7**  "He who knows the taste of sovereignty and who is the fulfiller of desires for those who desire, yet he does not wish to render his father's word false, out of reverence for dharma."

**2.30.8**  "Sītā, who could not be seen even by beings moving through the sky, is today seen by the people who have come into the royal road."

**2.30.9**  "Sītā, accustomed to body-unguents and to the use of red sandalwood paste, will quickly be discoloured by rain, heat, and cold."

**2.30.10**  "Surely today Daśaratha speaks under the sway of some other force — for a king ought not to banish a beloved son."

**2.30.11**  "How could there be banishment even of a son without virtues? — and how much more of him who has won the entire world by his conduct alone?"

**2.30.12**  "Non-cruelty, compassion, learning, good character, self-control, and tranquillity — these six virtues adorn Rāghava, the most excellent of men."

**2.30.13**  "Therefore, by the affliction done to him, the subjects are sorely tormented, like water-creatures in summer when the waters dry up."

**2.30.14**  "The entire world is afflicted by the affliction of this lord of the world, just as a fruit- and flower-bearing tree is afflicted by the striking of its root."

**2.30.15**  "Let us quickly follow, together with our kin, even as Lakṣmaṇa does — following where Rāghava goes."

**2.30.16**  "Abandoning our gardens, our fields, and our houses, sharing in one sorrow and one joy, let us follow the righteous Rāma."

**2.30.17**  "Our treasuries dug up and emptied, our courtyards ruined, our grain and wealth taken, stripped of all substance —"

**2.30.18**  "— our houses covered with dust, abandoned by the gods, deserted by us — let Kaikeyī have them."

**2.30.19**  "Let the forest where Rāghava goes become the city; and let this city, abandoned by us, become a forest."

**2.30.20**  "Let all the fanged beasts take to the lairs and the mountain slopes to the deer and birds; and what we abandon — let them inhabit what was frequented, and abandon what was abandoned."

**2.30.21**  Rāma heard all these diverse words uttered by people of every kind; and having heard them, his mind was not shaken.

**2.30.22**  Waiting for those kinsfolk who were suffering, yet himself appearing without distress and almost smiling, Rāma went forward, wishing to see his father and to fulfil his father's command in due order.

**2.30.23**  Before that, the great-souled son of the Ikṣvāku line, Rāma, about to depart for the forest, paused on seeing Sumantra there, intending to use him as an intermediary to his father.

**2.30.24**  The Rāghava, devoted to dharma and with his resolve firmly fixed on entering the forest in accordance with his father's command, looked at Sumantra and said: "Announce my arrival to the king."

**2.31.1**  Sent swiftly by Rāma, his senses clouded with grief, the charioteer entered and found the king sighing deeply.

**2.31.2**  Seeing the king's mind utterly distraught and lamenting for Rāma alone, the greatly wise charioteer sat down with folded hands.

**2.31.3**  "This son of yours, tiger among men, stands at the door, having distributed wealth to the brāhmaṇas and to all who depend upon him."

**2.31.4**  "May Rāma of true valor see you — may it be well with you; having taken leave of all his friends, he now desires to behold you."

**2.31.5**  "He is about to depart for the great forest — behold him, O lord of the earth, encircled by all the qualities of a king, as the sun is encircled by its rays."

**2.31.6**  That king — truthful, righteous in soul, deep as the ocean, spotless as the sky — answered him.

**2.31.7**  "Sumantra, bring me all my wives who are here; surrounded by all my consorts I wish to behold Rāghava."

**2.31.8**  Passing into the inner apartments, he spoke to those women: "The noble king summons you — go there without delay."

**2.31.9**  Thus addressed by Sumantra on the king's command, all the women set out for that dwelling, heeding their lord's decree.

**2.31.10**  Those seven hundred and fifty women of copper-red eyes, firm in their vows, surrounding Kausalyā, moved forward slowly.

**2.31.11**  When his wives had arrived, the lord of the earth surveyed them and said to the charioteer: "Sumantra, bring me my son."

**2.31.12**  The charioteer then swiftly led Rāma, Lakṣmaṇa, and Maithilī before the lord of the earth.

**2.31.13**  The king, seeing his son approaching from afar with joined hands, leapt swiftly from his seat in anguish, surrounded by the women.

**2.31.14**  The lord of the people rushed toward Rāma with speed; before he could reach him, stricken with sorrow, he fell to the ground in a swoon.

**2.31.15**  Rāma rushed quickly to him, and so did the great warrior Lakṣmaṇa — to the king who lay as though senseless, overwhelmed with grief.

**2.31.16**  A wail arose from a thousand women throughout the royal palace — the cry "Alas, Rāma!" suddenly overwhelmed by the clamor of their ornaments.

**2.31.17**  Both Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa embraced him with their arms and, weeping, laid him together with Sītā upon his couch.

**2.31.18**  Then, after a moment, when the lord of the earth had regained consciousness — inundated in an ocean of grief — Rāma spoke to him with joined hands.

**2.31.19**  "I take leave of you, great king — you are the lord of us all; behold me, who am about to depart for the Daṇḍaka forest, and send me off with your blessing."

**2.31.20**  "Grant leave also to Lakṣmaṇa, and to Sītā who follows me into the forest — though dissuaded with many true reasons, neither of them is willing to desist."

**2.31.21**  "Grant leave to all of us — Lakṣmaṇa, Sītā, and me — setting aside your grief, O giver of honor, as Prajāpati gives leave to his creatures."

**2.31.22**  As Rāghava waited patiently for the leave-granting of the lord of the earth, the king gazed upon him and spoke of the forest-dwelling.

**2.31.23**  "I, O Rāghava, have been deluded by the boon-gift of Kaikeyī; restrain me now and yourself become king of Ayodhyā this very day."

**2.31.24**  Thus addressed by the king, Rāma — best among upholders of dharma, skilled in speech — replied to his father with joined hands.

**2.31.25**  "You are lord of the earth for a thousand years, O king; I shall dwell in the forest. No falsehood need be done by you on my account."

**2.31.26**  "For your welfare and prosperity, dear father, and for my return hereafter — go in peace, undisturbed, by a path free from all danger."

**2.31.27**  "But do not go this very night at all, my son; seeing your mother and me, spend this night here; gratified in all your desires, you may set out at dawn."

**2.31.28**  Then Rāma, having heard thus the words of his grief-stricken father, spoke sorrowfully to his brother Lakṣmaṇa.

**2.31.29**  "Whatever merits I may acquire today, who will grant me those same ones tomorrow? Therefore I choose departure itself over all pleasures."

**2.31.30**  "Let this earth — with its kingdoms, its people, abundant in wealth and grain, relinquished by me — be given over to Bharata."

**2.31.31**  "Let your grief depart; do not be overwhelmed with tears; for the invincible ocean, lord of rivers, is not disturbed."

**2.31.32**  "I desire neither kingship, nor comfort, nor even Maithilī — I desire only your truth, not untruth, O bull among men."

**2.31.33**  "The city, the kingdom, and the whole earth — relinquished by me — let them be given to Bharata; I, honoring your command, shall go to the forest to serve it for a long time."

**2.31.34**  "Let Bharata govern this earth — relinquished by me — with its mountain ranges, its cities, and its forests, auspicious and well-bounded; only let what you have said, O king, stand as it is."

**2.31.35**  "My mind is not so fixed on great pleasures, O king, nor even on what is dear to myself, as it is on fulfilling your command approved by the righteous — let your sorrow on my account depart, O sinless one."

**2.31.36**  "Therefore today I would choose neither the imperishable kingdom, nor all pleasures, nor comfort, nor Maithilī, nor even life itself, so as to involve you in falsehood — let your vow of truth remain so."

**2.31.37**  "Eating fruits and roots in the forest, beholding mountains, rivers, and lakes, entering the forest with its manifold trees, I shall be content — may you find peace."

**2.32.1**  Then the scion of Ikṣvāku, tormented by this pledge, breathing in deep sighs with tears, spoke again and again.

**2.32.2**  "Charioteer, let an army of the fourfold force, richly furnished with gems, be swiftly prepared to escort Rāghava."

**2.32.3**  "Let graceful courtesans of excellence, and merchants of great wealth, spread out and adorn the prince's retinue."

**2.32.4**  "Those who depend upon him and those with whom he delights to display his valor — assign them here too, after giving them manifold gifts."

**2.32.5**  "Hunting deer and elephants, drinking the wild honey of the forest, beholding the various rivers, he will not remember the kingdom."

**2.32.6**  "Whatever treasury of grain and treasury of wealth I possess — let both follow Rāma as he dwells in the desolate forest."

**2.32.7**  "Performing sacrifices at sacred places, distributing gifts with fitting fees, and meeting with sages, he will dwell happily in the forest."

**2.32.8**  "Long-armed Bharata shall protect Ayodhyā; may glorious Rāma be fully provided with all that he desires and then return."

**2.32.9**  As the scion of Kakutstha spoke thus, fear came upon Kaikeyī; her face grew parched and her voice choked.

**2.32.10**  Dejected and fearful, Kaikeyī spoke: "O virtuous one, a kingdom emptied of its people, like wine from which the cream has been drunk, utterly tasteless and barren — Bharata will not accept such."

**2.32.11**  When Kaikeyī spoke thus, utterly shameless, in the most cruel manner, King Daśaratha said to her of the wide eyes: "Why do you torment me, harnessing me to a yoke not fit to bear?"

**2.32.12**  Kaikeyī, doubly enraged, said this to the king: "In your own lineage, Sagara obstructed his eldest son — known as Asamañja — and so this one also deserves to go."

**2.32.13**  Thus addressed, King Daśaratha only exclaimed "Shame!" and all the assembled people were mortified — but she took no heed of this.

**2.32.14**  There an aged chief minister, Siddhārtha by name, pure in conduct and highly regarded by the king, said this to Kaikeyī.

**2.32.15**  "Asamañja, seizing children to play with along the path and flinging them into the waters of the Sarayū — this wicked-minded one amused himself thus."

**2.32.16**  "Seeing this, all the citizens, enraged, said to the king: choose between Asamañja alone and us, O increaser of the realm."

**2.32.17**  "The king then said to them: what is the cause of this fear? And the subjects, questioned by the king, spoke their grievance."

**2.32.18**  "'This one, out of his senses, snatches our young children to play with and out of folly casts them into the Sarayū, enjoying incomparable delight.'"

**2.32.19**  "Hearing the words of his subjects, O lord of men, Sagara abandoned that harmful son, wishing to do what was dear to them."

**2.32.20**  "Thus the most righteous king Sagara indeed abandoned his son. What sin has Rāma committed, that he should be obstructed in this manner?"

**2.32.21**  Hearing the words of Siddhārtha, the king — his voice still more exhausted — spoke thus to Kaikeyī in a voice afflicted by grief.

**2.32.22**  "I shall follow Rāma today, abandoning the kingdom, comfort, and wealth; together with King Bharata, enjoy the kingdom at leisure for a long time as you please."

**2.33.1**  Having heard the words of the minister, Rāma then addressed Daśaratha, versed in courtesy, speaking in a courteous manner.

**2.33.2**  "For me who have renounced enjoyments, O king, living in the forest on forest fare, who have relinquished all attachments — what need is there of an escort?"

**2.33.3**  "For one who has given away the finest elephant and then sets his mind upon the halter-rope — what use is that rope's attachment to one who has relinquished the finest elephant?"

**2.33.4**  "So for me likewise, O best among the righteous, O lord of the earth — what use is an army? I renounce everything; let only bark-cloth garments be brought for me."

**2.33.5**  "Bring also a spade and a basket for me as I depart; I shall dwell in the forest for fourteen years."

**2.33.6**  Then Kaikeyī herself fetched bark-cloth garments and, shamelessly, before the multitude, said to Rāghava: "Put these on."

**2.33.7**  That tiger among men accepted the bark-cloth from Kaikeyī, laid aside his fine garment, and put on the ascetic's dress.

**2.33.8**  Lakṣmaṇa too, right there, setting aside his auspicious garments, took on the hermit's covering before his father.

**2.33.9**  Then Sītā — clad in silk — gazed at the bark-cloth meant for her own wearing, trembling like a doe caught in a snare.

**2.33.10**  Receiving it as if with shame, downcast in heart, she said to her husband — who was like the king of gandharvas: "How do the sages who dwell in the forest tie the bark-cloth?"

**2.33.11**  She, the daughter of Janaka, unskilled in this and ashamed, draped one strip of bark-cloth around her neck and held another in her hand, and stood there not knowing how to proceed.

**2.33.12**  Coming quickly to her, Rāma — best among upholders of dharma — himself tied the bark-cloth over Sītā's silk garment.

**2.33.13**  As she who had a protector was being dressed in bark-cloth as if she had none, all the people cried out: "Shame upon you, Daśaratha!"

**2.33.14**  Sighing a hot breath, the scion of Ikṣvāku said to that wife: "Kaikeyī, Sītā does not deserve to go clad in kusa-bark."

**2.33.15**  "Is not the banishment of Rāma itself enough of a sin for you, wicked woman? What need of these further wretched transgressions committed by you?"

**2.33.16**  Even as his father spoke thus, Rāma — already setting out for the forest — spoke these words to him who sat with his head bowed down.

**2.33.17**  "This Kausalyā — righteous, illustrious, my mother, aged and of noble character — let her not be held in contempt by you in an act censured by the gods."

**2.33.18**  "O giver of boons, you must take thought once more for her — bereft of me, plunged into an ocean of grief, facing a misfortune such as she has never known before."

**2.33.19**  "You must so provide for this my mother — who bore within her a child comparable to great Indra — that, wasting away in grief while I abide in the forest, she does not lay down her life and depart to the realm of Yama."

**2.34.1**  Having heard Rāma's words and having seen him clad in the garb of an ascetic, the king, together with his wives, lost all consciousness.

**2.34.2**  Tormented by grief, he could not look upon that Rāghava, nor, gazing at him, could he bring himself to speak — so wretched was he in his mind.

**2.34.3**  For a moment that lord of the earth, afflicted and senseless, lamented, his great arms hanging limp, his thoughts fixed on Rāma alone.

**2.34.4**  "Surely in a former life I must have separated many creatures from their young, or else slain living beings — and therefore this calamity has come upon me."

**2.34.5**  "Yet the life-breath does not depart the body before its appointed hour; even as I am tormented by Kaikeyī, death does not find me."

**2.34.6**  "I who look upon my own son standing before me, radiant as fire, having set aside his fine garments for the dress of an ascetic —"

**2.34.7**  "For the sake of one woman alone — Kaikeyī — who strives for her own ends and has resorted to this base deception, all this people suffers."

**2.34.8**  Having spoken these words, his eyes sealed shut with tears, he uttered once only the name "Rāma" and could speak no more.

**2.34.9**  After a moment that king of the earth recovered his senses and, with eyes brimming with tears, spoke these words to Sumantra:

**2.34.10**  "Harness the royal chariot with the finest horses and come; convey this most fortunate one beyond the boundaries of this kingdom."

**2.34.11**  "Such, I believe, is said to be the fruit of virtue for the virtuous — that a righteous hero is banished to the forest by both his father and his mother."

**2.34.12**  Understanding the king's command, Sumantra, swift of movement, harnessed the chariot adorned with horses and drove it there.

**2.34.13**  The charioteer, joining his palms together, announced to the prince that chariot adorned with gold and yoked with the finest steeds.

**2.34.14**  The king, knowing time and place, quickly summoned the treasurer who was engaged in the management of the treasury and spoke to him clearly, with full resolution.

**2.34.15**  "Count out the years of her exile and bring quickly the most precious garments and the finest ornaments befitting Vaidehī."

**2.34.16**  Thus addressed by the king, the treasurer went to the treasury and, having brought everything, gave it all immediately to Sītā.

**2.34.17**  She who was born of noble lineage, Vaidehī, setting forth for the forest, adorned her graceful limbs with those many-coloured ornaments.

**2.34.18**  Vaidehī, thus richly adorned, illumined that palace as the radiance of the rising sun illumines the sky at the hour of dawn.

**2.34.19**  Her mother-in-law embraced her in both arms, kissed her on the crown of the head, and spoke, even as Maithilī showed no sign of despair:

**2.34.20**  "In this entire world, women who are constantly honoured and pampered by their dear ones do not follow their husbands when they fall upon misfortune — such women are false."

**2.34.21**  "My son who has been sent into exile must not be slighted by you; whether he be penniless or wealthy, let him be your deity."

**2.34.22**  Understanding her mother-in-law's words, which were rooted in righteousness and truth, Sītā stood before her, folded her palms, and replied:

**2.34.23**  "I shall do all that the noble lady instructs me. I know well how one ought to conduct oneself toward one's husband — this I have heard and learnt."

**2.34.24**  "The noble lady need not count me among ignoble women. I am no more capable of straying from dharma than moonlight is of straying from the moon."

**2.34.25**  "A vīṇā without its strings does not sound; a chariot without its wheel does not move; a wife does not flourish in happiness without her husband, even if she has a hundred sons."

**2.34.26**  "A father gives in measure; a mother gives in measure; a son gives in measure — but a husband gives without measure. What woman would not revere such a giver?"

**2.34.27**  "Being thus, I am the best, well-versed in the highest and lowest teachings of dharma. Noble lady, why should I dishonour my husband? For a husband is indeed the deity of women."

**2.34.28**  Having heard Sītā's words, which touched the heart, Kausalyā — pure of nature — suddenly shed tears born at once of sorrow and of joy.

**2.34.29**  Then Rāma, supremely versed in dharma, drew near with joined palms to his mother who was honoured above all among the mothers, and addressed her:

**2.34.30**  "Mother, do not grieve. Attend to my father. The period of forest exile will come to an end swiftly."

**2.34.31**  "The fourteen years will pass for you as if in sleep, and you shall see me returned here, complete, surrounded by friends."

**2.34.32**  Having spoken these words of well-measured meaning to his mother, he looked and beheld the three hundred and fifty mothers gathered there.

**2.34.33**  To those mothers, equally distressed, the son of Daśaratha spoke this righteous word with palms joined together:

**2.34.34**  "Whatever harsh thing has been done by me from living together in close quarters, or from ignorance, please forgive me all of it — and I take leave of you all."

**2.34.35**  Then, as Rāghava spoke thus, a wail arose from those wives of the lord of men — like the cry of krauñca cranes.

**2.34.36**  The mansion of Daśaratha, which had once resounded like the rumble of murajas and paṇavas and thunder-clouds, now became utterly wretched, filled with lament and wailing, plunged into calamity.

**2.35.1**  Then Rāma, Sītā, and Lakṣmaṇa, with joined palms, embraced the king and, pitifully, performed the rite of circumambulation around him.

**2.35.2**  Having obtained the king's leave, Rāghava, the knower of dharma, dazed with grief, then paid obeisance to his mother together with Sītā.

**2.35.3**  After his brother, Lakṣmaṇa paid obeisance to Kausalyā, and then in turn clasped the feet of his own mother Sumitrā.

**2.35.4**  His mother, weeping, wishing his good, kissed Lakṣmaṇa of the mighty arms on the crown of the head as he bowed, and spoke to the son of Sumitrā:

**2.35.5**  "You were born for this forest sojourn, deeply devoted to those dear to you. Son, be not heedless toward Rāma as your elder brother proceeds on his way."

**2.35.6**  "Whether he be in adversity or in prosperity, he is your refuge, O sinless one. This is the dharma of the good in this world — to be obedient to one's elder brother."

**2.35.7**  "This is the eternal, fitting conduct of this lineage: giving, consecration in sacrifices, and the offering of one's body in battle."

**2.35.8**  "Know Rāma as Daśaratha, know me as Janaka's daughter, know the forest as Ayodhyā — go, dear child, at your ease."

**2.35.9**  Then Sumantra, humble and well-versed in courtesy — as Mātali addresses Vāsava — spoke to that scion of the Kākutstha line with joined palms:

**2.35.10**  "Mount the chariot — good fortune to you, O prince of great renown. I shall swiftly convey you, Rāma, wherever you direct me."

**2.35.11**  "For fourteen years are to be spent by you in the forest; those years must now be entered upon, as the queen has urged upon you."

**2.35.12**  Sītā, that noble lady, adorned herself and then, with a joyful heart, mounted that chariot resplendent as the sun.

**2.35.13**  Likewise the two brothers placed in the body of the chariot all manner of weapons, their armour, and the leather quivers with their hard casings.

**2.35.14**  Having seen the three — Rāma, Sītā, and Lakṣmaṇa — mounted, Sumantra boldly urged on the chosen horses, swift as the wind in their speed.

**2.35.15**  But as Rāghava set out for the great forest for so many long nights, a swoon fell over the city, a swoon fell over the army and the people alike.

**2.35.16**  That city, thrown into confusion and agitation, resounded with great noise — the trumpeting of frenzied elephants and the jingling of horses.

**2.35.17**  Then that city — young and old together — stricken with extreme anguish, rushed after Rāma alone, as the heat-parched earth rushes toward water.

**2.35.18**  Clinging to the sides and the rear of the chariot, all faces turned toward him, their faces filled with tears, all spoke to him in deep distress:

**2.35.19**  "Hold back the reins of the horses, charioteer — drive slowly, slowly! I would behold Rāma's face, which shall be hard for us to see again."

**2.35.20**  "Surely the heart of Rāma's mother must be made of iron, since it does not break even as one who is like the offspring of a god goes to the forest."

**2.35.21**  "Vaidehī has truly accomplished her life's purpose — like a shadow she follows her husband, she who is devoted to dharma forsakes him not, as the sun's radiance does not forsake Mount Meru."

**2.35.22**  "O Lakṣmaṇa, you have achieved your purpose, you who shall attend upon your brother who is ever kind in speech and radiant as a god."

**2.35.23**  "This indeed is a great achievement for you; this is a great rise of fortune; this is the very path to heaven — that you follow him." So saying, those people could not hold back the tears that had come upon them.

**2.35.24**  Then the king, his spirit utterly desolate, surrounded by wretched women, came forth from the palace, saying "I will see my dear son."

**2.35.25**  And ahead of him was heard the great wailing of the women weeping — like the cry of she-elephants when a great bull elephant is bound in chains.

**2.35.26**  And the father, that glorious king of the Kākutstha line, appeared at that moment stricken and diminished — like the full moon at the appointed time swallowed by a eclipse.

**2.35.27**  Then a great tumult of wailing arose behind Rāma as the people looked upon the king sinking under his terrible grief.

**2.35.28**  Some among the people cried "Ah, Rāma!" and others cried "Ah, Rāma's mother!" — and the prosperous inner palace lamented and wept all around.

**2.35.29**  As Rāma looked back he saw the king — dejected, his mind reeling — and his mother both following along the road; bound by the bonds of dharma, he could not openly look upon them.

**2.35.30**  Seeing those two who deserved to ride in a vehicle, who deserved no sorrow and were accustomed to happiness, now proceeding on foot, he urged the charioteer: "Drive swiftly!"

**2.35.31**  For that tiger among men could not endure that painful sight of his father and mother — as an elephant cannot endure the goad.

**2.35.32**  Again and again he looked back at Kausalyā weeping and running after that chariot, crying "Rāma! Rāma!" and "Ah, Sītā! Lakṣmaṇa!" — his mother then appeared to him as though she were dancing.

**2.35.33**  The king cried out "Stop!" while Rāghava called out "Drive on! Drive on!" — and Sumantra's spirit was caught between the two, as between the two wheels of a chariot.

**2.35.34**  "Even if you are reproached, you can say to the king: 'I did not hear' — for this deception, long the cause of suffering, is the least sinful course," so Rāma spoke to him.

**2.35.35**  Acting on Rāma's word and taking leave of those people, the charioteer urged on the horses to speed, even as they were already in motion.
