**2.35.36**  The people of the king turned back after circumambulating Rāma — yet in their hearts, and in the rush of their tears, no human being turned back.

**2.35.37**  "One who wishes him to return should not follow him far" — thus the ministers spoke their word to the great king Daśaratha.

**2.35.38**  Hearing their words, endowed with every quality, the wretched king — his body bathed in sweat, his appearance sunken — stood still together with his wife, his eyes fixed upon his son.

**2.36.1**  When that tiger among men departed with his palms joined, a great cry of anguish arose from the women in the inner apartments.

**2.36.2**  "He who was the resort and refuge of this helpless, weak, and afflicted people — where now does that protector go?"

**2.36.3**  "He who does not grow angry even when falsely accused, who avoids all occasions of wrath, who appeases those who are wrathful, who regards the suffering of others as his own — where does he go?"

**2.36.4**  "He of great splendour, that great-souled one, who conducts himself toward us exactly as he conducts himself toward his mother Kausalyā — where does he now go?"

**2.36.5**  "Urged to the forest by a king tormented by Kaikeyī — he who was the protector of these people and of the world — where does he now go?"

**2.36.6**  "Alas, the king is without wisdom — Rāma, beloved of all the living world, devoted to dharma and to the vow of truth, will now dwell in forest exile!"

**2.36.7**  Thus all those great queens, afflicted with sorrow, wept and wailed aloud, like cows that have lost their calves.

**2.36.8**  The lord of the earth, already scorched by grief for his son, heard that terrible cry of anguish from the inner apartments and was plunged into yet deeper sorrow.

**2.36.9**  The sacred agnihotra fires were not kindled; the sun seemed to disappear; elephants dropped their mouthfuls of food; cows did not suckle their calves.

**2.36.10**  Triśaṅku, the red-limbed one (Mars), Bṛhaspati, and Budha too — all the planets, malefic, drew close to the Moon and stood there in dreadful conjunction.

**2.36.11**  The nakṣatras lost their radiance and the planets lost their lustre; Viśākhā herself appeared in the sky shrouded in smoke.

**2.36.12**  Suddenly all the townspeople fell into a state of dejection; no one applied his mind either to eating or to recreation.

**2.36.13**  The people moving along the royal road had faces clouded with tears; not one person was to be seen with joy — all were given over entirely to grief.

**2.36.14**  The cool wind did not blow; the moon did not show its gentle face; the sun gave no warmth to the world — the entire universe was thrown into confusion.

**2.36.15**  Sons indifferent to their mothers, husbands indifferent to their wives, brothers indifferent to their brothers — all, abandoning everything, thought only of Rāma.

**2.36.16**  Those who were friends of Rāma had all become bewildered in mind; overwhelmed by the weight of grief, they did not then resort to their beds.

**2.36.17**  Then Ayodhyā, bereft of that great-souled one — as the earth bereft of Indra — shook terribly, oppressed by the weight of fear; her elephants, warriors, and troops of horses all cried out.

**2.37.1**  As long as the dust-cloud raised by his departure was visible, the lord of the Ikṣvākus kept his own eyes fixed upon it, unable to withdraw them.

**2.37.2**  As long as the king gazed upon his beloved and supremely righteous son, the earth seemed to swell and rise before him in the sight of that son.

**2.37.3**  When the lord of the earth could no longer see even the dust of Rāma's chariot, stricken with grief and utterly desolate, he fell to the ground.

**2.37.4**  Kausalyā, that noble lady, supported his right arm, while Kaikeyī, dear to Bharata, supported his left side.

**2.37.5**  The king, endowed with statecraft, righteousness, and propriety, looked at Kaikeyī and, his every sense tormented, spoke to her thus.

**2.37.6**  "Touch not my limbs, O Kaikeyī, you of wicked conduct! I have no wish to see you — you are neither wife nor kin to me."

**2.37.7**  "Those who depend upon you — I am not theirs, nor are they mine. You who have abandoned dharma in pursuit of mere self-interest — I renounce you."

**2.37.8**  "Whatever vows I made when I took your hand, whatever acts I performed when we circled the sacred fire — I hereby release all of that, both in this world and the next."

**2.37.9**  "If Bharata takes pleasure in this indestructible kingdom once he obtains it, let whatever he might offer on my behalf for my funerary rites never come to me."

**2.37.10**  Then Queen Kausalyā, wasted by grief, lifted up the lord of men who had been cast down and covered in dust, and turned back.

**2.37.11**  The righteous-souled king burned with remorse — as one who has slain a brahmin out of passion, as one who has touched sacred fire with bare hands — reflecting upon his ascetic son.

**2.37.12**  Turning back again and again, sinking into despondency on the chariot-path, the king's form shone not at all — like the sun when it is swallowed up in eclipse.

**2.37.13**  Tormented by sorrow, recalling his beloved son, the king lamented; and understanding that his son had reached the outskirts of the city, he cried out.

**2.37.14**  "The hoofprints of the finest steeds that bore my son are still visible on the road, yet that great-souled one himself is nowhere to be seen."

**2.37.15**  "Surely he will take shelter at the root of some tree this very night, and rest his head upon a log or a stone."

**2.37.16**  "And he will rise pitifully from the earth, smeared with dust, sighing deeply — like a great bull elephant rising from a stream."

**2.37.17**  "The forest-dwellers will surely behold long-armed Rāma, the lord of the world, rising and walking as though he were lordless and without refuge."

**2.37.18**  "Rejoice in your desire, O Kaikeyī! Rule the kingdom as a widow — for without that tiger among men, I cannot bring myself to live."

**2.37.19**  Thus lamenting, the king, surrounded by a throng of people, entered that finest of cities — like one who has bathed without performing the obligatory rites after an ill omen.

**2.37.20**  Its crossroads and inner courts were deserted; its shops and shrines were shuttered; its people were weary, weak, and grief-stricken; its great thoroughfares were no longer crowded.

**2.37.21**  Surveying the entire city and thinking only of Rāma, the lamenting king entered his palace as the sun enters a cloud.

**2.37.22**  The palace, bereft of Rāma, of Vaidehī, and of Lakṣmaṇa, was like a great deep lake, once unagitated, whose serpent has been carried off by Suparṇa.

**2.37.23**  "Take me quickly to the home of Kausalyā, Rāma's mother" — so saying, the king was led by the doorkeeper to her dwelling.

**2.37.24**  Having entered Kausalyā's apartments and even having mounted his couch there, his mind remained utterly distraught.

**2.37.25**  Seeing that, the great king, valiant though he was, raised his arm and cried aloud in a loud voice: "Ah, Rāghava! You have abandoned me!"

**2.37.26**  "Blessed indeed will those best of men be at that time — they shall live in happiness, embracing Rāma upon his return!"

**2.37.27**  "I do not see you, Kausalyā — touch me kindly with your hand. My eyes have followed after Rāma and even now will not come back."

**2.37.28**  Seeing the lord of men lying on the couch thinking only of Rāma, the queen sat beside him, her form utterly overcome with anguish, and she lamented with sighs and great difficulty.

**2.38.1**  Then Kausalyā, herself tormented by grief for her son, looked at the lord of the earth who had sunk upon his couch in sorrow, and spoke to the great king.

**2.38.2**  "O tiger among men, Kaikeyī, having sown her poison like a two-tongued serpent, will now roam freely — like a serpent that has shed its skin."

**2.38.3**  "Having exiled Rāma, that fortunate woman has achieved her desire and, composed, will continue to terrify me again and again — like a vicious serpent within the house."

**2.38.4**  "Better it would have been for Rāma to remain in the city begging for alms and living in our home than to grant his stepmother's wish — even if it meant his becoming her servant."

**2.38.5**  "Having toppled Rāma from his rightful place as she desired, Kaikeyī has assigned the share — as it were — to the rākṣasas, like sacrificial oblations cast into fire at a lunar rite."

**2.38.6**  "That hero with the gait of a lordly elephant, long-armed, bearing his bow — surely he has entered the forest now, accompanied by his wife and together with Lakṣmaṇa."

**2.38.7**  "By your consent to Kaikeyī's command, you have abandoned to forest-exile those who have never known hardship — what will become of them in the forest?"

**2.38.8**  "Those tender ones, stripped of every gem and jewel, banished in the fruiting season — how will they live pitifully, subsisting on fruit and roots?"

**2.38.9**  "Will that auspicious time ever come — the end of my grief — when I may behold Rāghava here once more, together with his wife and his brother?"

**2.38.10**  "On the very news of those two heroes' arrival — when will Ayodhyā again be glorious, its people rejoicing, adorned with tall raised banners?"

**2.38.11**  "When, beholding those two tigers among men returned from the forest, will the city rejoice with delight — as the ocean rejoices at the full moon?"

**2.38.12**  "When will that long-armed hero enter the city of Ayodhyā, placing Sītā before him on the chariot — as a bull leads the cow at his side?"

**2.38.13**  "When will thousands of living beings strew parched grain upon those two tamers of enemies, my sons, as they enter along the royal road?"

**2.38.14**  "When will the maidens, bearing flowers and the fruits of the twice-born, cheerfully circumambulate the city in their joy?"

**2.38.15**  "When will he — matured in wisdom and in years, radiant as the cāmara white yak-tail fan — he who knows dharma, approach me playfully as if he were a child of three?"

**2.38.16**  "Doubtless, O hero, I believe that in a former life I must have cruelly severed, out of wretched selfishness, the breasts of mother animals from their suckling young."

**2.38.17**  "And now I, loving and devoted, have been made childless — like a cow bereft of her calf by a lion, O tiger among men — as Kaikeyī has forcibly taken my young calf."

**2.38.18**  "For I, who have but one son — one so endowed with virtues and learned in all the śāstras — I cannot bring myself to live without my son."

**2.38.19**  "For me, not seeing my beloved son — long-armed and of great strength — there is no reason for this life to continue."

**2.38.20**  "This fire of grief for my son, having arisen, consumes me — as the blessed sun in the hot season scorches this earth with his blazing rays."

**2.39.1**  But as that foremost of noble women Kausalyā lamented thus, Sumitrā — herself steadfast in dharma — spoke to her these words befitting dharma.

**2.39.2**  "O noble lady, your son is endowed with all good qualities — that best of men. What purpose is served by your lamenting thus, by your pitiful weeping?"

**2.39.3**  "O noble lady, your son of great might has gone — having renounced the kingdom — doing right by his great-souled father who had given his word in truth."

**2.39.4**  "Rāma stands firm in that dharma which the virtuous have ever rightly practiced, which yields its fruit in the life beyond — he is the foremost of men and is never to be mourned."

**2.39.5**  "And Lakṣmaṇa, ever sinless and compassionate toward all beings, serves that great-souled one in the noblest manner — he has gained great good fortune through this."

**2.39.6**  "Vaidehī, though accustomed to comfort and well aware of the hardships of forest life, follows your son, the soul of righteousness — this too speaks of his greatness."

**2.39.7**  "The lord who waves as his banner throughout the world the flag that is his very glory, who is devoted to self-restraint, truth, and sacred vows — what has your son not already attained?"

**2.39.8**  "Knowing Rāma's purity and his supreme greatness, the sun himself surely would not dare to scorch his body with his rays."

**2.39.9**  "Auspicious at all times, blowing forth from the forest groves, the gentle wind — pleasantly cool, neither too cold nor too warm — will attend upon Rāghava."

**2.39.10**  "As Rāma sleeps at night, the moon — embracing the sinless one as a father would — will refresh him, touching him with its cool rays."

**2.39.11**  "And Brahmā himself bestowed divine weapons upon him of great might, having seen him slay in battle Timi's banner-son, the lord of the Dānavas."

**2.39.12**  "That bull among men, Rāma, will soon be consecrated together with these three — the earth, Vaidehī, and royal Śrī — as his companions."

**2.39.13**  "The tears of sorrow you now shed gazing upon him as he departed — from those same eyes you will quickly shed tears of joy."

**2.39.14**  "Seeing your son bow to you in greeting, surrounded by his beloved companions, you will quickly shed tears of gladness — like a rain-cloud of the monsoon season."

**2.39.15**  "Your boon-granting son will swiftly return to Ayodhyā and press your feet with his own soft and full hands."

**2.39.16**  Hearing those words of Lakṣmaṇa's mother, spoken to the mother of Rāma, the queen consort of the lord of men — immediately the grief in her body dissolved, like an autumn cloud diminished of its water.

**2.40.1**  The people, deeply devoted to that great-souled Rāma of true valour, followed him as he set out for his forest sojourn.

**2.40.2**  Even when the king and the circle of his friends were forcibly turned back, those who had followed Rāma's chariot would not themselves return.

**2.40.3**  For to the men dwelling in Ayodhyā, that one of great fame, endowed with every virtue, was as dear as the full moon.

**2.40.4**  That descendant of Kakutstha, though entreated by his own subjects, held true his word to his father and went on into the forest.

**2.40.5**  Gazing upon them with affection, drinking them in as it were with his eyes, Rāma addressed those subjects with the tenderness one shows one's own children.

**2.40.6**  "The love and high regard that you, the residents of Ayodhyā, bear toward me — let that be especially directed to Bharata for my sake.

**2.40.7**  He is of auspicious conduct, the increaser of Kaikeyī's joy, and he will duly accomplish all that is pleasing and beneficial to you.

**2.40.8**  Though young in years, he is mature in wisdom, gentle in nature, endowed with the virtue of valour — he will be a worthy lord for you and a remover of all fear.

**2.40.9**  He has been recognized as a crown prince furnished with every royal virtue. And moreover, it is your duty, as men of good conduct, to follow the command of your lord.

**2.40.10**  And the great king must not grieve when I have gone into the forest — see to it so, I pray you, out of your desire to please me."

**2.40.11**  The more that son of Daśaratha stood firm in righteousness, the more his subjects desired him as their lord.

**2.40.12**  Rāma, together with Saumitrī, seemed to draw back that wretched, tear-veiled populace by the bonds of his virtues, as if drawing them again to their seats.

**2.40.13**  Those brāhmaṇas, aged in the threefold manner — by knowledge, by years, and by vigour — their heads trembling with age, called out from a distance these words:

**2.40.14**  "Ho, ho, O swift horses of noble breed that carry Rāma — turn back, you must not go on! Be loyal to your lord: your lord is one to be borne forward, not to be borne away from the city to the forest!"

**2.40.15**  Beholding those aged brāhmaṇas lamenting in such anguished cries, Rāma suddenly descended from the chariot.

**2.40.16**  He then went on foot, together with Sītā and Lakṣmaṇa, Rāma placing his steps close to the ground, his course set upon the forest.

**2.40.17**  That Rāma, devoted to righteous conduct, compassionate of eye, could not bear to pass those brāhmaṇas who went on foot by riding ahead in his chariot.

**2.40.18**  Seeing him walking on nonetheless, their minds overwhelmed with distress, those brāhmaṇas, utterly anguished, addressed this speech to Rāma:

**2.40.19**  "All this entire brāhmaṇa community, devoted to brāhmaṇical conduct, follows you; even these sacred fires, borne upon the shoulders of brāhmaṇas, follow you.

**2.40.20**  Behold these parasols, earned through the Vājapeya sacrifice, following behind us like a flock of geese at the season of floods.

**2.40.21**  For you who have no parasol of your own and are scorched by the sun's rays, we shall make shade with these our Vājapeya-won parasols.

**2.40.22**  The mind of ours that was ever devoted to following the Vedic hymns — for your sake, dear child, that mind has now made itself devoted to following you into the forest.

**2.40.23**  The Vedas, which are our highest wealth, abide within our hearts; and our wives, guarded by their own virtue, will remain in our houses.

**2.40.24**  We shall make no further calculation about your going — our minds are nobly resolved. And when you yourself disregard dharma, what value is there in attending to dharma?

**2.40.25**  O man of self-restrained conduct, we implore you — turn back — by these our heads with hair white as swans, bowed down and grey with the dust of prostrations upon the earth.

**2.40.26**  The many sacrifices of the brāhmaṇas who have come here are spread out and begun; their completion, dear child, depends upon your return.

**2.40.27**  All creatures, both moving and unmoving, are filled with devotion to you. When these devotees implore you, show your devotion to your devotees.

**2.40.28**  These trees, unable to follow you, raised high by the force of their roots, are crying out as it were, driven by the rush of the wind.

**2.40.29**  Even the birds, motionless, having ceased eating and moving, fixed each upon a single tree, are beseeching you, O compassionate one to all beings."

**2.40.30**  As those brāhmaṇas cried out thus imploring him to return, the river Tamasā appeared there as if blocking the way of Rāghava.

**2.41.1**  Then Rāghava, having reached the beautiful bank of the Tamasā, looked up at Sītā and spoke these words to Saumitrī:

**2.41.2**  "This is the first night, O Saumitrī, since we set out for the forest. Good fortune in this forest sojourn — you must not be sorrowful.

**2.41.3**  See how the forests seem to weep on every side, as the deer and birds draw near to their respective shelters and settle there.

**2.41.4**  Today the city of Ayodhyā, my father's royal capital, will grieve for our departure, men and women alike — of that there is no doubt.

**2.41.5**  Bharata, who is truly righteous of soul, will comfort my father and my mother with words encompassing dharma, artha, and kāma.

**2.41.6**  Reflecting again and again upon Bharata's compassionate nature, I do not grieve for either my father or my mother, O Lakṣmaṇa.

**2.41.7**  You, O tiger among men, have done what had to be done by following me; for the sake of protecting Vaidehī, help must be sought.

**2.41.8**  But I shall pass this night, O Saumitrī, on water alone — this pleases me, even though a variety of forest food is available.

**2.41.9**  Having spoken thus to Saumitrī, Rāghava also addressed Sumantra: "Be vigilant, O gentle one, in attending to the horses," he said.

**2.41.10**  Sumantra, having attended to the horses, and when the sun had set, having given them abundant fodder, stood close by in readiness.

**2.41.11**  After the auspicious twilight had been duly observed and night had come, the charioteer, together with Saumitrī, prepared Rāma's bed.

**2.41.12**  Seeing that bed made of tree-leaves on the bank of the Tamasā, Rāma lay down upon it together with his wife and with Saumitrī.

**2.41.13**  Seeing his brother thus fallen fast asleep with his wife, Lakṣmaṇa recounted to the charioteer the manifold virtues of Rāma.

**2.41.14**  For Saumitrī, keeping vigil through that entire night and recounting the virtues of Rāma to the charioteer on the bank of the Tamasā, the sun rose.

**2.41.15**  At a distance from the Tamasā whose banks were crowded with herds of cattle, Rāma passed that night there together with his people.

**2.41.16**  Rising up, that one of great splendour, having observed those subjects, spoke to his brother Lakṣmaṇa, he of auspicious marks:

**2.41.17**  "See, O Saumitrī, these people who have abandoned all concern for us and even for their own homes are now fast asleep at the roots of the trees, O Lakṣmaṇa.

**2.41.18**  The manner in which these citizens are making a vow to prevent our return — they will forfeit their very lives rather than abandon their resolve.

**2.41.19**  While they are yet asleep, let us swiftly mount the chariot and depart upon the road where no fear comes from any quarter.

**2.41.20**  Hereafter again let these devoted residents of the Ikṣvāku city not sleep at the roots of trees on my account.

**2.41.21**  For it is the duty of princes to free their citizens from self-incurred suffering; certainly the people of the city must not be made to suffer through one's own actions."

**2.41.22**  Lakṣmaṇa replied to Rāma, who stood there like righteousness personified: "This pleases me, O greatly wise one — let us mount at once."

**2.41.23**  The charioteer then quickly harnessed the chariot with those finest of horses and, with joined palms, reported to Rāma that it was ready.

**2.41.24**  For the purpose of confusing the citizens, Rāma spoke these words to the charioteer: "Mount the chariot and drive northward, O charioteer.

**2.41.25**  Drive swiftly for a moment, then turn the chariot about and return; do this attentively so that the citizens do not discover me."

**2.41.26**  The charioteer, having heard Rāma's command, did exactly so, and having returned, reported the chariot to Rāma.

**2.41.27**  Rāghava, with all his equipment, mounted that chariot and crossed the swift-flowing river Tamasā with its eddying whirlpools.

**2.41.28**  Having crossed over, that long-armed and glorious one reached the great road — auspicious, thornless, and without fear for those who fear.

**2.41.29**  But when night had brightened into dawn, those citizens, without Rāghava, were stunned by grief, motionless, their minds stricken.

**2.41.30**  Their faces wasted with tears born of grief, looking here and there, those wretched ones could not catch even a glimpse of Rāma.

**2.41.31**  Then, having followed the road for a little while, overwhelmed by great despondency at the loss of the trail,

**2.41.32**  those nobly-minded men turned back at the loss of the chariot's tracks, saying: "What is this? What shall we do, struck down as we are by fate?"

**2.41.33**  Then, by the very same road by which they had come, with exhausted hearts, they all went back to Ayodhyā, the city whose good people were afflicted with grief.

**2.42.1**  For the city-dwellers who had followed Rāma and now turned back, their inner beings seemed to have departed, leaving them spiritless.

**2.42.2**  Each arriving at his own dwelling, surrounded by his children and wife, all shed tears with faces veiled by weeping.

**2.42.3**  Merchants neither rejoiced nor conducted their trade; their wares held no lustre; householders did not cook.

**2.42.4**  A man who had lost his wealth felt no grief upon seeing a great windfall of riches; a mother who had obtained her first-born son felt no joy.

**2.42.5**  In house after house women wept and, in their anguished grief, reproached their husbands who had come home, goading them with words as one goads elephants.

**2.42.6**  "What use are their houses to those who do not see Rāghava? What use are wives, or wealth, or sons, or even pleasures?

**2.42.7**  There is but one true man in this world — Lakṣmaṇa — who together with Sītā follows Kākutstha Rāma, serving him in the forest.

**2.42.8**  Blessed are those rivers, those lotus ponds and lakes, in whose pure waters Kākutstha will immerse himself and bathe.

**2.42.9**  The lovely forest glades, the great marshy rivers, and the mountains with their peaks will make Kākutstha radiant.

**2.42.10**  Whatever forest or mountain Rāma approaches, none of them will be able to receive him without honouring him, as though a beloved guest had arrived.

**2.42.11**  The mountains, adorned with garlands of varied flowers and bearing abundant clusters of blossoms, will out of compassion display to Rāma as he comes even the finest flowers and fruits out of season.

**2.42.12**  The trees on the mountain peaks, revealing manifold and wondrous waterfalls again and again, will delight Rāghava.

**2.42.13**  Where Rāma is there is no fear; there is no defeat there — for he is a hero, long-armed, and the son of Daśaratha.

**2.42.14**  "Before he has gone too far, let us follow Rāghava — the shade of the feet of such a great-souled lord is a sweet comfort; for he is this people's lord, their refuge, and their final resort."

**2.42.15**  "We women shall attend upon Sītā; you men shall attend upon Rāghava" — thus the women of the city, consumed by grief, spoke various such words to their husbands.

**2.42.16**  "Rāghava will provide your welfare and security in the forest; Sītā will provide welfare and security for the women folk."

**2.42.17**  Who could find contentment in this disagreeable, joyless habitation, with its people pining and longing, their minds stolen away?

**2.42.18**  If the kingdom should be under Kaikeyī's sway — unrighteous and without a true protector — then we have no use for life, let alone for sons or wealth.

**2.42.19**  She who has abandoned both her son and her husband for the sake of power — that Kaikeyī, the disgrace of her lineage — whom else would she spare?

**2.42.20**  We shall never dwell in the kingdom as hirelings of Kaikeyī — we swear this by our very lives and by the lives of our children!

**2.42.21**  She who mercilessly sends the son of the king of kings into exile — that unrighteous, wickedly acting woman — who could live happily under her sway?

**2.42.22**  For the lord of the earth will not survive when Rāma has gone into exile; when Daśaratha dies, clearly devastation will follow.

**2.42.23**  "Those of you whose merit is exhausted and who are truly wretched — drink poison mixed and prepared, or follow Rāghava, or go where you will never hear of him again."

**2.42.24**  Rāma has been wrongfully sent into exile, together with his wife and Lakṣmaṇa; we have been handed over to Bharata like animals to a butcher.

**2.42.25**  The women of the city, lamenting thus, cried out in fierce anguish as though at the approach of death.

**2.42.26**  Those women, afflicted on Rāma's account, wept and lamented helplessly as though their own son or brother had been sent away — for he had become dearer to them than their own sons.

**2.43.1**  Rāma too, that tiger among men, keeping his father's command ever in mind, covered a great distance through the remaining hours of the night.

**2.43.2**  As he journeyed on in this way, the auspicious night passed away; having duly observed the auspicious twilight rite, he pressed onward to the border of the realm.

**2.43.3**  Beholding villages with their far-flung boundaries and flowering forests, he sped swiftly past, as if borne by arrows, on those excellent horses.

**2.43.4**  He heard the words of the people dwelling in the villages along the way: "Shame on King Daśaratha, who has fallen under the sway of desire!"

**2.43.5**  "Alas, how cruel is Kaikeyī today — wicked, ever trailing wickedness, harsh, one who has shattered all propriety and pursues a terrible deed!"

**2.43.6**  "She who sends away into forest exile such a son of the king — righteous, supremely wise, compassionate, and ever diligent!"

**2.43.7**  Hearing these words of the people who dwelt in the villages, that hero, the lord of the Kosalas, passed beyond the land of Kosala.

**2.43.8**  Then, having crossed the river named Vedaśruti, whose waters are auspicious and wholesome, he set out toward the quarter once inhabited by the sage Agastya.

**2.43.9**  Having traveled for a long while, he then crossed the river Gomatī, whose waters are cool, whose banks are rich with cattle and meadows, and which flows down to the ocean.

**2.43.10**  And having passed beyond the Gomatī, Rāghava, with his swift-footed horses, crossed the river Syandikā, which resounds with the cries of peacocks and geese.

**2.43.11**  Rāma pointed out to Vaidehī that prosperous land, encircled by the kingdom, which was once granted to Ikṣvāku by King Manu in the days of old.

**2.43.12**  Repeatedly addressing the charioteer as "sūta," that glorious bull among men, his voice deep and resonant as a swan in rut, spoke thus:

**2.43.13**  "When shall I come back again and roam in sport through the flowering forest along the Sarayū, reunited with my mother and my father?"

**2.43.14**  "I do not overly crave the hunt in the forest of the Sarayū — yet this pleasure is peerless in the world and is esteemed by the assemblies of royal sages."

**2.43.15**  Thus that scion of Ikṣvāku journeyed on, addressing his charioteer in a sweet voice, speaking one thought after another as each came to his mind.

**2.44.1**  Having traversed the wide and lovely lands of Kosala, the elder brother of Lakṣmaṇa, that mighty-armed one, drew near to the city of Śṛṅgaverapura.

**2.44.2**  There Rāghava beheld the divine Gaṅgā, flowing across three paths, her waters auspicious and free of algae, holy and frequented by sages.

**2.44.3**  She was filled with the clamor of swans and sāras cranes, the cooing of cakravāka birds; she was frequented by śiṃśumāra dolphins, crocodiles, and serpents.

**2.44.4**  Gazing upon her, swirling with eddies set in motion by her waves, that great warrior spoke to the charioteer Sumantra: "Here we shall encamp today."

**2.44.5**  "Not far from the river stands this great iṅgudī tree, laden with many blossoms and tender shoots — let us stay right here, O charioteer."

**2.44.6**  Both Lakṣmaṇa and Sumantra replied "So be it" to Rāghava, and thereupon drove the horses up to that iṅgudī tree.

**2.44.7**  Rāma, that joy of the Ikṣvāku line, approached that lovely tree and descended from the chariot together with his wife and Lakṣmaṇa.

**2.44.8**  Sumantra too alighted, and having unharnessed those excellent horses, stood before Rāma who had seated himself at the foot of the tree, with hands joined in reverence.

**2.44.9**  There lived a king named Guha, a dear friend of Rāma and equal to him in spirit, born of the Niṣāda people, powerful, and renowned as the lord of that territory.

**2.44.10**  Hearing that Rāma, that tiger among men, had arrived in his territory, he came forward surrounded by his elders, ministers, and kinsmen.

**2.44.11**  Seeing the lord of the Niṣādas standing at a distance, Rāma together with Saumitri went forward and met with Guha.

**2.44.12**  Guha, filled with grief, embraced Rāghava and said: "Rāma, this land is as Ayodhyā to you — what may I do for you?"

**2.44.13**  Then, having gathered food and eatables of excellent quality in great variety, he quickly brought the honorific offering of arghya as well, and spoke these words:

**2.44.14**  "Welcome, O mighty-armed one! This whole earth is yours; we are your servants and you are our lord — graciously rule over our kingdom."

**2.44.15**  "Food to chew, food to eat, drink, and things to lick — all stand ready; and the finest beds are prepared, and fodder for your horses."

**2.44.16**  To Guha as he was still speaking, Rāghava replied: "We are honored and gladdened by you in every way."

**2.44.17**  Squeezing him with both his well-formed arms — both on account of his coming on foot and as a display of affection — he spoke these words:

**2.44.18**  "It is my good fortune to see you, Guha, in good health together with your kinsmen. Is all well with you in your kingdom, among your friends and in your wealth?"

**2.44.19**  "As for all that you have prepared out of affection, I accept it — yet I do not stand in need of receiving gifts."

**2.44.20**  "Know me to be one who wears kuśa grass and bark-cloth garments, who subsists on fruits and roots, devoted to dharma, an ascetic whose range is the forest."

**2.44.21**  "I desire nothing but fodder for the horses — by this much alone shall I be well honored by your good self."

**2.44.22**  "For these horses are beloved to the king, to my father Daśaratha — by having these horses well cared for shall I be truly honored."

**2.44.23**  Guha then straightway commanded his men right there: "Let both water and fodder for the horses be brought at once!"

**2.44.24**  Then, with his upper garment of bark-cloth, having duly attended the evening twilight rite, he took only water as his meal — the food was fetched by Lakṣmaṇa himself.

**2.44.25**  As he lay upon the ground together with his wife, Lakṣmaṇa washed his feet, and then, drawing near, stood leaning against the tree.

**2.44.26**  Guha too, together with the charioteer, conversing with Saumitri, kept vigilant watch over Rāma through the night, bow in hand and alert.

**2.44.27**  Thus, as that wise and glorious great-souled son of Daśaratha lay resting — he who had never known suffering, accustomed to comfort — that night passed slowly away.

**2.45.1**  Guha, burning with anguish, spoke to Rāghava — addressing Lakṣmaṇa, who kept watch without pretense for the sake of his brother:

**2.45.2**  "Dear friend, this comfortable bed has been prepared for your sake — rest well upon it, O prince, at your ease."

**2.45.3**  "All these people are accustomed to hardship; it is you who are accustomed to comfort. We shall keep watch through the night for the protection of Kākutstha."

**2.45.4**  "There is no one on earth dearer to me than Rāma — I speak this truth, and by truth itself I swear it to you."

**2.45.5**  "By his grace I hope for supreme fame in this world, for abundant attainment of dharma and for the sole attainment of wealth."

**2.45.6**  "Therefore I shall guard my dear friend Rāma as he sleeps with Sītā, bow in hand, together with my kinsmen on all sides."

**2.45.7**  "Nothing in this forest is unknown to me, who roam here constantly — indeed we could withstand even a very great fourfold army."

**2.45.8**  Lakṣmaṇa then replied to him: "Being guarded by you, O sinless one, who ever have dharma in view, we have nothing here to fear."

**2.45.9**  "How could I obtain sleep, or life, or pleasures, when the son of Daśaratha lies upon the bare ground together with Sītā?"

**2.45.10**  "Behold him who could not be overpowered in battle by all the gods and asuras together — now lying comfortably on the grass with Sītā."

**2.45.11**  "This one son of Daśaratha, who was obtained by sacred rites, austerities, and various efforts, possessing every auspicious mark — he alone."

**2.45.12**  "With him gone into exile, the king will not live long — the earth will surely become widowed very soon."

**2.45.13**  "The women, exhausted with weeping, having cried out with a great lament, have fallen silent — I believe the king's palace has grown quiet, dear friend."

**2.45.14**  "Kausalyā, and the king, and likewise my own mother — I do not hope that all of them will survive this night."

**2.45.15**  "My mother might yet live, sustained by care from Śatrughna — yet it is grief that Kausalyā, the mother of this hero, shall perish."

**2.45.16**  "That city, filled with devoted people, offering delight and joy to those who behold it, now touched by the calamity of the king, shall perish."

**2.45.17**  "Gone, utterly gone — without having fulfilled his heart's desire, without having installed Rāma in the kingdom, my father shall perish."

**2.45.18**  "Those whose purpose is accomplished — when that hour arrives and the king has passed — shall perform all the funerary rites and consecrate the lord of the earth."

**2.45.19**  "With its lovely squares and intersections, its great highways well laid out, adorned with palaces and mansions, graced by the finest of courtesans —"

**2.45.20**  "— thronged with chariots, horses, and elephants, resounding with the music of instruments, filled with every prosperity, and crowded with cheerful and well-fed people —"

**2.45.21**  "— rich with pleasure groves and gardens, distinguished by assemblies and festivals — may the happy ones roam through my father's royal capital."

**2.45.22**  "May we too, safe and sound, enter Ayodhyā together with him of true vow, when this forest sojourn has come to its end."

**2.45.23**  As the great-souled prince stood there, lamenting and stricken with grief, the night passed fully away.

**2.45.24**  For as that prince, devoted to the welfare of his people, spoke thus truthfully, Guha, oppressed by calamity and burning with grief out of his deep affection for his master, shed tears — like an elephant tormented by pain.

**2.46.1**  When the night had brightened into dawn, the great-famed Rāma, standing by the broad trees, spoke to Lakṣmaṇa, son of Sumitrā, that one bearing auspicious marks.

**2.46.2**  "The hour of the sun's rising is come; the blessed night has departed. There, dear one, that dark-plumaged bird, the kokila, calls out."

**2.46.3**  "The cry of peacocks is heard calling in the forest. Let us cross the Jāhnavī, dear friend — swift-flowing, bound for the ocean."

**2.46.4**  Understanding Rāma's words, Sumitrā's son, the joy of his friends, took leave of Guha and the charioteer and stood before his brother.

**2.46.5**  Then, fastening their quivers and girding on their swords, the two Rāghavas, bearing bows, went together with Sītā toward the Gaṅgā.

**2.46.6**  The charioteer, approaching Rāma — that knower of dharma — humbly with palms joined, said: "What shall I do for you?"

**2.46.7**  "Turn back" — so he told him — "this much has been done for me. Leaving the chariot behind, we shall go on foot into the great forest."

**2.46.8**  Then the charioteer Sumantra, distressed to see himself thus dismissed, spoke these words to the tiger among men, the Aikṣvāka prince.

**2.46.9**  "No one in this world has ever surpassed this — that you, together with your brother and wife, should dwell in the forest like a common man."

**2.46.10**  "I do not think there is any fruit in celibacy, in sacred study, or in gentleness and uprightness, if such misfortune has come upon you."

**2.46.11**  "Dwelling in the forest with the Vaidehī and your brother, you shall attain a station, O hero, as if conquering the three worlds."

**2.46.12**  "We are truly ruined, O Rāma, we who have been deceived by her — we shall come under the sway of that sinful Kaikeyī, sharers in sorrow."

**2.46.13**  Speaking thus, the charioteer Sumantra — so dear to himself as Rāma was — seeing Rāma gone far away, wept long in his grief.

**2.46.14**  Then, when his tears had subsided, Rāma spoke sweet words again and again to the charioteer, who had touched water and was ritually pure.

**2.46.15**  "I see no friend equal to you among the Ikṣvākus. Do what must be done so that King Daśaratha does not grieve for me."

**2.46.16**  "The lord of the earth is stricken in mind by grief, he is old, and he is weighed down by the burden of desire — it is for that reason I say this to you."

**2.46.17**  "Whatever the great-souled lord of the earth may command, whatever must be done for the sake of gratifying Kaikeyī's wish, do that without hesitation."

**2.46.18**  "For this purpose do kings rule their kingdoms — that their minds are not thwarted in any of their undertakings."

**2.46.19**  "So do what must be done, Sumantra, that the great king does not suffer reproach, nor does he faint from sorrow."

**2.46.20**  "Having saluted him, you should speak these words to the king on my behalf — to that king who has not known such sorrow before, who is old, noble, and master of his senses."

**2.46.21**  "Neither I grieve, nor Lakṣmaṇa, nor the Maithilī — neither at having been cast out from Ayodhyā, nor at dwelling in the forest."

**2.46.22**  "Once the fourteen years have passed, you will soon see Lakṣmaṇa, me, and Sītā returned again."

**2.46.23**  Having thus addressed the king, greet my mother, and the other queens together, including Kaikeyī, again and again.

**2.46.24**  "Convey wishes of good health to Kausalyā and the salutation at her feet — from Sītā, from me, and at the word of the noble Lakṣmaṇa."

**2.46.25**  "Tell the great king: 'Bring Bharata quickly.' And when Bharata has come, he must be established in the royal office according to the king's wish."

**2.46.26**  "Having embraced Bharata and consecrated him as crown prince, the sorrow born of grief for us will no longer overpower you."

**2.46.27**  "Bharata too must be told: 'As you conduct yourself toward the king, so you must conduct yourself toward all the mothers without distinction.'"

**2.46.28**  "Just as Kaikeyī and Sumitrā are for you without distinction, so too must Queen Kausalyā be honored — she is my mother especially."

**2.46.29**  Being dismissed by Rāma and emaciated by grief, Sumantra, having heard all those words, spoke out of affection to the Kākutstha.

**2.46.30**  "Whatever I might say out of devotion and unguarded love — without ceremony, without distress — you must forgive those words of a loyal servant."

**2.46.31**  "How shall I, bereft of you, return to that city which, dear one, is like one overwhelmed by the grief of losing a son, on account of your separation?"

**2.46.32**  "When the people saw the chariot with Rāma on it, how will the city bear seeing the chariot now without Rāma — it would shatter at the sight."

**2.46.33**  "The city will sink into anguish seeing this empty chariot with only the charioteer remaining — like an army that has lost its hero in battle."

**2.46.34**  "The people, thinking of you dwelling afar yet standing before them in their minds, have surely gone without food today."

**2.46.35**  "The cry of anguish that the citizens raised at the time of your exile — on seeing me seated in the chariot, they will raise it a hundredfold."

**2.46.36**  "And what shall I say to the queen — 'Your son has been taken by me to his maternal uncle's house; do not grieve'? How could I say even this?"

**2.46.37**  "I would not speak even a falsehood of that kind. And how shall I speak this truth, which is so painful?"

**2.46.38**  "These excellent horses that serve at my command, that have carried your kinsmen — how will they draw the chariot bereft of you?"

**2.46.39**  "If you resolve to abandon even me though I beg you, then the moment you forsake me here, I shall enter fire together with the chariot."

**2.46.40**  "Whatever creatures in the forest might disturb your austerities, I will ward them off with the chariot, O Rāghava."

**2.46.41**  "The comfort I have gained from the service of the chariot — I aspire now to the comfort of forest-dwelling, done for your sake."

**2.46.42**  "Be pleased to let me be next to you in the forest. I wish to be told with affection: 'Be next to my lord.'"

**2.46.43**  "I will with bowed head render service to you, dwelling in the forest. Ayodhyā and the world of the gods — I abandon them both entirely."

**2.46.44**  "I cannot enter that Ayodhyā without you — just as an evildoer cannot enter the capital of great Indra."

**2.46.45**  "These horses too, O hero — if they serve you while you dwell in the forest, they will attain the highest state."

**2.46.46**  "When the period of forest-dwelling has come to an end, this is my heart's desire: to carry you back to the city in this very chariot."

**2.46.47**  "The fourteen years spent in the forest in your company will pass like a moment; whereas otherwise they would stretch like hundreds of years."

**2.46.48**  "O lord who loves your servants — one who is devoted, who stands steadfast in his duty, who stands in the path that follows the master's son — you should not abandon me."

**2.46.49**  When Sumantra begged thus piteously in many ways again and again, Rāma, who was compassionate toward his servants, spoke these words to him.

**2.46.50**  "I know your supreme devotion toward me, O one devoted to your master. Listen also to the reason why I am sending you back to the city from here."

**2.46.51**  "When she sees you returned to the city, my younger mother Kaikeyī will gain confidence that Rāma has indeed gone to the forest."

**2.46.52**  "For when I have gone to dwell in the forest, that queen, being satisfied, would not unduly suspect the righteous king of speaking falsely."

**2.46.53**  "This is my foremost consideration: that my younger mother should obtain the prosperous kingdom safe under the rule of her son Bharata."

**2.46.54**  "For my sake and for the king's sake, go to the city with the chariot. And convey the various messages you have been charged with, just as I have told you."

**2.46.55**  Having spoken thus and comforted the charioteer again and again, Rāma spoke firm and reasoned words to Guha: "I shall make matted locks and go — bring me the sap of the nyagrodha tree."

**2.46.56**  Guha quickly brought that sap to the prince. With it, Rāma made matted locks for Lakṣmaṇa and for himself.

**2.46.57**  Then the two brothers, Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa, clad in bark garments and bearing coils of matted hair, shone like sages.

**2.46.58**  Then, having entered the path of the forest-ascetics together with Lakṣmaṇa and enjoined his vow, Rāma spoke to his companion Guha.

**2.46.59**  "Be ever vigilant, Guha, in matters of army, treasury, the fort, and the countryside — for a kingdom is held to be the most difficult thing to guard."

**2.46.60**  Then, having taken leave of Guha, the delight of the Ikṣvākus departed swiftly and undistracted, with his wife and together with Lakṣmaṇa.

**2.46.61**  Seeing a boat on the bank of the river, the delight of the Ikṣvākus, wishing to cross the swift-flowing Gaṅgā, spoke thus to Lakṣmaṇa.

**2.46.62**  "Board this waiting boat gently, O tiger among men, and help the noble-minded Sītā on board, holding her close to you."

**2.46.63**  Hearing his brother's instruction and not opposing any of it, he first helped the Maithilī on board, and then the self-controlled one climbed up himself.

**2.46.64**  Then the radiant one, the elder brother of Lakṣmaṇa, himself boarded; after that the lord of the Niṣādas, Guha, urged his kinsmen on.

**2.46.65**  Having bidden farewell to Sumantra and to Guha along with his forces, Rāma boarded the boat and urged on the boatmen.

**2.46.66**  Then, urged by them, the boat, steered by the helmsman and driven by the swift strokes of well-crafted oars, quickly crossed the waters.

**2.46.67**  Reaching the middle of the Bhāgīrathī, the blameless Vaidehī, joining her palms in reverence, spoke these words to that river.

**2.46.68**  "This son of the wise great king Daśaratha — may he fulfill the command, O Gaṅgā, protected by you."

**2.46.69**  "Having dwelt in the forest for a full fourteen years with his brother and with me, he will return again."

**2.46.70**  "Then, O auspicious goddess, having returned safely, I shall joyfully worship you, O Gaṅgā, for the fulfillment of all desires."

**2.46.71**  "For you, O goddess, flow in three worlds and behold the realm of Brahmā; you are known in this world as the wife of the lord of the ocean."

**2.46.72**  "I salute you, O goddess, and praise you, O beautiful one, when the tiger among men has returned safely after regaining his kingdom."

**2.46.73**  "Hundreds of thousands of cows, fine garments, and food — I shall give these to Brāhmaṇas out of my desire to please you."

**2.46.74**  Speaking thus, the blameless Sītā, turning to the right, quickly reached the right bank.

**2.46.75**  Having reached the far bank and leaving the boat behind, the bull among men, the scorcher of foes, set forth together with his brother and the Vaidehī.

**2.46.76**  Then the mighty-armed one spoke to the joy-increaser of Sumitrā: "Go ahead, O Saumitrī; let Sītā follow you."

**2.46.77**  "I shall go behind, guarding both you and Sītā. Today the Vaidehī will come to know the hardship of forest life."

**2.46.78**  But Sumantra, that wretched one, gazing steadily at Rāma who had quickly crossed to the far bank of the Gaṅgā, with his gaze finally turned back by the distance of the road, shed tears in his anguish.

**2.46.79**  Those two, having slain four great forest animals there — a boar, a ṛśya antelope, a spotted deer, and a large deer — took the pure meat and, being hungry, quickly went at the proper time to a tree for shelter.

**2.47.1**  Having reached that tree and performed the evening twilight rite, Rāma, best among those who bring delight, spoke to Lakṣmaṇa.

**2.47.2**  "This is the first night spent outside the inhabited land, away from Sumantra — you should not be anxious about it."

**2.47.3**  "From this day forward we must both keep vigil through the nights without slackness, for the safety and well-being of Sītā rests on us."

**2.47.4**  "Somehow, Saumitrī, we shall pass this night, lying down on the ground on what we have gathered ourselves."

**2.47.5**  Lying down on the earth — he who was accustomed to beds of the highest worth — Rāma spoke these auspicious words to Saumitrī.

**2.47.6**  "Surely the great king sleeps in sorrow tonight, Lakṣmaṇa. But Kaikeyī, having achieved her desire, is fit to be satisfied."

**2.47.7**  "For that queen Kaikeyī, for the sake of the kingdom, would not even spare the life of the great king — not even when she sees Bharata arrived."

**2.47.8**  "Helpless, old, bereft of me, and enslaved to desire, come under Kaikeyī's control — what will the king do?"

**2.47.9**  "Looking at this calamity and at the king's confusion of mind, I think desire is greater than both wealth and dharma."

**2.47.10**  "For what man, even an ignorant one, would for the sake of a woman abandon a son who follows his wishes — as our father has abandoned me, Lakṣmaṇa?"

**2.47.11**  "How fortunate Bharata, son of Kaikeyī, must be — with his wife beside him, he alone will enjoy the joyful Kosalas like an overlord."

**2.47.12**  "For he will be the sole master of the whole kingdom, when our father has passed beyond his years and I have taken refuge in the forest."

**2.47.13**  "One who abandons wealth and dharma and follows desire alone quickly meets such ruin as King Daśaratha has met."

**2.47.14**  "I believe that Kaikeyī has come to bring about the end of Daśaratha, my banishment, and the kingdom for Bharata, O gentle one."

**2.47.15**  "I only hope that Kaikeyī, intoxicated by the pride of her good fortune, does not now oppress Kausalyā and Sumitrā on my account."

**2.47.16**  "Let not Queen Sumitrā suffer grief on my account. You should enter Ayodhyā from here when the time comes, Lakṣmaṇa."

**2.47.17**  "I alone will go to the Daṇḍakas with Sītā. For you will be the protector of Kausalyā, who would otherwise be without protector."

**2.47.18**  "For Kaikeyī, given to petty acts, might out of hatred do what is unjust. You must entrust my mother to the dharma-knowing Bharata."

**2.47.19**  "Surely in some past life women were separated from their sons — and now that same fate has come upon my mother, Saumitrī."

**2.47.20**  "Kausalyā, who nurtured me for so long and raised me through suffering, is now separated from me at the time of bearing fruit — shame on me!"

**2.47.21**  "Let no woman, Saumitrī, ever give birth to a son like me, who brings endless grief to his mother."

**2.47.22**  "I think that myna bird of Lakṣmaṇa is more blessed than I am — the one from whom those words are heard: 'Parrot, bite the foot of the enemy.'"

**2.47.23**  "What use is a son to one who is without son's benefit — to my grieving, ill-fortuned mother, when I have done nothing to help her, O subduer of foes?"

**2.47.24**  "Ill-fated is my mother Kausalyā, bereft of me — she lies stricken with the deepest sorrow, fallen into an ocean of grief."

**2.47.25**  "I alone, were I angry, could overcome Ayodhyā and the whole earth with my arrows — though valor without cause is no virtue, Lakṣmaṇa."

**2.47.26**  "But I am afraid of unrighteousness and afraid for the other world, O sinless one — it is for that reason, Lakṣmaṇa, that I do not consecrate myself today."

**2.47.27**  Lamenting thus and in many other piteous ways in that lonely place, his face full of tears, Rāma sat down in silence in the night.

**2.47.28**  Lakṣmaṇa comforted Rāma when he had finished his lament — like a fire whose flames have died down, like an ocean whose waves have stilled.

**2.47.29**  "Surely today, O Rāma, O best among warriors, the city of Ayodhyā is without luster since your departure — like a night bereft of the moon."

**2.47.30**  "It is not fitting, O Rāma, that you should torment yourself thus and grieve both Sītā and me, O bull among men."

**2.47.31**  "Neither Sītā without you nor I without you, O Rāghava, could live even for a moment — like fish drawn out of water."

**2.47.32**  "I would not wish to see our father, nor Śatrughna, nor Sumitrā, O scorcher of foes, nor even heaven itself without you."

**2.47.33**  Hearing these noble and full words of Lakṣmaṇa, the Rāghava, that scorcher of foes, having embraced dharma for a long time, composed himself with reverence for all the years of forest life ahead.

**2.48.1**  Having spent the auspicious night at that great tree, when the pure sun had risen, they departed from that place.

**2.48.2**  They went toward the place where the Bhāgīrathī Gaṅgā approaches the Yamunā, penetrating deep into the great forest.

**2.48.3**  Those illustrious ones came upon the earth through various regions — charming places never seen before — beholding them here and there.

**2.48.4**  Proceeding at ease and observing various trees, when the day had just turned, Rāma spoke to Saumitrī.

**2.48.5**  "Look around Prayāga, Saumitrī, at the tall column of smoke — the banner of the sacred fire. I think the sage is nearby."

**2.48.6**  "Surely we have reached the confluence of the Gaṅgā and the Yamunā — for the sound of water meeting water is heard."

**2.48.7**  "Wood cut by those who live off the forest, and these various trees are visible — this is the hermitage of Bharadvāja."

**2.48.8**  Those two bearing bows, traveling comfortably as the sun hung low, reached the abode of the sage at the confluence of the Gaṅgā and Yamunā.

**2.48.9**  Rāma, having approached the hermitage and startling the deer and birds, walked a moment's distance and came to Bharadvāja.

**2.48.10**  Then the two heroes, wishing to see the sage, having approached the hermitage with Sītā following them, stood at a respectful distance.

**2.48.11**  Seeing the great one who had offered his agnihotra oblation, Rāma, with joined palms, together with Saumitrī and with Sītā, saluted him.

**2.48.12**  The elder brother of Lakṣmaṇa presented himself to him: "We two are the sons of Daśaratha, O venerable one — Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa."

**2.48.13**  "This is my wife, the Vaidehī, the auspicious daughter of Janaka — blameless, she has followed me into this desolate forest hermitage."

**2.48.14**  "As I was being sent into exile by my father, this dear younger brother Saumitrī, firm in his vow, followed me into the very forest."

**2.48.15**  "Commanded by our father, O venerable one, we shall enter the forest hermitage; there we shall practice dharma alone, subsisting on roots and fruits."

**2.48.16**  Hearing those words of the wise prince, the righteous-souled one then brought forth a cow, arghya water, and drinking water.

**2.48.17**  Surrounded on all sides by deer, birds, and sages, the sage honored Rāma on his arrival and welcomed him with the word of welcome.

**2.48.18**  Having received that honor, Bharadvāja then spoke these dharma-filled words to Rāma as he sat down together with his companions.

**2.48.19**  "At long last, O Kākutstha, I see you arrived here. I have heard of this causeless exile of yours."

**2.48.20**  "This spot is secluded, at the confluence of two great rivers — sacred and beautiful. May your honor dwell here in happiness."

**2.48.21**  Being thus spoken to by Bharadvāja, the Rāghava Rāma, devoted to the welfare of all, replied with auspicious words.

**2.48.22**  "O venerable one, the people of the city and countryside are close by here; crowds will come to gaze upon the Vaidehī and upon me. For this reason I am not pleased to dwell here."

**2.48.23**  "Look, O venerable one, for an excellent and solitary hermitage-place where the Vaidehī, the daughter of Janaka who deserves happiness, may find delight."

**2.48.24**  Hearing these auspicious words of the Rāghava, the great sage Bharadvāja then spoke words that grasped his meaning.

**2.48.25**  "Ten krośas from here, dear one, is a mountain where you shall dwell — sacred, frequented by great sages, pleasant to behold from every side."

**2.48.26**  "Roamed by golāṅgūla monkeys, frequented by monkeys and bears — known as Citrakūṭa, it resembles the Gandhamādana mountain."

**2.48.27**  "As long as a person looks upon the peaks of Citrakūṭa, his mind settles in what is auspicious and does not turn toward evil."

**2.48.28**  "Many sages there, having sported through hundreds of autumns, have ascended to heaven through austerity — together with Kapālaśiras."

**2.48.29**  "I think that secluded dwelling will be pleasant for you — or else dwell here for your forest sojourn with me, O Rāma."

**2.48.30**  That knower of dharma, Bharadvāja, received Rāma — that beloved guest — together with his wife and brother, with all manner of hospitalities.

**2.48.31**  For Rāma, having come to Prayāga and arrived at that great sage, the sacred night passed by as they told various tales.

**2.48.32**  When the night had brightened into dawn, the tiger among men came to Bharadvāja and spoke to the sage of blazing splendor.

**2.48.33**  "O one of truthful nature, this night just dawning — we have spent it here in your hermitage. May your honor grant us leave for our dwelling."

**2.48.34**  When that night had been passed, Bharadvāja spoke thus: "Go to Citrakūṭa, which abounds in honey, roots, and fruits."

**2.48.35**  "There herds of elephants and herds of deer roam on every side through the forest glades — you will see them, O Rāghava."

**2.48.36**  "Reaching that auspicious mountain, resounding with the cries of delighted koyaṣṭika birds and kokila cuckoos, filled with intoxicated deer and many elephants — most beautiful — set up your hermitage and dwell there."

**2.49.1**  Having spent the night there, the two princes, those tamers of foes, saluted the great sage and then set out toward that mountain.

**2.49.2**  Watching them depart, he followed after them as a father follows his sons; then that great sage began to speak these words.

**2.49.3**  "When you reach the Kālindī, the swiftly flowing river, there you must build a raft and cross the river Aṃśumatī."

**2.49.4**  "Then, having reached the great banyan tree with its green canopy, grown tall amid many trees, dark and frequented by siddhas —"

**2.49.5**  "— having gone just one krośa from there, O Rāma, you will see a dark forest, mixed with palāśa trees, badara shrubs, and the bamboo groves of the Yamunā."

**2.49.6**  "That is the path to Citrakūṭa, which I have traversed many times — pleasant, gentle underfoot, and free of forest fires." Having thus pointed out the road, that great sage turned back.

**2.49.7**  When that sage had turned back, Rāma said to Lakṣmaṇa: "We are truly blessed, O son of Sumitrā, that the sage shows such compassion toward us."

**2.49.8**  Having thus taken counsel together, those two tiger-like men, steadfast in mind, placed Sītā ahead of them and proceeded to the river Kālindī.

**2.49.9**  Then the two of them assembled a very large raft from logs; Lakṣmaṇa, cutting the timber, fashioned it for Sītā's comfort and peace of mind.

**2.49.10**  There Rāma, son of Daśaratha, lifted his beloved — who was like an unimaginable Śrī, slightly bashful — onto the raft.

**2.49.11**  Then on the raft they crossed the Yamunā — swift-flowing Aṃśumatī, garlanded with waves, its banks lined with many trees.

**2.49.12**  Having crossed, they abandoned the raft and, setting forth from the Yamunā forest, they reached the dark banyan tree, cool and green-leafed.

**2.49.13**  "May I see Kausalyā, and glorious Sumitrā as well" — with these words Sītā, folding her hands in reverence, circumambulated the lord of the forest.

**2.49.14**  Having gone just one krośa from there, the two brothers, Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa, slew many pure deer and roamed about in the Yamunā forest.

**2.49.15**  Having sported in the auspicious forest resounding with flocks of peacocks, full of elephants and monkeys, they reached together the bank of the river and came to their appointed campsite — undaunted in bearing.

**2.50.1**  When the night had passed and he had slept, the joy of the Raghus gently awakened Lakṣmaṇa.

**2.50.2**  "O son of Sumitrā, hear the sweet calls of the forest birds; it is time, O scorcher of foes, to set out on our journey."

**2.50.3**  Lakṣmaṇa, who had been sleeping, was roused at the right moment by his brother; he shook off sleep, drowsiness, and the accumulated fatigue of the road.

**2.50.4**  Rising then, all of them touched the auspicious water of the river and set out on the path to Citrakūṭa that the sage had pointed out.

**2.50.5**  Then Rāma, setting forth at the right hour together with the son of Sumitrā, spoke these words to Sītā of lotus-petal eyes.

**2.50.6**  "Behold, O Vaidehī, the trees blazing as if aflame with blossoms all around — the kiṃśuka trees, adorned with their own flowers, as winter draws to its end."

**2.50.7**  "Behold the bhallātaka trees in full bloom, untouched by men, bending low with their fruit and leaves — surely I shall be able to live here."

**2.50.8**  "See, O Lakṣmaṇa, the honeycombs the size of a droṇa measure hanging from tree to tree, filled by the bees."

**2.50.9**  "Here the krauñca cries out, and the peacock calls back to it, in a charming corner of the forest dense with a carpet of flowers."

**2.50.10**  "Behold this mountain Citrakūṭa, followed by herds of elephants, resounding with flocks of birds, its peaks towering high."

**2.50.11**  Then the two of them, going on foot together with Sītā, reached the charming and lovely mountain Citrakūṭa.

**2.50.12**  Having reached that mountain teeming with diverse flocks of birds, he said: "O gentle one, let this be our dwelling; here let us take our delight for now."

**2.50.13**  "O Lakṣmaṇa, bring strong and excellent timbers and build a shelter, O gentle one — my mind delights in dwelling here."

**2.50.14**  Hearing those words of his, the son of Sumitrā brought various trees and thereupon built a leaf hut, O tamer of foes.

**2.50.15**  To him who was attentively and single-mindedly at work, Rāma spoke these words: "Having brought the flesh of a black antelope, let us consecrate the hall with sacrifice."

**2.50.16**  Then Lakṣmaṇa of great glory slew a pure black antelope and cast it onto the blazing sacrificial fire.

**2.50.17**  Knowing it to be well cooked, fully roasted, its blood drained, Lakṣmaṇa then spoke to Rāghava, that tiger among men.

**2.50.18**  "This dark one, whole in all its limbs, is cooked — the black antelope is ready. O god-like one, make the offering to the deity; you are skilled in this."

**2.50.19**  Rāma, having bathed and composed himself — virtuous, versed in recitation — performed the excellent oblation that pacifies sin.

**2.50.20**  Into that leaf-thatched dwelling — pleasing, well built in the right proportions, sheltered from the wind — all of them entered together for the night, as the hosts of gods enter the hall of Sudharmā.

**2.50.21**  In that excellent forest — swarming with countless diverse beasts and birds, adorned with trees bearing clusters of bright flowers, echoing with wild animals — they, masters of their senses, sported in great delight.

**2.50.22**  Having reached the supremely lovely Citrakūṭa and the river Mālyavatī with its good fords, teeming with deer and birds, Rāma rejoiced with gladness and left behind the grief of his banishment from the city.

**2.51.1**  Having spoken at length with Sumantra, deep in sorrow, when Rāma had reached the southern bank, Guha went to his own home.

**2.51.2**  Then Sumantra, having received leave, hitched the excellent horses and departed for the city of Ayodhyā, his heart deeply sorrowful.

**2.51.3**  Beholding fragrant forests, rivers, and lakes, he swiftly passed through villages and towns.

**2.51.4**  Then at the hour of evening, on the third day, the charioteer reached Ayodhyā and beheld it joyless.

**2.51.5**  Seeing it as though empty and silent, deeply downcast, Sumantra fell to brooding, struck by the force of grief.

**2.51.6**  "Has the city, with its elephants and horses, its people and its lord, been consumed by the fire of grief, burned by the anguish of sorrow over Rāma?" — so reflecting, the charioteer hastened and entered.

**2.51.7**  Hundreds and thousands of men ran up to Sumantra as he came, asking the charioteer: "Where is Rāma?"

**2.51.8**  He reported to them: "At the Gaṅgā I took leave of Rāghava; I was granted leave and turned back by that righteous great soul."

**2.51.9**  The people, learning that they had crossed, their faces filled with tears, sighed "Alas!" and cried aloud "O Rāma!"

**2.51.10**  And he heard the words of those standing in groups: "We are indeed undone, we who cannot see Rāghava here."

**2.51.11**  "At gifts and sacrifices, at marriages, at great assemblies — we shall never again see righteous Rāma among us."

**2.51.12**  "What is best for this people, what is pleasing, what brings happiness?" — thus had Rāma tended the city like a father.

**2.51.13**  He also heard the lamentations of women standing at their windows along the streets of the market, scorched by grief for Rāma.

**2.51.14**  Sumantra, covering his face, proceeded along the middle of the royal road to the very palace where King Daśaratha dwelt.

**2.51.15**  Swiftly dismounting from the chariot and entering the royal palace, he traversed the seven courts crowded with great throngs of people.

**2.51.16**  Then from the palaces here and there he heard the soft murmuring of Daśaratha's women, scorched by grief for Rāma.

**2.51.17**  "The charioteer who went out with Rāma has returned here without Rāma — what shall he ever say to Kausalyā who is mourning?"

**2.51.18**  "And I believe life is hard — truly it is no easy thing — since Kausalyā goes on living when her son has been taken away and sent forth."

**2.51.19**  Hearing those words of the king's women to be true in every sense, he entered the palace at once, blazing as it were with grief.

**2.51.20**  Entering the eighth court, he saw the king — wretched, incomparable in his anguish, utterly wasted by grief for his son — in the white inner chamber.

**2.51.21**  Approaching the lord of men as he sat and saluting him, Sumantra reported Rāma's words exactly as they had been spoken.

**2.51.22**  Hearing that, the king remained silent; his senses confused, he fainted and fell to the ground, tormented by grief for Rāma.

**2.51.23**  Then the inner apartments were thrown into commotion when the lord of the earth fainted; when the king fell to the ground, cries arose with arms upraised.

**2.51.24**  Then Kausalyā, together with Sumitrā, raised her fallen husband and spoke these words.

**2.51.25**  "O greatly fortunate one, why do you not speak to this messenger of that great doer of difficult deeds, who has arrived from the forest exile?"

**2.51.26**  "Today, having done this wrong to him, you are ashamed, O Rāghava; arise — may it go well with you; grief is no fitting companion."

**2.51.27**  "O lord, Kaikeyī, through fear of whom you do not question the charioteer about Rāma — she is not here; speak freely with confidence."

**2.51.28**  Having spoken thus to the great king, Kausalyā, overcome with grief, swiftly fell to the earth, her speech dissolved in tears.

**2.51.29**  Seeing Kausalyā lamenting thus and fallen to the ground, and looking at the king, all those women wept aloud.

**2.51.30**  Then, hearing that cry that rose from the inner apartments, the old and the young, men and all women, wept on every side; the city was once again thrown into tumult.

**2.52.1**  When the king had recovered and returned from his swoon, he summoned the charioteer again to learn of what had become of Rāma.

**2.52.2**  The charioteer was aged and utterly grief-stricken, sighing and brooding like an elephant newly captured, restless as a tormented bull-elephant.

**2.52.3**  The king, seeing the charioteer approach with his body covered in dust, his face full of tears, and looking wretched, spoke to him with the deepest anguish.

**2.52.4**  "Where will that righteous soul dwell, sheltered at the foot of a tree? That Rāghava who has always lived in the utmost comfort — what will he eat, O charioteer? How does this son of a king lie on the bare ground like one without a protector?"

**2.52.5**  "He whom infantry, chariots, and elephants followed as he went — how will that Rāma now dwell in the desolate forest, bereft of all company?"

**2.52.6**  "How have the two princes, together with Vaidehī, entered a forest roamed by wild beasts and deer and frequented by black serpents?"

**2.52.7**  "How did the two princes descend from the chariot on foot together with the delicate, suffering Sītā?"

**2.52.8**  "Fortunate indeed are you, O charioteer, for you have seen my two sons entering the forest's edge, like the twin Aśvins entering Mandara."

**2.52.9**  "What words did Rāma speak? What did Lakṣmaṇa say? And what did Maithilī say upon reaching the forest, O Sumantra? Tell me how Rāma sat, how he slept, how he ate."

**2.52.10**  Thus urged by the lord of men, the charioteer spoke to the king in a voice choked and suffused with tears.

**2.52.11**  "Rāghava, ever upholding righteousness, folded his hands and bowed his head and addressed me thus, O great king."

**2.52.12**  "'O charioteer, on my behalf bow your head at the feet of that great-souled father of mine who knows the true Self and is worthy of reverence, and salute those feet with your head.'"

**2.52.13**  "'Convey on my behalf greetings of health and respectful salutations without distinction to all the inner apartments, O charioteer.'"

**2.52.14**  "'And to my mother Kausalyā, convey my greeting of well-being and reverential salutation. O queen, honor the feet of the king as though they were the feet of a god.'"

**2.52.15**  "'Bharata is to be greeted with my words of well-being, and tell him: conduct yourself properly toward all the mothers in accordance with what is right.'"

**2.52.16**  "'That mighty-armed delight of the Ikṣvāku lineage is also to be told: holding the rank of crown prince, protect and serve the father who holds the sovereignty of the kingdom.'"

**2.52.17**  "Even as he spoke these words to me, O great king, that Rāma of great renown and lotus-red eyes shed abundant tears."

**2.52.18**  "But Lakṣmaṇa, greatly enraged, sighing deeply, spoke: 'For what offence has this prince been banished?'"

**2.52.19**  "'If Rāma has been exiled — whether because of greed, or on account of the granting of a boon — it is in every way a wicked deed. Yet in the abandonment of Rāma I can perceive no reason whatsoever.'"

**2.52.20**  "'This act, undertaken impulsively without proper deliberation, contrary to righteousness and born of shallow judgment, will generate universal lamentation — the exile of Rāghava.'"

**2.52.21**  "'As for me, I no longer recognize the great king in the role of father. Rāghava alone is my brother, my master, my kinsman, and my father.'"

**2.52.22**  "'Having abandoned one who is dear to all the world and devoted to the good of all the world — how can all the world rejoice in you through this deed?'"

**2.52.23**  "And Jānakī, O great king — the suffering one, sighing deeply — stood there like one whose mind has been seized by a spirit, like a cow whose calf has died."

**2.52.24**  "That illustrious princess, who had never before experienced such calamity, wept from that grief and said not a single word to me."

**2.52.25**  "With her face grown dry and gazing upward at her husband, she suddenly released a flood of tears upon seeing me depart."

**2.52.26**  "Just so, Rāma stood with tears on his face and hands folded in salutation, supported by the arm of Lakṣmaṇa. Just so, the suffering Sītā, weeping, watched the royal chariot — and so she watched me."

**2.53.1**  "When I turned back, my horses would not move forward on the road, shedding warm tears as Rāma set out for the forest."

**2.53.2**  "After saluting both princes with folded hands, I mounted the chariot and departed, bearing that grief within me."

**2.53.3**  "Together with Guha I remained there many days, hoping that Rāma might call me back again."

**2.53.4**  "In your domain, O great king, worn by the affliction of Rāma's exile, even the trees have withered, along with their flowers, sprouts, and buds."

**2.53.5**  "Creatures no longer creep about, nor do beasts of prey roam forth. Overwhelmed by grief for Rāma, that forest has fallen silent."

**2.53.6**  "O lord of men, the lotus ponds have their leaves and petals drooping, their waters turbid, their lotuses scorched; the fish and water-birds in the lotus pools have retreated and hidden."

**2.53.7**  "Water-born flowers and garlands, and those that grow on land, no longer shine as before — they are faint of fragrance — nor do the fruits appear as they once did."

**2.53.8**  "As I re-entered Ayodhyā no one welcomed me. The people, not seeing Rāma, sigh again and again."

**2.53.9**  "From their mansions, terraces, and palaces, the women — haggard from not seeing Rāma — observed the returning chariot and raised a cry of 'Alas! Alas!'"

**2.53.10**  "With long, clear eyes flooded with a torrent of tears, the women glanced at one another, clearly more distressed than ever."

**2.53.11**  "Among enemies, friends, and those who are indifferent — in their anguish I perceive no distinction whatsoever."

**2.53.12**  "Its people without joy, its horses and elephants wretched and troubled, its voice pained and faded, resounding with sighs —"

**2.53.13**  "— joyless, O great king, made incomparable in grief by the banishment of Rāma, Ayodhyā appears to me like Kausalyā bereft of her son."

**2.53.14**  Having heard the charioteer's words, the king, in a voice utterly desolate and overcome with tears, spoke these words to that charioteer.

**2.53.15**  "Instigated by Kaikeyī of wicked character, I did not deliberate this with the skilled elder counselors."

**2.53.16**  "Without consulting friends, ministers, or citizens — out of delusion, on account of a woman, I acted in haste in this matter."

**2.53.17**  "This great calamity has surely come by the force of fate, or by chance, for the destruction of this dynasty, O charioteer."

**2.53.18**  "O charioteer, if any merit has been accumulated by me as well, bring me swiftly to Rāma — my very life-breaths are urging me."

**2.53.19**  "Whatever command of mine can recall Rāghava — let it be used. Without Rāma I cannot live even for a moment."

**2.53.20**  "Or if that mighty-armed one has gone too far away, then mount me on the chariot and take me swiftly to show me to Rāma."

**2.53.21**  "Where is he — that great bowman with well-formed teeth, the elder brother of Lakṣmaṇa? If I should live, let me see him together with Sītā."

**2.53.22**  "If I do not see Rāma — the red-eyed, mighty-armed one adorned with jewelled earrings — I shall journey to the realm of Yama."

**2.53.23**  "What can be more painful than this — that I, having fallen into this plight, do not see here the joy of the Ikṣvākus, Rāghava?"

**2.53.24**  "Alas, Rāma! Alas, younger brother of Rāma! Alas, O suffering Vaidehī! You do not know that I am dying of grief like one without a protector. O queen, this ocean of sorrow is impossible to cross alive."

**2.53.25**  "Inauspicious is my fate — I who seek Rāghava here today yet cannot find him together with Lakṣmaṇa!" — lamenting thus, that king of great renown fell suddenly on his bed, overcome by unconsciousness.

**2.53.26**  As the king lamented and fell senseless, and hearing his words — more pitiable still, and redoubled with grief for Rāma — Rāma's mother, the queen, was seized once again by fear.

**2.54.1**  Then Kausalyā, trembling again and again as though possessed by a spirit, like one whose life had ebbed away to the ground, spoke to the charioteer.

**2.54.2**  "Take me to where Kākutstha is, where Sītā is, where Lakṣmaṇa is. Without them I have no courage to live here even for a moment."

**2.54.3**  "Turn the chariot around quickly and take me too to the Daṇḍaka forest. If I do not follow them, I shall go to the abode of Yama."

**2.54.4**  Struck by a flood of tears and his voice faltering, the charioteer, with hands folded in salutation, spoke these words to console the queen.

**2.54.5**  "Abandon grief, delusion, and the distress born of anguish. Shaking off affliction, Rāghava shall dwell well in the forest."

**2.54.6**  "And Lakṣmaṇa too, who knows righteousness and has conquered his senses, serves Rāma's feet in the forest and thereby wins merit for the world beyond."

**2.54.7**  "Even in the lonely forest, Sītā, having found her dwelling, is at ease as in a house — fearless, with her mind surrendered to Rāma."

**2.54.8**  "I perceive not the slightest sign of dejection in her, even the most subtle. Vaidehī appears to me as one accustomed to travel abroad."

**2.54.9**  "Just as she used to delight in going to the city's pleasure groves of old, so too does Sītā delight even in the solitary forests."

**2.54.10**  "Like a child she takes delight, this Sītā of moon-like face — truly, even dwelling in a solitary forest, her spirit is undaunted when she is with Rāma."

**2.54.11**  "For her heart is fixed on him alone and her life depends on him alone — even Ayodhyā would be as the forest to her if it were empty of Rāma."

**2.54.12**  "Along the road, Vaidehī asks about the villages and towns, and upon seeing the courses of rivers and the various trees, she questions about them too."

**2.54.13**  "Neither the road, nor the wind's force, nor commotion, nor the heat of the sun dims the radiance of Vaidehī — a radiance resembling the rays of the moon."

**2.54.14**  "The face of that Vaidehī, speaking so sweetly, resembling the lotus in form and the full moon in its light, does not waver or lose its lustre."

**2.54.15**  "Her two feet, even now as before, are as brilliant as lotus buds — red-hued as the dye of lac-resin even without application of lac."

**2.54.16**  "Even now, that spirited Vaidehī walks with a playful gait as if to the sound of her anklets, still radiant with that natural glow though her ornaments have been set aside."

**2.54.17**  "Sheltered in the forest, even upon seeing an elephant, or a lion, or a tiger, she takes no fright — for she takes refuge in the arms of Rāma."

**2.54.18**  "They are not to be mourned, nor are you to mourn for yourself, nor even for the lord of the people — for this conduct will be established in the world as eternally glorious."
