**2.54.19**  "Shaking off sorrow, with joyful hearts, well-settled on the path trodden by the great sages, delighting in the forest, subsisting on forest fruits — they are upholding your father's noble promise."

**2.54.20**  Yet even so, though restrained by the charioteer speaking such well-reasoned words, the queen, worn thin by grief for her son, did not cease her cries of "My dear!" and "My son!" and "O Rāghava!"

**2.55.1**  When Rāma, the foremost of the righteous and the finest of those who bring delight, had gone to the forest, Kausalyā, weeping and overcome with grief, spoke thus to her husband.

**2.55.2**  'Even in worlds other than this, your glory is proclaimed — that Rāghava is compassionate, generous, and speaks kindly.

**2.55.3**  How, O best of noble men, will those two sons, raised in comfort, bear hardship in the forest together with Sītā, now wretched?

**2.55.4**  She, the Maithilī, is young, dark-complexioned, delicate, and accustomed only to comfort — how will she endure heat and cold?

**2.55.5**  Having eaten fine food accompanied by savory condiments, how will wide-eyed Sītā subsist on forest fare — uncooked roots and fruits?

**2.55.6**  She who has heard only the auspicious sounds of song and music — how will that blameless one bear hearing the dreadful roars of flesh-eating lions?

**2.55.7**  Where now does he sleep — he of great arms, resembling Mahendra's banner, resting his arm that is like an iron bar, he of mighty strength?

**2.55.8**  When shall I again behold Rāma's face — lotus-hued, with beautiful hair at its end, fragrant as a lotus-breath, the finest of faces, with lotus eyes?

**2.55.9**  My heart is surely made of the essence of adamant — there is no doubt — since, not seeing him, it does not shatter into a thousand pieces.

**2.55.10**  If after fifteen years Rāghava returns, will he renounce the kingdom and the treasury that Bharata has been enjoying?

**2.55.11**  O lord of the people, a kingdom once enjoyed by a younger brother — why would an elder brother, who is the most worthy, not disdain it?

**2.55.12**  A tiger does not wish to eat prey that has been seized by another; in the same way, this tiger among men will not accept what has already been licked by another.

**2.55.13**  Oblations, clarified butter, sacrificial cakes, kuśa grass, and posts of khadira wood — once used, these are not employed again in a sacrifice.

**2.55.14**  Likewise, this kingdom — once appropriated — Rāma will not consecrate, just as one does not reconsecrate liquor stripped of its essence, or a sacrifice from which the Soma is lost.

**2.55.15**  Rāghava will not tolerate such an insult, just as a powerful tiger will not endure the touch of a child's hand.

**2.55.16**  He of such lion-like strength, bull-eyed, that bull among men — he has been slain by his own father, as a lotus-born son might be slain by a river.

**2.55.17**  The eternal duty taught by scripture and practiced by the twice-born — if you are devoted to dharma, was it thus when you banished your son?

**2.55.18**  The husband is the foremost refuge of a woman; the son is her second; kinsmen are the third — O king, there is no fourth in this world.

**2.55.19**  Of these, you are lost to me, and Rāma has taken to the forest. I have no wish to go to the forest either — for you have utterly destroyed me in every way.

**2.55.20**  By you this kingdom with its realm has been ruined, so too your very self along with your ministers; I am destroyed with my son, and the citizens are destroyed — while your son and wife rejoice.'

**2.55.21**  Hearing these words filled with such harsh sounds, the king fainted in his grief. Then that lord of the earth plunged deeper into sorrow, and once again he recalled his own past sin.

**2.56.1**  Thus made to hear harsh words by Rāma's mother, who was both angry and grief-stricken, the king fell into sorrowful reflection.

**2.56.2**  As he reflected, there rose before him the evil deed he had committed long ago in ignorance — when he had shot by sound alone.

**2.56.3**  His mind undone by that grief and by grief for Rāma, burning with both sorrows, that master, the lord of the earth, spoke to Kausalyā.

**2.56.4**  'I beg your grace, Kausalyā — I fold my hands before you. You are always gentle and without cruelty, even toward strangers.

**2.56.5**  Surely the husband — whether possessed of virtues or devoid of them — is, for women who reflect on dharma, the very visible deity, O queen.

**2.56.6**  You who are ever devoted to dharma, who have seen the ways of this world and the next — even in your great grief you ought not speak harshly to one who is himself deeply afflicted.'

**2.56.7**  Hearing those piteous, compassionate words of the wretched king, Kausalyā shed tears like a watercourse releasing fresh water.

**2.56.8**  Weeping, she joined her palms like a lotus and held them to her head before the king; then, frightened and agitated, she spoke words in quick succession.

**2.56.9**  'Be gracious — I beg you with my head bowed to the ground. I am wretched in my asking, O lord; I am not one who should be destroyed by you.

**2.56.10**  She is not truly a woman worthy of praise by a wise man in either world, O hero, who must be won over by her husband with supplication.

**2.56.11**  I know dharma, and O knower of dharma, I know you to be a speaker of truth. Whatever I said was said by me while overcome with grief for my son.'

**2.56.12**  Grief destroys courage; grief destroys learning; grief destroys everything. There is no enemy equal to grief.

**2.56.13**  A blow falling from an enemy's hand can be endured; but grief, even the most subtle, when it falls cannot be borne.

**2.56.14**  Today marks five nights of Rāma's forest exile — yet for me, whose joy is shattered by grief, it seems the equal of five years.

**2.56.15**  As I think of him, this grief swells in my heart with the force with which the vast waters of the ocean rise unchecked.

**2.56.16**  While Kausalyā was thus speaking these worthy words, the sun's rays grew dim and night drew near.

**2.56.17**  Then the king, somewhat comforted by the words of Queen Kausalyā yet overcome by sorrow, fell under the sway of sleep.

**2.57.1**  Awakening repeatedly, his mind shattered by grief, King Daśaratha then fell into sorrowful thought.

**2.57.2**  By reason of the banishment of Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa, a calamity seized that man equal to Vāsava, as darkness seizes the sun at the time of a demon's eclipse.

**2.57.3**  That king Daśaratha, on the sixth night after Rāma had gone forth to the forest, at midnight, recalling the evil deed he had done, spoke these words to Kausalyā, who was afflicted by grief for her son.

**2.57.4**  'O auspicious one, whatever a person does — whether good or evil — that very fruit born of the act he himself receives, O blessed one.

**2.57.5**  One who, at the commencement of actions, does not know the weight or lightness of purposes, nor the fruit, nor the fault — such a one is called a child.

**2.57.6**  Someone cuts down a mango grove and instead waters palāśa trees; greedy at the sight of blossoms, he repents when the fruit-time comes.

**2.57.7**  That am I — I cut down the mango grove and watered the palāśa trees; having abandoned Rāma when the fruit-time came, I now repent in my foolishness.

**2.57.8**  O Kausalyā, when I was a young man just gaining fame as an archer, this sin was committed by me — with a bow I shot at a sound I could not see; and now, O queen, this self-made sorrow has come upon me.

**2.57.9**  As a child in confusion might swallow poison, so too by me unknowingly the fruit of shooting by sound alone has been consumed.

**2.57.10**  O queen, you were as yet unwed; I had just become crown prince; then the rains arrived, that season which swells passion and desire.

**2.57.11**  Having drawn up the earthly waters and scorched the world with his rays, the sun now entered the dreadful quarter where the dead journey.

**2.57.12**  The heat immediately subsided; moist clouds appeared; and thereupon all frogs, spotted deer, and peacocks rejoiced.

**2.57.13**  Covered repeatedly by water falling and still falling, the spotted deer blazed with intoxication like a mountain covered with a mass of water.

**2.57.14**  In that most pleasant season, armed with bow and arrows, mounted in my chariot, resolved on exercise, I followed the Sarayū river.

**2.57.15**  Wishing to slay a buffalo, an elephant, or some other wild beast that might come to the watering-place at night, with my senses still untamed.

**2.57.16**  Then in the darkness I heard the sound of a pot being filled in the water, like the trumpeting of an elephant beyond the range of sight.

**2.57.17**  Then I drew a blazing arrow, like a venomous serpent, and released that sharp shaft.

**2.57.18**  Then a clear voice arose from the forest-dweller — 'Alas!' as he fell into the water; it was a human voice there: 'How could a weapon fall upon a suffering ascetic such as I?'

**2.57.19**  'I came alone at night to this solitary river to draw water. By whose arrow am I struck? Whose enemy am I? What have I done?'

**2.57.20**  'How can the killing by a weapon be ordained for one such as I — an ascetic who has laid aside the staff, who lives in the forest on forest fare?'

**2.57.21**  'One who bears the weight of matted locks, clad in bark and deer-skin — who could wish to slay me? What wrong have I done to anyone?'

**2.57.22**  'Such a deed, undertaken in vain, bringing only harm — no one would approve it, even as no one approves one who violates his teacher's bed.'

**2.57.23**  'I do not grieve so much for my own loss of life; it is for both my mother and father that I grieve — for those like me.'

**2.57.24**  'That aged pair, long sustained by me — when I have gone to the five elements, how will they live?'

**2.57.25**  'Both aged parents, and I slain by a single arrow — we are all destroyed by some utter child, one of unformed self.'

**2.57.26**  Hearing those piteous words of that lover of dharma, my bow with its arrow fell from my stricken hands to the ground.

**2.57.27**  Coming to that spot, wretched in spirit and deeply sorrowful, I saw the ascetic lying wounded by my arrow on the bank of the Sarayū.

**2.57.28**  He looked up at me with both eyes — I was terrified, my mind in disorder — and spoke harsh words, as though burning me with his energy.

**2.57.29**  'What wrong have I done you, O king? I who dwell in the forest, who came here seeking water for my teacher's sake — why have you struck me?'

**2.57.30**  'By a single arrow that has struck my vital spot, two blind old ones are slain — my mother and my father.'

**2.57.31**  'Those two, surely weak and blind, waiting for me, thirsting — they will long endure that terrible, hope-begotten thirst.'

**2.57.32**  'Surely there is no fruit of asceticism or of sacred learning, since my father does not know that I lie fallen on the ground.'

**2.57.33**  'And even if he knew, what could he do — he who is without power, unable to move, like one tree unable to save another that is being felled?'

**2.57.34**  'Go quickly yourself to my father and tell him, O Rāghava; in his anger he will not burn you up like a blazing fire consuming a forest.'

**2.57.35**  'This single path, O king, leads to where my father's hermitage is. Go there and propitiate him; even though angry, he will not curse you.'

**2.57.36**  'Remove the arrow from me, O king; the sharp shaft blocks my vital spot, causing swelling and pain, like a flood-current stopping up a soft riverbank.'

**2.57.37**  'I am not a twice-born, O king — let there be no anguish in your mind. I was born of a Vaiśya father upon a Śūdrā woman, O lord of the land.'

**2.57.38**  As he was speaking thus with difficulty — his vital spot struck by the arrow, his body bending — I drew out that arrow.

**2.57.39**  His body wet with water, lamenting with difficulty, his vital wound unceasing, still breathing — then, O blessed one, seeing him lying on the bank of the Sarayū, I was overcome with the deepest anguish.

**2.58.1**  Having committed that great sin through ignorance, with senses distraught, I pondered alone in my mind: how could any good come of this?

**2.58.2**  Then, taking up that pot filled with the finest water, I reached the hermitage by the path that had been described to me.

**2.58.3**  There I saw his parents — blind, frail, aged, bereft of a guide, like two birds with clipped wings.

**2.58.4**  Seated, unable to move about, conversing on matters concerning him, they were deprived of that hope I should have brought, sitting helpless like the unprotected.

**2.58.5**  Upon hearing the sound of my footsteps, the sage spoke these words: "Why do you delay, my son? Bring the water quickly."

**2.58.6**  "On account of what, dear child, were you sporting in the water so long? Your mother here has been anxious — enter the hermitage quickly."

**2.58.7**  "Whatever offence your mother or I may have given you, dear son, an ascetic such as you ought not to take it to heart."

**2.58.8**  "You are the refuge of the refugeless, the eyes of those without sight — our very lives cling to you. Why do you not speak even a word to us?"

**2.58.9**  Frightened, like one truly stricken with fear, I looked upon that sage and spoke to him in a voice that faltered, unclear, and almost without sound.

**2.58.10**  Steadying the strength of speech with effort of mind and body, I disclosed to him the dread calamity born of the destruction of his son.

**2.58.11**  "I am Daśaratha the kṣatriya — I am not the son of the great soul. This grief, scorned by good men, has come upon me through my own deed."

**2.58.12**  "Reverend one, I came to the bank of the Sarayū, bow in hand, seeking to kill some beast of prey or an elephant that had come to the watering-place."

**2.58.13**  "There I heard a sound of a pot being filled with water; taking it for an elephant, I struck with an arrow."

**2.58.14**  "Going then to the river's bank, I saw an ascetic lying on the ground, his heart pierced by the arrow, life departed."

**2.58.15**  "Venerable one, having detected that sound while I was seeking to slay an elephant, I released an iron-tipped arrow into the water — by that your son has been slain."

**2.58.16**  "He, with the arrow drawn out, attained heaven right there, lamenting and weeping over you both, the blind ones."

**2.58.17**  "Your son was struck by me suddenly, through ignorance. Whatever must follow given what has occurred — may the sage be gracious to me."

**2.58.18**  Upon hearing those cruel words, heavily sighing, emaciated by grief, that great and radiant sage spoke to me as I stood before him with folded hands.

**2.58.19**  "Had you not yourself confessed this inauspicious deed to me, O king, your head would have instantly shattered into a hundred thousand fragments."

**2.58.20**  "A killing committed by a kṣatriya knowingly and deliberately, especially of a forest-dweller, would unseat even the wielder of the thunderbolt from his station."

**2.58.21**  "Because this was done through ignorance, you live by that very reason alone. Otherwise the entire Rāghava lineage might perish this very day — how much more you yourself."

**2.58.22**  "Lead us to that place, O king" — thus he also addressed me: "We wish today to see our son for a last sight."

**2.58.23**  His limbs smeared with blood, his bark garment scattered, lying on the ground without consciousness, passed into the power of the king of righteousness.

**2.58.24**  Then I alone, leading those two who were deeply stricken with grief to that place, caused that sage — together with his wife — to touch their son.

**2.58.25**  Having touched and approached their son, those two ascetics fell upon his body, and his father spoke these words over him:

**2.58.26**  "Am I then not dear to you, my son? Look upon your mother, O righteous one. Why do you not embrace me, dear tender child? Speak a word."

**2.58.27**  "From whom in the latter part of the night shall I hear the sweet, heart-stirring sound of recitation of scripture, or any other discourse especially dear to me?"

**2.58.28**  "Who, having worshipped at twilight, bathed, and offered into the sacred fire, will praise and sit attending upon me, tormented as I am by grief and fear for a son?"

**2.58.29**  "Who will bring tubers, roots, and fruit and feed me as a beloved guest — helpless, unprotected, without a guide?"

**2.58.30**  "How, my son, shall I maintain this wretched mother of yours — blind, aged, an ascetic woman, pitifully longing for a son?"

**2.58.31**  "Stay, do not go, my son, toward the abode of Yama. Tomorrow you shall go together with me, accompanied by your mother as well."

**2.58.32**  "Both of us too, stricken with grief, helpless and wretched in the forest, bereft of you, shall very quickly go to the realm of Yama."

**2.58.33**  "Then, upon seeing Vaivasvata, I shall speak this plea: 'May the king of righteousness pardon me; let him sustain these parents of mine.'"

**2.58.34**  "Just as you, my son, though slain by the deed of a sinner, are yourself without sin — by that truth, go swiftly to the worlds of those who fight with weapons."

**2.58.35**  "The supreme goal attained by heroes who do not retreat in battles, slain while facing the enemy — to that supreme goal, my son, do you proceed."

**2.58.36**  "The goal attained by Sagara, Śaibyā, Dilīpa, Janamejaya, Nahuṣa, and Dhundhumāra — to that goal go, dear child."

**2.58.37**  "The goal of all virtuous ones, and the goal of one who has fallen from his own Vedic study, of a giver of land, of one who has kindled sacred fires, of one who has kept the vow of a single wife —"

**2.58.38**  "— the goal of givers of a thousand cows, the goal of those who have served their teachers, and the goal of those who have surrendered their bodies — to that goal go, dear child. For no one born in this lineage goes to an ill-fated destination."

**2.58.39**  Thus he lamented miserably there over and over again; then he proceeded, together with his wife, to perform the water-offering for him.

**2.58.40**  But the sage's son, by virtue of his own deeds, having taken on a divine form, consoled his parents for a moment and then spoke these words:

**2.58.41**  "Through service to you both I have attained a great station. You two shall also swiftly come to me at my abode."

**2.58.42**  Having spoken thus, the sage's son — his senses mastered — quickly ascended to heaven in a radiant divine chariot.

**2.58.43**  That ascetic of great radiance, having quickly performed the water-offering together with his wife, spoke to me as I stood before him with folded hands.

**2.58.44**  "Slay me today, O king — I feel no anguish at death. For by your arrow you have made me, who had but one son, sonless."

**2.58.45**  "But because my pure son was slain by you through ignorance, by that I shall curse you with an exceedingly terrible and dreadful sorrow."

**2.58.46**  "Just as this grief born of the loss of a son is now mine, so too, O king, shall you meet your end through grief for a son."

**2.58.47**  Therefore, O auspicious one, that is the noble utterance which has come upon me — that today I am abandoning my life through grief for a son.

**2.58.48**  "If Rāma were to touch me even once today, or embrace me even once, it would not equal what I have done to the Rāghava, O queen."

**2.58.49**  "I cannot see you with my eyes; my memory is failing. These messengers of Vaivasvata, O Kausalyā, are pressing me to hasten."

**2.58.50**  "What grief could be greater than this — that at the extinguishing of my life I cannot see Rāma, the knower of righteousness, the refuge of truth?"

**2.58.51**  "Those who will behold Rāma's face adorned with beautiful auspicious earrings again in the fifteenth year — they are not mere mortals but gods."

**2.58.52**  "Blessed are they who will behold Rāma's face — lotus-petal-eyed, fair-browed, with beautiful teeth and lovely nose — resembling the lord of stars."

**2.58.53**  "Blessed are they who shall see the face of my lord — like the autumn moon, like a bloomed lotus, fragrant."

**2.58.54**  "Happy are they who shall see Rāma returned from forest-dwelling, come back to Ayodhyā — like the planet Śukra traversing its path."

**2.58.55**  "This grief born of my own self assails me — helpless, unconscious — with force, as a river's current assails its bank."

**2.58.56**  "Ah, Rāghava of mighty arms! Ah, destroyer of my afflictions!" — grieving thus, King Daśaratha reached the end of his life.

**2.58.57**  Thus narrating in misery, the lord of men — anguished by the banishment of his beloved son, tormented by overwhelming grief — when midnight had passed, that noble-visaged king gave up his life.

**2.59.1**  Then, when the night had passed and the next day's morning came, the bards presented themselves around the royal dwelling.

**2.59.2**  Then those skilled in courtly attendance, pure in conduct — a group consisting mostly of women of the finest quality — presented themselves as was customary.

**2.59.3**  Those versed in the lore of bathing brought water mixed with yellow sandal in golden pots at the proper time and in the proper manner.

**2.59.4**  Other women — many of them young maidens — also brought the auspicious implements to be touched and the articles to be tasted.

**2.59.5**  Then those women who attended most closely upon the bedchamber of the lord of Kośala came together and tried to awaken their husband.

**2.59.6**  Seized with trembling and anxious for the life of the king, they trembled like the tips of grass in a current against the stream.

**2.59.7**  Then, seeing the trembling women and the king, that which had been dreaded as evil became a certainty.

**2.59.8**  Then those noble women cried out aloud in distress, like female elephants in the forest whose herd-leader has strayed from his place.

**2.59.9**  Kausalyā and Sumitrā, their consciousness suddenly roused by the sound of their wailing, abandoned sleep.

**2.59.10**  Kausalyā and Sumitrā, seeing and touching the king, cried out "Ah, my lord!" and fell upon the ground.

**2.59.11**  She, the daughter of the lord of Kośala, writhing upon the earth, covered with dust, shone not — like a star fallen from the sky.

**2.59.12**  That scene — with people in panic and confusion, thronged with anxious crowds, tumultuous lamentation on all sides, and kinsmen stricken with anguish —

**2.59.13**  — with joy instantly extinguished, with a sight of misery and distress, became the royal dwelling of the king who had gone to his fated end.

**2.59.14**  Knowing that the most illustrious bull among kings had departed, his wives surrounded him and — weeping bitterly, overcome with deep sorrow, raising their arms — lamented pitifully, like the unprotected.

**2.60.1**  Beholding the king who had gone to heaven — like a fire extinguished, like an ocean drained of water, like the sun robbed of its radiance —

**2.60.2**  — Kausalyā, her eyes full of tears, emaciated by grief in many ways, clasping the head of the king, addressed Kaikeyī:

**2.60.3**  "Your wish is fulfilled, Kaikeyī — enjoy the kingdom free of thorns. Having abandoned the king, O cruel woman, O woman of wicked conduct, be single-minded now."

**2.60.4**  "Rāma has gone, abandoning me; my husband has gone to heaven. Like a traveller stranded on a wrong road without companions, I have no wish to live."

**2.60.5**  "Abandoning that husband as her deity — what woman would wish to live otherwise? Only Kaikeyī, she who has abandoned righteousness."

**2.60.6**  "The greedy one perceives no fault — as if consuming something cooked; for the sake of a hunchback, Kaikeyī has destroyed the lineage of the Rāghavas."

**2.60.7**  "Rāma together with his wife was exiled by the king who was engaged in what was not his proper duty. Janaka, upon hearing this, will grieve as deeply as I do."

**2.60.8**  "Rāma of lotus-petal eyes has gone from here to the destruction of his comfortable life. And Sītā — that afflicted daughter of Videha's king, unaccustomed to sorrow — will tremble with anguish in the forest."

**2.60.9**  "Hearing in the nights the fearsome roars of beasts and birds, surely terrified, she will cling to Rāghava."

**2.60.10**  "Old and with few children, not thinking of Vaidehī, he too — seized by grief — will surely abandon his life."

**2.60.11**  Then, embracing that afflicted woman as she lamented, the attendants led away Kausalyā who was overwhelmed with deep grief.

**2.60.12**  Then the ministers, having laid the lord of the earth in a vat of oil, were instructed and immediately performed all the rites for the king.

**2.60.13**  However, the all-knowing ministers did not dare to perform the king's cremation without his son; therefore they preserved the king's body.

**2.60.14**  Knowing that that lord of men had been laid by the ministers in the vat of oil — "Ah, he is dead!" — those women lamented.

**2.60.15**  Raising their arms in misery, with faces streaming from their eyes, weeping, scorched by grief, they lamented pitifully.

**2.60.16**  Like a night bereft of stars, like a woman bereft of her husband, the city of Ayodhyā did not shine, deprived of the great-souled king.

**2.60.17**  With people overwhelmed with tears, with the women of noble families crying "Alas! Alas!", with empty crossroads and house-interiors, it shone not as before.

**2.60.18**  Like a sky that has lost its radiance without the sun, like a night from which the company of stars has departed, the city bereft of the great-souled king had no splendour — nor did its roads and crossroads throng with voices choked with tears.

**2.60.19**  Men and women, coming together in groups and reviling the mother of Bharata, were stricken with anguish at the destruction of their king, and found no comfort in that city.

**2.61.1**  When that night had passed and the sun had risen, the royal ministers and the twice-born assembled together and proceeded to the council hall.

**2.61.2**  Mārkaṇḍeya, Maudgalya, Vāmadeva, Kāśyapa, Kātyāyana, Gautama, and the greatly renowned Jābāli —

**2.61.3**  these twice-born ones, together with the ministers, each raised their voice and turned to Vasiṣṭha, the foremost royal preceptor.

**2.61.4**  "That night has passed in grief — a night that seemed to us like a hundred years — now that this king has passed away into the five elements amid sorrow for his son."

**2.61.5**  "The great king has gone to heaven; Rāma has taken refuge in the forest; and the radiant Lakṣmaṇa has gone along with Rāma himself."

**2.61.6**  "Both Bharata and Śatrughna, scorchers of foes, are in the Kekaya country, in the delightful city of Rājagṛha, in the residence of their maternal grandfather."

**2.61.7**  "Let a king of the Ikṣvāku line be appointed here today. For a kingless realm of ours must not come to ruin."

**2.61.8**  In a kingless realm, the cloud-bearer garlanded with lightning and resounding with thunder does not shower the earth with divine waters.

**2.61.9**  In a kingless realm, no one sows a handful of seed; in a kingless realm, neither son nor wife remains under the father's authority.

**2.61.10**  Where there is no king, there is no wealth; where there is no king, there is no wife either. This and other extreme calamities arise — whence can truth exist where there is no king?

**2.61.11**  In a kingless realm, men do not build assembly halls, nor pleasant gardens, nor auspicious houses with joy.

**2.61.12**  In a kingless realm, the sacrifice-devoted twice-born do not attend soma-sessions; self-controlled brāhmaṇas with sharpened vows do not assemble.

**2.61.13**  In a kingless realm, abundant actors and dancers do not flourish, nor do the festivals and fairs that cause the realm to prosper.

**2.61.14**  In a kingless realm, those accomplished in affairs are not delighted by tales told by storytellers dear to the art of narration.

**2.61.15**  In a kingless realm, men do not venture forth into the forests with swift conveyances, accompanied by women, in the spirit of pleasure.

**2.61.16**  In a kingless realm, the wealthy do not sleep well-protected with open doors, nor do those who live by agriculture and cattle-herding.

**2.61.17**  In a kingless realm, merchants who travel far do not journey safely along the roads laden with abundant goods.

**2.61.18**  In a kingless realm, the self-restrained sage who meditates upon himself through himself does not wander alone wherever he makes his evening abode.

**2.61.19**  In a kingless realm, welfare and security do not prevail; and in a kingless realm the army cannot withstand enemies in battle.

**2.61.20**  As rivers without water, as a forest without grass, as cows without a cowherd — so is a realm without a king.

**2.61.21**  In a kingless realm, nothing belongs to anyone; men devour one another constantly like fish.

**2.61.22**  Even those who have broken all bounds, atheists devoid of doubt — even they are brought to proper conduct when constrained by the king's rod of punishment.

**2.61.23**  Alas, this world would be like darkness — nothing whatsoever would be discerned — if there were no king to distinguish the virtuous from the unvirtuous.

**2.61.24**  "Even when the great king was alive, we all did not transgress your word, just as the ocean does not transgress its shore."

**2.61.25**  "Considering thus the conduct befitting the best of twice-born — the realm without a king become like a wilderness — do thou thyself consecrate here as king the generous prince, the son of the Ikṣvāku line."

**2.62.1**  Having heard their words, Vasiṣṭha replied to all of them — the assembled allies, ministers, and those brāhmaṇas — with these words:

**2.62.2**  "Since Bharata, accompanied by his brother Śatrughna, dwells happily in the city of Rājagṛha, the home of his maternal kinsmen —"

**2.62.3**  "— let swift messengers depart at once with fleet horses to bring back those two heroic brothers. Why should we hesitate?"

**2.62.4**  Then all of them said to Vasiṣṭha, "Let them go." Having heard their words, Vasiṣṭha spoke again:

**2.62.5**  "Come, Siddhārtha, Vijaya, Jayanta, Aśoka, Nandana — hear what is to be done. I speak to all of you."

**2.62.6**  "Go quickly to the city of Rājagṛha with swift-galloping horses; by my command, let Bharata be told this, setting aside all grief:"

**2.62.7**  "'The preceptor greets you well, as do all the ministers; depart in haste — there is an urgent matter requiring your action.'"

**2.62.8**  "But do not tell him that Rāma has gone away, nor that his father has died; you must not announce to him this destruction of the Rāghava line."

**2.62.9**  "Take quickly silk garments and choice ornaments for the king and for Bharata, and depart." Permitted by Vasiṣṭha, the messengers went forth in great haste.

**2.62.10**  They crossed the Gaṅgā at the city of Hastina and turned westward, reaching the Pāñcāla country and passing through the middle of the Kurujāṅgala.

**2.62.11**  With speed they passed the Śaradaṇḍā — a divine river of clear waters, frequented by many birds, and thronging with people.

**2.62.12**  Reaching the Nikūla tree — that divine place of sacred vows — they approached and saluted it, then entered the city of Kuliṅgā.

**2.62.13**  Then reaching the proper time, worn down by the exertion, they passed through the Bāhlīka country and the mountain Sudāmā, gazing upon Viṣṇu's footprint, and also the Vipāśā river and the Śālmalī tree.

**2.62.14**  Those messengers, with their mounts weary, traveling along a long and good road, swiftly and straightway reached Girivraja, that excellent city.

**2.62.15**  For the sake of pleasing their lord, for the protection of the lineage, and for the sustaining of their master's dynasty — without any hesitation, those messengers hastened and arrived at that very city in the night.

**2.63.1**  On the very same night that those messengers entered the city, Bharata too saw this ill-omened dream.

**2.63.2**  When that night was over and he had seen that inauspicious dream, the son of the king of kings was deeply tormented.

**2.63.3**  Perceiving him to be distressed, his friends, who spoke pleasant words, began telling tales in the assembly-hall, intending thereby to drive away his suffering.

**2.63.4**  Some played musical instruments to soothe him; others danced; yet others performed plays and various comic entertainments.

**2.63.5**  Yet that great-souled Bharata, that Rāghava, did not rejoice with those friends who spoke kindly to him and who made merry with tales and laughter.

**2.63.6**  His dear friend, attended by companions and surrounded by well-wishers, said to Bharata: "Why, friend, do you not take delight?"

**2.63.7**  To his friend speaking thus, Bharata replied: "Hear what is the cause of this despondency that has come upon me."

**2.63.8**  "In a dream I saw my father, begrimed and with hair unbound, falling from a mountain peak into a turbid lake of cow-dung."

**2.63.9**  "And I saw him wallowing in that lake of cow-dung, drinking oil with cupped hands, laughing as it were again and again."

**2.63.10**  "Then, having eaten sesame-rice, again and again with his head downward, his whole body anointed with oil, he plunged into the oil itself."

**2.63.11**  "In the dream I also saw the ocean dried up, the moon fallen suddenly to the earth, and the sacrificial fire extinguished all at once."

**2.63.12**  "I see the earth rent asunder, various trees withered and shattered, and the mountains smoking."

**2.63.13**  "I saw the king seated on an iron throne, dressed in black garments, while dark-tawny women laughed and mocked at him."

**2.63.14**  "And that righteous-souled one, adorned with red garlands and red unguents, was hastening away on a chariot yoked with donkeys, heading southward."

**2.63.15**  "Thus have I seen this terror-inducing vision this night. Either Rāma, or the king, or Lakṣmaṇa will die."

**2.63.16**  "For the man who in a dream travels in a conveyance yoked with donkeys — before long the smoke of his funeral pyre is seen rising. It is for this reason that I am wretched; therefore I cannot respond to your cheer."

**2.63.17**  "My throat seems to dry up; my mind is as though unsettled; I seem to shudder within myself — yet I see no cause for it."

**2.63.18**  "Having witnessed this course of evil dreams — so manifold, so unforeseen — that great fear does not leave my heart, as I reflect upon the king whose sight is beyond imagining."

**2.64.1**  While Bharata was recounting his dream, those messengers with their weary horses entered the fair city of Rājagṛha, whose moat none could easily cross.

**2.64.2**  Having been received and honoured by both the king and the prince, they clasped the king's feet and then addressed these words to Bharata.

**2.64.3**  "The royal priest sends you his well-wishes, as do all the ministers. Depart with all speed — there is urgent business requiring your attention."

**2.64.4**  "Here your maternal uncle the king offers twenty crores of gold, and ten crores fully complete besides, O son of a king."

**2.64.5**  Bharata, ever affectionate toward his friends, accepted all those gifts, honoured the messengers with further presents, and then spoke to them.

**2.64.6**  "Is my father King Daśaratha in good health? And is all well with great-souled Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa?"

**2.64.7**  "Is Kausalyā, the noble one devoted to righteousness, who knows and perceives dharma, mother of wise Rāma — is she free of illness?"

**2.64.8**  "Is Sumitrā, the dharma-knowing mother of Lakṣmaṇa and valiant Śatrughna — is that middle queen also free from illness?"

**2.64.9**  "And Kaikeyī — selfish, ever fierce, quick to anger, who thinks herself wise — is she too without illness? What has my mother been saying?"

**2.64.10**  When thus questioned by great-souled Bharata, the messengers gave this modest reply to him then: "Those whose welfare you seek are well, O tiger among men."

**2.64.11**  Bharata, having heard their reply, in turn addressed the messengers: "I shall take leave of the great king; the messengers are urging me to make haste."

**2.64.12**  Having said this to the messengers, and being urged onward by them, Bharata the son of a king spoke these words to his maternal grandfather.

**2.64.13**  "O king, I shall go to my father, summoned as I am by the messengers. I will come again whenever you think of me."

**2.64.14**  So addressed by Bharata, the king who was his maternal grandfather then smelled the Rāghava's head and spoke these auspicious words to him.

**2.64.15**  "Go, my dear child, I permit you. Kaikeyī is blessed with such a son as you. Give my kind regards to your mother and father, O scorcher of foes."

**2.64.16**  "And give my regards to the royal priest and to the other finest among the twice-born, and to those two great archers, your brothers Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa."

**2.64.17**  Honouring him fittingly, Kaikeya gave to Bharata fine elephant-loads of gifts — richly coloured woollen blankets and antelope skins.

**2.64.18**  Kekaya, honouring the son of Kaikeyī, bestowed wealth upon him: two thousand gold niṣkas and sixteen hundred horses.

**2.64.19**  Aśvapati likewise promptly gave Bharata trustworthy, virtuous ministers of the kind he desired as companions for the journey.

**2.64.20**  His maternal uncle gave him wealth in the form of fair-looking elephants of the Airāvata and Aindraśiras breeds, and swift, well-harnessed mules.

**2.64.21**  He also presented as a gift hounds from the inner palace — great-bodied, armed with fangs, possessing the strength and ferocity of tigers.

**2.64.22**  Taking leave of his maternal grandfather and his maternal uncle Yudhājit, Bharata mounted his chariot and departed, accompanied by Śatrughna.

**2.64.23**  Having yoked more than a hundred chariots with circular wheels, the servants followed Bharata as he travelled, with camels, cattle, horses and mules.

**2.64.24**  Great-souled Bharata, guarded by his army and accompanied by ministers as dear to him as kinsmen, took Śatrughna and departed from that house, like a perfected sage departing from the world of Indra.

**2.65.1**  That valiant delight of the Ikṣvāku line, facing eastward, set forth from Rājagṛha and crossed the Śatadrū, that wide-banked, wave-bearing river flowing against the current, and the Hrādinī as well.

**2.65.2**  Having crossed the river at Eladhāna and reached the Aparaparpaṭas, he crossed the rocky Śilā river and passed through Āgneya, the thorn-cutting region.

**2.65.3**  True to his pledges, pure, and splendid, he surveyed the stone-bearing river as he crossed the great mountains, making his way toward the Caitraratha forest.

**2.65.4**  Crossing the swift river named Kuliṅgā and the Hrādinī girt by mountains, he reached the Yamunā and, having forded it, rested his troops.

**2.65.5**  Having cooled his limbs, refreshed the weary horses, bathed and drunk there, he set off again, taking water along.

**2.65.6**  The auspicious prince, in his fine conveyance, crossed the great forest rarely traversed by others as swiftly as the wind crosses the sky.

**2.65.7**  Passing Toraṇa by its southern side, the son of Daśaratha reached Jambūprastha and then proceeded to the pleasant village of Varūtha.

**2.65.8**  Having rested there in that lovely forest, he set off again facing eastward, toward the garden of Ujjihānā where the priyaka trees grow.

**2.65.9**  Reaching the sāla and priyaka groves, Bharata mounted swift horses, gave orders to the army, and pressed forward in haste.

**2.65.10**  Having rested at Sarvatīrtha and crossed the river Uttānakā and various other rivers with mountain-bred horses,

**2.65.11**  reaching Hastipṛṣṭhaka and passing through Kuṭikā, that tiger among men crossed the Kapīvatī at Lauhitya, and then the Ekasāla, Sthāṇumatī, and the river Gomatī at Vīṇatā.

**2.65.12**  Then reaching the sāla forest near the city of Kaliṅga, Bharata arrived swiftly, though his horses were utterly exhausted.

**2.65.13**  Quickly crossing through the forest, at the crimson dawn following the dark of night, he caught sight of Ayodhyā, that city built by King Manu.

**2.65.14**  That tiger among men, having spent seven nights on the road, saw Ayodhyā ahead and spoke from his chariot to the charioteer.

**2.65.15**  "This famed Ayodhyā with its holy gardens, seen from afar, does not appear altogether familiar to me, O charioteer — its soil looks pale."

**2.65.16**  "Guarded by the best of royal sages, it was once filled to abundance with virtuous sacrificing Brāhmaṇas who had mastered the Vedas."

**2.65.17**  "In Ayodhyā there always used to be heard a great tumultuous sound from men and women on all sides — today I do not hear it."

**2.65.18**  "In the evenings the gardens used to shine on all sides with men who had finished their play and were hurrying about in every direction — but not so today."

**2.65.19**  "Those gardens today seem to be weeping, as if abandoned by those who love pleasure. The city appears to me, O charioteer, as though it has become a wilderness."

**2.65.20**  "Nor are the foremost men seen here coming and going as before, in their vehicles, on their elephants, or on their horses."

**2.65.21**  "I see various evil and sinister omens — unpleasant portents — and on account of these my heart is sinking."

**2.65.22**  He entered through the Vaijayanta gate, his horses worn out. The gatekeepers rose to greet him and, after being asked for the welfare of those at the gate, accompanied him onward.

**2.65.23**  With his heart troubled by many forebodings, the Rāghava received the gatekeeper's people with honour and then spoke to the weary charioteer of Aśvapati.

**2.65.24**  "The signs I have heard of that betoken the ruin of kings — all of those signs I now see here, O charioteer."

**2.65.25**  "I see people in the city — both women and men — filthy, eyes brimming with tears, wretched, sunk in thought, emaciated, and long-suffering."

**2.65.26**  Having spoken thus to the charioteer, Bharata with dejected heart, observing those ill omens in Ayodhyā, proceeded to the royal palace.

**2.65.27**  Beholding that city — whose crossroads, houses, and streets were empty, whose gate-panels and bolts were reddened with dust — a city that once rivalled Indra's own in splendour — he became filled to overflowing with grief.

**2.65.28**  Seeing the many things unpleasant to his mind that had never existed before in his city, that great-souled man entered his father's palace with downcast head, dejected in mind, and devoid of joy.

**2.66.1**  Not finding his father there in his father's dwelling, Bharata went to his mother's apartments to see his mother.

**2.66.2**  When Kaikeyī saw her son who had returned from abroad, she sprang up with joy, leaving behind her golden seat.

**2.66.3**  Upon entering his own house and seeing it bereft of prosperity, the righteous-souled Bharata grasped the auspicious feet of his mother.

**2.66.4**  Smelling his head in affectionate greeting, embracing that illustrious one, she seated Bharata on her lap and began to question him.

**2.66.5**  "How many nights has it been now since you left your grandfather's house? Surely you have road-fatigue from coming so swiftly by chariot?"

**2.66.6**  "Is your grandfather in good health? And Yudhājit, your maternal uncle? Please tell me all, my son — was the sojourn pleasant in every respect?"

**2.66.7**  When thus questioned by Kaikeyī, lotus-eyed Bharata, delight of kings, told his mother everything she desired to know.

**2.66.8**  "This is the seventh night since I left my grandfather's house. My grandfather is well, O mother, and so is my uncle Yudhājit."

**2.66.9**  "The wealth and jewels that the mighty king gave me became exhausted on the road, and so I came on ahead of the rest."

**2.66.10**  "I came hastened by the messengers bearing the king's words. What I now wish to ask, O mother, you must tell me."

**2.66.11**  "This gold-adorned couch of yours lies empty, and the people of the Ikṣvāku line do not appear joyful to me."

**2.66.12**  "The king is generally to be found here in your apartments, O mother. I do not see him today, though I have come here wishing to see him."

**2.66.13**  "I want to clasp my father's feet — tell me where he is, I ask you. Is he perhaps in the apartments of the eldest, Kausalyā?"

**2.66.14**  Kaikeyī answered him with words that were dreadful though spoken tenderly — she who should have known better yet was blinded by greed for the kingdom, addressing him as though he did not know: "The end that comes to all living beings — your father has gone to that end."

**2.66.15**  Hearing those words, Bharata — pure, of noble lineage, devoted to righteousness — suddenly fell to the ground, overwhelmed by the force of grief for his father.

**2.66.16**  Then, enveloped in grief, tormented by the pain of his father's death, that great and illustrious one lamented, his mind distraught and agitated.

**2.66.17**  "This was once the very beautiful couch of my father — today it does not shine, bereft as it is of that wise one."

**2.66.18**  Beholding him thus afflicted and fallen to the ground — he who was like a god — she raised up the grief-stricken one and spoke these words.

**2.66.19**  "Rise up, rise up — why do you lie prostrate, O prince of great renown? Men of your kind do not grieve — the righteous who are honoured in assemblies do not."

**2.66.20**  He, having wept for a long time and rolled on the ground, then spoke these words to his mother, wrapped in manifold griefs.

**2.66.21**  "'The king will consecrate Rāma; he will perform the sacrifice' — with this resolve and in gladness I set out on the journey."

**2.66.22**  "But all of this has turned out otherwise, and my mind is split with anguish, for I do not see my father, who was ever devoted to what was dear and beneficial."

**2.66.23**  "By what illness did the king pass away in my absence, O mother? Blessed are all of them — Rāma and the others — by whom the last rites of our father were themselves performed."

**2.66.24**  "Surely the illustrious great king does not know that I have arrived — for otherwise my father would have hastened to bend down and smell my head in greeting."

**2.66.25**  "Where is that gentle hand of my father of blameless deeds, with which he used to wipe the dust from me time and again?"

**2.66.26**  "He who is my brother, my father, and my kinsman — of that wise Rāma of blameless deeds whose servant I am — tell me of him quickly."

**2.66.27**  "For the eldest son who knows dharma becomes a father to the noble man. I will grasp his feet — he is my refuge now."

**2.66.28**  "What did the king, my father of true valour, say at the last, O noble lady? I wish to hear his final good message for me."

**2.66.29**  Thus asked truthfully, Kaikeyī spoke: "Lamenting 'Rāma!', 'Ah, Sītā!', 'Lakṣmaṇa!', that great-souled one, the foremost among those who have attained the highest goal, departed to the other world."

**2.66.30**  "This was the last utterance of your father — seized by the noose of Time and Death's law, like a great elephant caught in a snare."

**2.66.31**  "Those men will have achieved their purpose who will see Rāma return together with Sītā and long-armed Lakṣmaṇa come back again."

**2.66.32**  Hearing this second piece of dear news yet sinking still further, his face despondent, he questioned his mother again.

**2.66.33**  "Where has that righteous soul gone now — he who is the increaser of Kausalyā's joy — together with his brother Lakṣmaṇa and Sītā?"

**2.66.34**  Thus asked, his mother proceeded to tell the full truth, delivering an unwelcome word in the hope that it would be taken as welcome.

**2.66.35**  "For that prince, my son, clad in bark-garments, has gone to the great Daṇḍaka forest together with the daughter of Videha, followed by Lakṣmaṇa."

**2.66.36**  Hearing this, Bharata was alarmed and, out of anxiety regarding his brother's conduct and the greatness of his own lineage, proceeded to enquire further.

**2.66.37**  "Has Rāma taken the life of any Brāhmaṇa? Has he caused harm to any innocent person — rich or poor?"

**2.66.38**  "Or does the prince lust after another man's wife? Why has he been banished to the Daṇḍaka forest like one who has slain an embryo?"

**2.66.39**  Then his fickle mother, in keeping with her own nature as a woman, began to speak of her own deed exactly as it had occurred.

**2.66.40**  "Rāma has taken no property of any Brāhmaṇa; he has harmed no innocent person, rich or poor; nor does Rāma even glance with his eyes at another man's wife."

**2.66.41**  "But I, my son, as soon as I heard of Rāma's consecration, asked your father for the kingdom and for Rāma's banishment."

**2.66.42**  "Your father, abiding by his own nature, did exactly that; and Rāma was sent away along with Saumitri and Sītā."

**2.66.43**  "That ruler of the earth of great renown, not beholding his beloved son, consumed by grief over his son, attained the five elements — that is, he died."

**2.66.44**  "Now, O knower of dharma, the kingship must be taken up by you — for it was for your sake that I did all of this."

**2.66.45**  "Therefore, my son, quickly, in accordance with rite, together with Vasiṣṭha at the head of the brahmins who know the ritual — summon the king and have yourself, a man of undaunted spirit, consecrated on this earth."

**2.67.1**  Having heard of his father's death and his two brothers exiled, Bharata, scorched with grief, spoke these words:

**2.67.2**  "What use is a kingdom to me, a ruined and grieving man, bereft of my father and of a brother who is equal to a father?"

**2.67.3**  "You have heaped sorrow upon my sorrow, pouring salt into a wound — having reduced the king to the state of a departed spirit and Rāma to an ascetic."

**2.67.4**  "You have come like the night of doom for the destruction of our lineage; my father, embracing a live coal, did not perceive the danger."

**2.67.5**  "It would be hard for Kausalyā and Sumitrā, both tormented by grief for their sons, to survive with you as my mother."

**2.67.6**  "Does not the noble and righteous Rāma, who knows how to conduct himself toward elders, treat you with the highest regard, as one would treat one's own mother?"

**2.67.7**  "And Kausalyā, my eldest mother, far-sighted as she is, conducts herself toward you, upholding dharma, as toward a younger sister."

**2.67.8**  "Having sent her son — a man of perfected self, clad in bark-cloth garments — into forest exile, how do you not grieve, O sinful woman?"

**2.67.9**  "Having banished him who sees no evil in others, that hero, that self-perfected, glorious man, clad in bark — what reason do you see for it?"

**2.67.10**  "I think you, greedy one, did not consider my regard for Rāghava; for this great calamity has been brought about by you for the sake of the kingdom."

**2.67.11**  "Without seeing those two tigers among men, Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa, by what power and prowess can I muster the will to protect this kingdom?"

**2.67.12**  "The great king, mighty as he was, ever leaned on that powerful and righteous man, as Mount Meru leans upon the forests of Meru."

**2.67.13**  "How shall I, like a young ox put under a yoke fit for a great draught-animal, bear this burden by what strength?"

**2.67.14**  "Or if by discipline or by the strength of wisdom I find the power, I will not gratify you, you who are greedy for sons to rule — I shall bring back from the forest that brother who is dear to all our people."

**2.67.15**  Having spoken thus, the great-souled Bharata, tormenting her with these harsh words, and overcome with grief, roared once more like a lion lodged in a mountain cavern.

**2.68.1**  Having thus reproached his mother, Bharata, seized with great rage, spoke again:

**2.68.2**  "Fall from the kingdom, Kaikeyī, O cruel and wicked woman! Cast off by righteousness, do not weep over the dead!"

**2.68.3**  "In what did the king wrong you, or Rāma, most steadfast in dharma — those two for whom, because of you, death and exile have alike arrived?"

**2.68.4**  "You have incurred the guilt of brahminicide through the destruction of this lineage; go to hell, Kaikeyī — and do not share the world of your husband!"

**2.68.5**  "By this dreadful deed you have perpetrated such sin, abandoning him who is beloved of all the world, and have brought fear upon me as well."

**2.68.6**  "Because of you my father has departed, and Rāma has taken refuge in the forest; and through you I have been endowed with infamy in the world of the living."

**2.68.7**  "You, who appear as a mother but are my enemy — you cruel, kingdom-hungry, wicked woman, murderess of your husband — I will not speak with you!"

**2.68.8**  "Kausalyā, Sumitrā, and all my other mothers are overwhelmed by great sorrow through you, the defiler of our lineage."

**2.68.9**  "You are not the daughter of Aśvapati, the wise and righteous king — you are a rākṣasī born there, the destroyer of your father's lineage."

**2.68.10**  "Because you sent the righteous Rāma, ever devoted to truth, to the forest in sorrow, and my father has gone to the three worlds above —"

**2.68.11**  "— the sin whose chief author you are falls on me, left without a father, abandoned by my brothers, and hateful in the eyes of all the world."

**2.68.12**  "Having separated Kausalyā, who is united with dharma, from her son by your wicked resolve — what world will you attain today, O you who are destined for hell?"

**2.68.13**  "Why, O cruel woman, do you not understand the certain bond of kinship with Rāma — the eldest, equal to a father — the very son born of Kausalyā?"

**2.68.14**  "A son is born from every limb and from the heart itself; therefore a son is dearer to a mother than even the dearest kinsman."

**2.68.15**  "It is told that once upon a time, Surabhī, the wish-granting cow, honored by the gods, goddess of dharma, saw her two sons utterly spent on the earth —"

**2.68.16**  "— seeing those two sons weary at midday on the ground, she wept with grief for her children, her eyes swimming with tears."

**2.68.17**  "Tiny fragrant drops fell from her onto the body of the great-souled king of gods who was passing below her."

**2.68.18**  "Seeing her, the glorious one, scorched with grief, the wielder of the thunderbolt, Indra, king of gods, spoke with joined palms and in agitation:"

**2.68.19**  "'Is there perhaps some great fear from us or from anywhere? What is the cause of your grief? Tell me, O you who wish the welfare of all.'"

**2.68.20**  Thus addressed by the wise king of gods, the steady Surabhī, skilled in speech, spoke to her lord:

**2.68.21**  "'May what is auspicious befall you — there is no danger from you or from anywhere, O lord of immortals; but I grieve for my two sons sunk in hardship.'"

**2.68.22**  "'Seeing these two lean and pitiful bullocks, scorched by the sun's rays, being beaten by a ploughman, O lord of gods —'"

**2.68.23**  "'— these two born from my own body, pained and crushed by their burden — seeing them I am afflicted. There is nothing as dear as a son.'"

**2.68.24**  "If even Surabhī, the wish-fulfilling cow who has thousands of sons, grieves thus, how then shall Kausalyā, bereft of Rāma, bear to go on?"

**2.68.25**  "She is virtuous, has but a single son, and you have made her bereft as a cow that has lost its calf; therefore you shall suffer constantly, both in this world and the next."

**2.68.26**  "I myself will fully honor the debt to my brother and to my father, and I will increase their glory — of that there is no doubt."

**2.68.27**  "Having brought back the exceedingly radiant son of Kausalyā, I myself will enter the forest frequented by sages."

**2.68.28**  Thus speaking, he fell to the ground in furious rage like an elephant in the forest driven by goad and lance, hissing like a serpent.

**2.68.29**  With reddened eyes, his garments loose, all his ornaments shaken off, that scorcher of enemies, the son of the king, lay fallen on the ground like the banner of Śacī's lord at the close of a festival.

**2.69.1**  Even as the great-souled Bharata was crying out in that manner, Kausalyā, recognizing his voice, said this to Sumitrā:

**2.69.2**  "Bharata, the son of cruel-deeded Kaikeyī, has come; I wish to see that far-sighted Bharata."

**2.69.3**  Having spoken thus to Sumitrā, she — pale, wearing soiled garments, trembling and all but senseless — made her way to where Bharata was.

**2.69.4**  And Bharata, that younger brother of Rāma, accompanied by Śatrughna, then made his way to Kausalyā's dwelling.

**2.69.5**  Then Śatrughna and Bharata, seeing Kausalyā afflicted with grief, embraced her — stricken with sorrow, fallen to the ground, her senses gone.

**2.69.6**  Kausalyā, deeply grief-stricken, addressed Bharata: "This uncontested kingdom desired by you has been obtained — alas, gained swiftly by Kaikeyī through a cruel deed."

**2.69.7**  "Having sent my son clad in bark-cloth to dwell in the forest, what virtue does cruel-sighted Kaikeyī see in it?"

**2.69.8**  "Kaikeyī should quickly send me away as well, to wherever my son of surpassing glory, Hiraṇyanābha, dwells."

**2.69.9**  "Or else I myself, with Sumitrā accompanying me, will gladly set out, placing the agnihotra fire before me, to wherever Rāghava is."

**2.69.10**  "Or if you wish, you yourself should lead me there today, to where that tiger among men, my son, performs his austerities."

**2.69.11**  "This vast kingdom, filled with wealth and grain, full of elephants, horses, and chariots — this has been handed over to you by her."

**2.69.12**  Then Bharata, with joined palms, addressed Kausalyā thus as she lamented, overwhelmed by many sorrows:

**2.69.13**  "Revered one, why do you reproach me, innocent as I am and knowing nothing? You know my great and steadfast love for Rāghava."

**2.69.14**  "May there never arise in him whose sanction the noble one departed — that truest of the faithful, best among the righteous — a thought contrary to the scriptures."

**2.69.15**  "Let him in whose consent the noble one went become a slave of the wicked, make water toward the sun, and kick a sleeping cow with his foot."

**2.69.16**  "Whatever sin falls on a master who assigns a great and useless task to a servant — let that same sin fall on him in whose sanction the noble one departed."

**2.69.17**  "The sin of those who betray a king who protects his people like his own children — let that sin fall on him in whose sanction the noble one departed."

**2.69.18**  "The sin of a king who extracts the sixth part of the harvest as tribute but does not protect his subjects — let that sin fall on him in whose sanction the noble one departed."

**2.69.19**  "The sin of those who promise sacrificial fees to ascetics at a soma sacrifice and then go back on their word — let that sin fall on him in whose sanction the noble one departed."

**2.69.20**  "May he in whose sanction the noble one departed never observe the dharma of the righteous in the midst of battle, crowded with elephants, horses, and chariots, and bristling with weapons."

**2.69.21**  "Let that wicked-souled one in whose sanction the noble one departed destroy the scripture of subtle meaning carefully taught by a wise man."

**2.69.22**  "Let that merciless one in whose sanction the noble one departed eat rice-pudding, porridge, and goat-flesh uselessly, and disrespect his elders as well."

**2.69.23**  "Let him in whose sanction the noble one departed eat choice food alone, even while surrounded in his own house by his sons, wives, and servants."

**2.69.24**  "Let them incur the sin declared for the killing of royal women, children, and the elderly, and the sin for the abandonment of one's servants."

**2.69.25**  "The sin reckoned for one who sleeps through both twilight junctures — let that sin also befall him in whose sanction the noble one departed."

**2.69.26**  "Whatever sin falls on one who sets fire to another's house, whatever sin on one who strikes his elder, and whatever sin on one who betrays a friend — let them incur all those sins."

**2.69.27**  "May he in whose sanction the noble one departed never render service to the gods, to the ancestors, or to his own mother and father."

**2.69.28**  "Let him in whose sanction the noble one departed fall immediately this very day from the world of the righteous, from the fame of the righteous, and from deeds held dear by the righteous."

**2.69.29**  Even as he spoke thus to console Kausalyā — bereft of both husband and son — the king's son, overcome with grief, fell to the ground.

**2.69.30**  Then Kausalyā spoke to Bharata, who was swearing those terrible oaths, senseless and scorched with sorrow:

**2.69.31**  "My grief, my son, is multiplied anew; for as you swear these oaths, you choke the very life out of me."

**2.69.32**  "By good fortune your soul, together with Lakṣmaṇa's, has not swerved from dharma; my dear child, true to your vow, you shall attain the worlds of the righteous."

**2.69.33**  Thus lamenting, the great-souled one, afflicted with grief, his mind disordered from both stupor and the onslaught of sorrow, was utterly broken.

**2.69.34**  For him — delirious, senseless, his reason lost, fallen on the ground, heaving long sighs again and again — that night passed in grief.

**2.70.1**  To Bharata, son of Kaikeyī, who was thus consumed by grief, spoke Vasiṣṭha, foremost among speakers, a sage of most excellent speech.

**2.70.2**  "Enough of grief — may it be well with you, O prince of great renown. Perform now the final last rites due to the king in their proper time."

**2.70.3**  Hearing the words of Vasiṣṭha, Bharata composed himself; and he, versed in dharma, caused all the funerary rites to be performed.

**2.70.4**  The king, his face pale and sallow in hue, was lifted out of the oil vessel and laid upon the ground, as if merely asleep.

**2.70.5**  Having placed Daśaratha upon a splendid couch adorned with many jewels, his son lamented in deepest sorrow.

**2.70.6**  "What was your resolve, O king — while I was away and had not yet returned — to banish Rāma, who knows dharma, and the mighty Lakṣmaṇa?

**2.70.7**  "Where will you go, O great king, abandoning this afflicted people, now bereft of Rāma the lion among men, whose deeds are ever effortless?"

**2.70.8**  "Who, O king, will provide for the welfare and security of this city now that you have departed, dear father, and Rāma has taken refuge in the forest?"

**2.70.9**  "The earth, O king, bereft of you, shines no more — it is widowed. The city appears to me as a night without the moon."

**2.70.10**  To Bharata lamenting thus, his mind cast down in sorrow, the great sage Vasiṣṭha again spoke these words.

**2.70.11**  "Whatever funerary rites are to be performed for this lord of the people — let them be carried out without delay and without hesitation, O mighty-armed one."

**2.70.12**  Saying "So be it" and honouring the word of Vasiṣṭha, Bharata urged on in all matters the officiating priests, the family chaplain, and the ācāryas.

**2.70.13**  Those sacred fires that had been taken out from the fire-house before the king were now carried in due ritual order by the officiating priests and sacrificers.

**2.70.14**  Placing the king, now devoid of consciousness, upon the funeral bier, his attendants — their throats choked with tears and their minds distraught — bore him forth.

**2.70.15**  Scattering silver and gold and garments of many kinds, the people went along the road before the king.

**2.70.16**  Others brought sandalwood and fragrant resins of aguru, the sarala pine, padmaka wood, and devadāru, and with these they built the funeral pyre.

**2.70.17**  Having placed fragrant substances of various high and low kinds thereon, the officiating priests then laid the lord of the earth in the centre of the pyre.

**2.70.18**  Having offered oblations to the sacred fire, the priests recited their sacred formulas for him; and the Sāma-singers chanted the Sāma hymns there in accordance with the scriptures.

**2.70.19**  The royal women, in palanquins and conveyances befitting their station, went forth from the city, escorted by elders.

**2.70.20**  The officiating priests performed the pradakṣiṇā in the reverse direction around the king lying on the fire-heaped pyre; and the women, consumed by grief, with Kausalyā at their head, did likewise.

**2.70.21**  The wailing of the women there was heard — piteous and sorrowful at that hour — like the cries of female krauñca birds, thousands of them crying out in anguish.

**2.70.22**  Then the noble women, weeping, helpless, and lamenting again and again, descended from their conveyances at the bank of the Sarayū.

**2.70.23**  Having performed the water-offerings together with Bharata, the royal women, ministers, and the family priest re-entered the city with eyes flooded with tears and spent ten days lying on the ground in their grief.

**2.71.1**  Then, when the ten days had passed, the prince, having completed the purification rites, caused the śrāddha ceremonies to be performed on the twelfth day.

**2.71.2**  He gave to the brāhmaṇas gems, wealth, and abundant food; and also bāstika grain, much white cloth, and cows by the hundreds.

**2.71.3**  The son of the king gave to the brāhmaṇas female and male servants, vehicles, and great mansions as the post-mortem offerings for his father.

**2.71.4**  Then, at the time of dawn on the thirteenth day, the mighty-armed Bharata lamented, overwhelmed and faint with grief.

**2.71.5**  With voice choked by sobbing, having come there for the purification rites, he stood at the foot of his father's pyre and spoke these words in deepest sorrow.

**2.71.6**  "O father, that brother Rāghava to whose care you entrusted me has gone away to the forest — and thus, bereft of him, I am left desolate by you."

**2.71.7**  "Her son has been sent away to the forest, leaving her without refuge — abandoning that mother Kausalyā, O father, where have you gone, O king?"

**2.71.8**  Beholding that reddish-grey ash, the circle of the place where his father's bones had been burned, he groaned and sank down, devastated, at the extinction of his father's body.

**2.71.9**  Weeping, wretched, he fell to the ground; and as they raised him up he was like the ceremonial banner of Indra toppling from its post.

**2.71.10**  Then all his ministers rushed to him — as sages rush to Yayāti, that pure-vowed king, when he falls at the time of death.

**2.71.11**  Śatrughna too, seeing Bharata overwhelmed by grief, lost consciousness and fell to the ground, remembering their father the protector of the earth.

**2.71.12**  Like one deranged, bereft of his senses, he lamented in deepest sorrow, recalling again and again those many virtues and qualities of his father.

**2.71.13**  A fierce ocean of grief — its source Mantharā, its current the grasping of Kaikeyī, its waves the granting of the boon — immeasurable and impassable, engulfed them.

**2.71.14**  "O father, this Bharata who is tender and young, always fondly indulged by you — leaving him here weeping, where have you gone?"

**2.71.15**  "Did you not always freely offer us food, drink, garments, and ornaments — who will do that for us now?"

**2.71.16**  "Now surely is the time for the earth to split asunder, yet it does not split — it that is bereft of you, the king who knew dharma, the great-souled one."

**2.71.17**  "With my father having attained heaven and Rāma having taken refuge in the forest, what purpose does my life serve? I shall enter the sacred fire."

**2.71.18**  "Bereft of both brother and father, I will not enter Ayodhyā — that city once guarded by the Ikṣvākus — now empty; I will enter the hermitage forest."

**2.71.19**  Hearing the laments of those two and witnessing their calamity, all those who followed them were all the more stricken and afflicted.

**2.71.20**  Then both Śatrughna and Bharata, dejected and exhausted, writhed upon the ground like two bulls with broken horns.

**2.71.21**  Then Vasiṣṭha, their father's family priest, composed and wise, raised Bharata up and spoke these words to him.

**2.71.22**  "Three pairs of opposites operate universally and without exception among all beings; and since these are unavoidable, you ought not to be thus prostrated."

**2.71.23**  And Sumantra too, the knower of truth, raised Śatrughna up, consoled him, and caused him to hear of the arising and passing away of all beings.

**2.71.24**  The two tigers among men rose up, those illustrious ones, and appeared — drenched by rain and heat — like two separate banners of Indra.

**2.71.25**  Wiping away their tears, their eyes reddened, speaking in mournful tones, the ministers urged the two sons toward the remaining funerary observances.

**2.72.1**  Then Śatrughna, younger brother of Lakṣmaṇa, spoke these words to Bharata, who was consumed by grief and was planning the journey.

**2.72.2**  "He who is the refuge of all beings in their distress — how much more then of ourselves — that Rāma, endowed with every virtue, has been sent to the forest by a woman."

**2.72.3**  "And that Lakṣmaṇa — powerful and endowed with valour — why does he not free Rāma, even at the cost of restraining their father?"

**2.72.4**  "Indeed, he who had taken a crooked path, fallen under the dominion of a woman — the king — ought to have been restrained from the very beginning, weighing what was right and wrong."

**2.72.5**  While Śatrughna, younger brother of Lakṣmaṇa, was speaking thus, Mantharā the hunchback appeared at that moment at the eastern gate, adorned with every ornament.

**2.72.6**  Anointed with the finest sandalwood paste, wearing royal garments, garlanded about her waist with bright and variegated strings of ornaments, she looked like a monkey tied with a rope.

**2.72.7**  The doorkeeper, seeing that perpetrator of grave sin, seized the hunchback without mercy and reported her to Śatrughna.

**2.72.8**  "On whose account Rāma is in the forest and your father has laid down his body — this is that wicked and cruel woman. Do with her as you see fit."

**2.72.9**  Śatrughna, steadfast in his vows and deeply aggrieved, upon learning of this spoke thus to all the inhabitants of the inner apartments.

**2.72.10**  "She by whom such intense suffering has been inflicted upon my brothers and my father — let her now reap the fruit of that cruel act."

**2.72.11**  Thus addressed by him, Mantharā, surrounded by her circle of companions, was seized with great force and made the inner chamber resound with her cries.

**2.72.12**  Then all her companions, deeply alarmed, perceiving Śatrughna's fury, fled in every direction.

**2.72.13**  And all the companions of Mantharā conferred together, saying: "This one, having begun thus, will utterly destroy every one of us."

**2.72.14**  "Let us take refuge with Kausalyā, who is compassionate, generous, versed in dharma, and illustrious — for she shall be our unfailing refuge."

**2.72.15**  And Śatrughna, that scorcher of enemies, his eyes reddened with fury, then dragged the shrieking hunchback across the ground.

**2.72.16**  As Mantharā was being dragged hither and thither, her colourful ornaments of many kinds were scattered and broken upon the ground.

**2.72.17**  The splendid royal residence, strewn with those scattered ornaments, shone all the more brightly then, like the autumnal sky.

**2.72.18**  That bull among men, that powerful one, blazing with fury, seized Kaikeyī, threatened her harshly, and spoke rough words.

**2.72.19**  Stricken to the quick by those harsh and painful words, Kaikeyī, greatly distressed and terrified of Śatrughna, came to her son for refuge.

**2.72.20**  Seeing her, Bharata spoke to the wrathful Śatrughna: "Women are inviolable to all beings — let it be forgiven."

**2.72.21**  "I would slay this wicked Kaikeyī, this evil-doer, were it not that righteous Rāma would reproach a matricide."

**2.72.22**  "And if Rāghava were to learn that this hunchback too had been slain, that dharma-souled one would surely never speak to you or to me again."

**2.72.23**  Hearing the words of Bharata, Śatrughna, younger brother of Lakṣmaṇa, desisted from his fury and released Mantharā.

**2.72.24**  Mantharā fell at the feet of Kaikeyī, gasping, tormented by intense pain, and lamented pitifully.

**2.72.25**  Seeing the hunchback, whose senses had been scattered and stunned by Śatrughna's rough handling, Bharata's mother gently consoled her — who had the appearance of one in distress, like a female krauñca bird clinging in desperation as she watched.

**2.73.1**  Then, at the break of dawn on the fourteenth day, the ministers of state assembled and addressed Bharata with these words.

**2.73.2**  "Daśaratha, who was the most revered of our elders, has gone to heaven — he who sent away Rāma, the eldest, and the mighty Lakṣmaṇa into exile."

**2.73.3**  "O prince of great glory, be our king today. Through circumstance alone this kingdom fails — for a realm without a ruler is no trifling matter."

**2.73.4**  "O Rāghava, O son of a king, your kinsmen and all the guilds await you, with all the requisites for royal consecration at hand."

**2.73.5**  "Accept, O Bharata, this great kingdom inherited from your father and grandfathers. Consecrate yourself and protect us, O bull among men."

**2.73.6**  Having circumambulated in reverence all the vessels and materials for the consecration rite, Bharata, firm in his vows, addressed the entire assembly with this reply.

**2.73.7**  "Kingship for our lineage properly belongs always to the eldest. Wise men such as you ought not to speak to me in this manner."

**2.73.8**  "Rāma, our elder brother, shall be lord of the earth. I shall dwell in the forest for fourteen years."

**2.73.9**  "Let a great army of four divisions be made ready. I shall bring back my elder brother, Rāghava, from the forest."

**2.73.10**  "Taking all these consecration materials arrayed before me and placing them at the fore, I shall set out toward the forest for Rāma's sake."

**2.73.11**  "Having consecrated that tiger among men there itself and honored him, I shall bring Rāma back — as fire is brought from a sacrifice."

**2.73.12**  "I shall not fulfill the desires of this woman who still smells of her mother's milk. I shall dwell in the difficult forest; Rāma shall be king."

**2.73.13**  "Let craftsmen level the road — smoothing rough places; let scouts who know difficult terrain march ahead and follow along the path."

**2.73.14**  As the prince spoke thus for Rāma's sake, the entire assembly replied with words that were auspicious and utterly excellent.

**2.73.15**  "May Śrī, the lotus-born goddess of prosperity, attend upon you who speak thus — you who desire to give the earth to your elder, the king's son."

**2.73.16**  "Those words you have spoken, O son of a king, are most excellent." Hearing them resound in their ears, tears of joy fell upon him — drops born from the eyes and faces of noble men.

**2.73.17**  Having heard his words, the ministers, the full assembly, and those whose grief had departed spoke these words with joy; and the people devoted to that foremost of men, as well as the companies of craftsmen, were set in motion by his command.

**2.74.1**  Then those skilled in surveying the land, expert in the craft of laying lines, diligent in their own work, brave — the diggers and the operators of machines —

**2.74.2**  — the work-overseers and master builders, men skilled in mechanical devices, carpenters, road-layers, and tree-cutters —

**2.74.3**  — well-diggers, plasterers, and bamboo-workers, along with all capable overseers — these set out in the vanguard.

**2.74.4**  That vast flood of people, marching toward that destination with joy, shone brilliantly with great momentum, like the ocean at the full moon.

**2.74.5**  Those skilled in road-making, taking their respective turns and equipped with instruments of every kind, set out ahead in orderly procession.

**2.74.6**  Cutting through creepers, vines, thickets, stumps, rocks, and trees of every kind, those men cleared the road.

**2.74.7**  In stretches devoid of trees some planted trees; others cut here and there with axes, picks, and sickles.

**2.74.8**  Others, mightier still, demolished the clumps of vīraṇa grass and cleared away difficult passages and rough ground in every direction.

**2.74.9**  Others filled wells and long-sunken pits with earth; still others leveled the low-lying sections all around.

**2.74.10**  Men then bound together what needed binding, crushed what needed crushing, and broke through what needed breaking, in each stretch of land.

**2.74.11**  In no great span of time they constructed canals carrying abundant water, of many forms, resembling the ocean; and many wells of various kinds, adorned with surrounding parapets.

**2.74.12**  The road had a surface smoothed with plaster, great trees in full bloom, flocks of intoxicated birds uttering their calls, and was adorned with pennants.

**2.74.13**  Sprinkled with sandalwood-scented water and decorated with flowers of every kind, the army's road shone magnificently — like the path to heaven.

**2.74.14**  Then, having received their orders, the appointed officers, each acting according to his assignment, in pleasant regions abounding with sweet fruits —

**2.74.15**  — beautified yet further, with every kind of ornament, the encampment that the high-souled Bharata had intended — that which was itself like an ornament.

**2.74.16**  Those who were versed in such matters established the encampment of the high-souled Bharata under auspicious lunar mansions and at auspicious moments.

**2.74.17**  With many mounds of earth, surrounded by moats, with gateposts resembling Indra's bolt, adorned with grand avenues —

**2.74.18**  — fitted with rows of palaces, enclosed by walls of mansions, beautified with banners, all furnished with well-laid grand highways —

**2.74.19**  — with lofty pavilion-topped towers seeming to spread across the sky, those encampments, tall and majestic, resembled the city of Indra.

**2.74.20**  Having reached the Jāhnavī — the Gaṅgā — with her forests of diverse trees, her cool and pure waters, and her abundance of great fish —

**2.74.21**  — as the sky at night shines immaculately, adorned with the moon and multitudes of stars, so in due time that road shone resplendent — delightful, fashioned by skilled craftsmen of good craft.

**2.75.1**  Then, on that night dedicated to joyous ceremony, bards and panegyrists skilled in the art of speech praised Bharata with hymns filled with auspicious words.

**2.75.2**  The time-drum struck with golden mallets resounded to life; hundreds of conches were blown, along with instruments of high and low tones.

**2.75.3**  That very great noise of the instruments, as though filling the sky, pierced Bharata, already scorched by grief, with fresh grief yet again.

**2.75.4**  Then Bharata, having awakened, caused that din to be stopped; and having declared, "I am not the king," he said this to Śatrughna:

**2.75.5**  "Look, Śatrughna — the great harm that Kaikeyī has done to the world. Leaving all sorrows upon me, King Daśaratha has departed."

**2.75.6**  "The royal glory of that righteous king, that high-souled one — a glory rooted in dharma — now drifts about, like a rudderless ship on water."

**2.75.7**  Seeing Bharata lamenting thus, beside himself, pitiable, all the women then wept aloud.
