**2.75.8**  While he was lamenting thus, Vasiṣṭha, the illustrious one, learned in the dharma of kings, entered the assembly hall of the lord of the Ikṣvākus.

**2.75.9**  The righteous-souled one, together with his attendants, entered that hall — beautiful, fashioned of gold, richly set with gems and jewels — as one enters the Sudharmā, the assembly of the gods.

**2.75.10**  He, the knower of all the Vedas, took his seat upon a golden throne covered with the most precious of cushions, and then gave instructions to the messengers.

**2.75.11**  "Bring quickly, without delay, the brāhmaṇas, the kṣatriyas, the warriors, the ministers, and the heads of the guilds — for we have pressing business."

**2.75.12**  Then a great clamor arose as people came thronging with chariots, horses, and elephants.

**2.75.13**  Then the subjects welcomed Bharata as he arrived, just as the immortals welcome Indra — even as they had once welcomed Daśaratha.

**2.75.14**  The assembly, graced by the son of Daśaratha, shone as it had in Daśaratha's own time of old — like a lake teeming with great fish and noble elephants, its waters still, strewn with gems, conches, and pebbles.

**2.76.1**  Bharata, endowed with wisdom, beheld that assembly hall filled with noble men and adorned with garlands, like a night illumined by the full moon.

**2.76.2**  As the nobles took their seats in proper order, it appeared like a night of the full moon at the close of the rainy season.

**2.76.3**  The purohita, knowing dharma, having surveyed all the subjects of the king fully assembled, then spoke these gentle words to Bharata.

**2.76.4**  "Dear son, King Daśaratha has gone to heaven, having practiced dharma, and having bestowed upon you this prosperous earth, rich in wealth and grain.

**2.76.5**  Rāma likewise, firm in truth, recalling the dharma of the virtuous, has not abandoned his father's command — just as the risen moon does not abandon its moonlight.

**2.76.6**  The kingdom, freed of thorns, has been given to you by your father and your brother. Enjoy it with your ministers in gladness; let the consecration be performed at once.

**2.76.7**  Let the peoples of the north, the west, the south in their entirety, and of Koṭyāparānta along with the sea-coasts bring their jewels and tributes to you."

**2.76.8**  Hearing those words, Bharata, overwhelmed by grief, knowing dharma and longing for righteousness, went to Rāma in his mind.

**2.76.9**  That young man, his voice like the cry of a swan, choked with tears, lamented in the midst of the assembly and reproached the purohita.

**2.76.10**  "Who like me — one who has observed the vow of brahmacarya, who is a bath-graduate of learning, who is wise, who strives steadfastly in dharma — who would seize the kingdom?

**2.76.11**  How could one born of Daśaratha become a usurper of the kingdom? You ought here to declare that both the kingdom and I belong to Rāma by right.

**2.76.12**  The eldest, the best, the righteous-souled Kākutstha, comparable to Dilīpa and Nahuṣa, deserves to receive the kingdom, just as Daśaratha did.

**2.76.13**  If I were to commit this sin, fit only for the ignoble and not leading to heaven, I would become the disgrace of the Ikṣvāku lineage in this world.

**2.76.14**  Whatever sin has been committed by my mother, I do not approve of it. Standing here, I shall pay homage with joined palms to him who dwells in the forest fastness.

**2.76.15**  I shall follow Rāma himself. He is king, the foremost of bipeds. The Rāghava deserves the sovereignty of all three worlds.

**2.76.16**  Hearing those words full of dharma, all the members of the assembly, their hearts set on Rāma, shed tears of joy.

**2.76.17**  But if I shall not be able to bring the noble one back from the forest, I shall dwell there in that very forest, just as the noble Lakṣmaṇa has done.

**2.76.18**  I shall employ every means to forcibly bring him back, in the presence of the venerable ones, the virtuous, and those who walk the path of good qualities.

**2.76.19**  Having spoken thus, the righteous-souled Bharata, devoted to his brothers, addressed Sumantra, the counselor skilled in deliberation, who stood nearby.

**2.76.20**  "Rise quickly and go, Sumantra, by my command. Order the march without delay and muster the army at once."

**2.76.21**  Thus instructed by the great-souled Bharata, Sumantra, filled with joy, ordered everything exactly as commanded, just as he had desired.

**2.76.22**  Those subjects and the commanders of the army were filled with joy upon hearing the march proclaimed for the bringing back of the Rāghava.

**2.76.23**  Then, learning of the departure, all the wives of warriors joyfully hastened their husbands in every household.

**2.76.24**  The commanders of the forces urged the entire army along with the warriors, using horses, swift ox-drawn carts, and chariots swift as thought.

**2.76.25**  Seeing the army readied, Bharata, in the presence of his elders, said to Sumantra standing beside him: "Make my chariot ready at once."

**2.76.26**  Receiving that command of Bharata's, Sumantra, filled with great joy, took up the chariot yoked with the finest horses and set it forward.

**2.76.27**  Then that Rāghava — firm in truth, resplendent, eloquent, mighty in truth and valor — spoke well, intending to appease his illustrious elder kinsman who had gone to the great forest.

**2.76.28**  "Rise at once and go, Sumantra, to muster the army. I wish, for the good of the world, to appease Rāma who dwells in the forest and to bring back the foremost of the army."

**2.76.29**  That son of the charioteer, properly commanded by Bharata and with his desire fully fulfilled, directed all the chief citizens, the foremost commanders of the army, and the circle of friends.

**2.76.30**  Then, rising up, the kṣatriyas, vaiśyas, śūdras, and brāhmaṇas in every household yoked their camel-drawn carts, donkeys, elephants, and horses of noble breed.

**2.77.1**  Then Bharata rose at dawn, mounted his finest chariot, and set out swiftly, longing to behold Rāma.

**2.77.2**  Before him proceeded all the ministers and the purohitas, having mounted chariots yoked with horses, resembling the chariot of the sun.

**2.77.3**  Nine thousand fully caparisoned elephants followed Bharata as he journeyed — the delight of the Ikṣvāku lineage.

**2.77.4**  Sixty thousand chariots bearing archers armed with various weapons followed the illustrious prince Bharata as he marched.

**2.77.5**  A hundred thousand mounted horses followed the illustrious prince Bharata the Rāghava as he marched.

**2.77.6**  Kaikeyī, Sumitrā, and the illustrious Kausalyā, rejoiced at the prospect of Rāma's return, traveled in their resplendent conveyances.

**2.77.7**  The gathered noble folk set forth to see Rāma together with Lakṣmaṇa, their minds delighted, telling wonderful tales about him.

**2.77.8**  "When shall we behold Rāma, dark as a raincloud, long-armed, steady in spirit, firm in his vow — he who destroys the grief of the world?"

**2.77.9**  "For the Rāghava, once seen, will remove our grief, just as the rising sun dispels the darkness of the entire world."

**2.77.10**  Speaking thus, delighted, sharing these auspicious tales and embracing one another, the townspeople set out.

**2.77.11**  All others who were respected there, and all the guild-members too, proceeded joyfully toward Rāma — all the subjects marched.

**2.77.12**  The gem-cutters and the fine potters, the weavers and those who live by arms —

**2.77.13**  The peacock-trainers, sawyers, dyers, piercers, ivory-carvers, plasterers, and those who live by selling fragrances —

**2.77.14**  The renowned goldsmiths, blanket-washers, bath-attendants, clothiers, physicians, incense-sellers, and liquor dealers —

**2.77.15**  The washermen, tailors, headmen of villages and cowpens, actors along with their women, and fishermen likewise went.

**2.77.16**  Composed, learned in the Vedas, brāhmaṇas esteemed for their conduct followed Bharata as he marched, in ox-drawn carts by the thousands.

**2.77.17**  Well-attired, clad in clean garments, anointed with red-tinged unguents, all of them followed Bharata at a measured pace in various conveyances.

**2.77.18**  That army, joyful and delighted, followed the son of Kaikeyī along with their kinsmen; the army that attended Bharata took up position.

**2.77.19**  Surveying the army that followed and the Gaṅgā with its auspicious waters, Bharata, skilled in speech, addressed all his ministers.

**2.77.20**  "Have my army encamp fully in accord with my intention. Having rested, we shall cross the great river tomorrow."

**2.77.21**  "And I wish to descend to the river to offer water for the sake of the post-mortem rites of the king who has gone to heaven."

**2.77.22**  As he spoke thus, the ministers, saying "So be it," composed themselves and caused the various contingents to encamp, each according to its own will, separately.

**2.77.23**  Having encamped the splendidly equipped army along that great river, the Gaṅgā, in due order, Bharata lodged there, brooding at that time upon the return of the great-souled Rāma.

**2.78.1**  Then the king of the Niṣādas, seeing the army encamped along the Gaṅgā river, hastily addressed his kinsmen.

**2.78.2**  "This great army visible here resembles the ocean; I cannot perceive its end, even when I reflect upon it in my mind."

**2.78.3**  "That large-bodied one there with the kovidāra banner on his chariot — he will either bind the fishermen or he will slay us."

**2.78.4**  "Or perhaps Bharata, son of Kaikeyī, advances to slay Rāma, son of Daśaratha, who was exiled from the kingdom by his father."

**2.78.5**  "Rāma, son of Daśaratha, is both my lord and my friend. Standing here on the bank of the Gaṅgā, stand armed and devoted to his cause."

**2.78.6**  "Let all the fishermen stand guard along the Gaṅgā river, armed in force, as guards of the river, subsisting on flesh, roots, and fruits."

**2.78.7**  He urgently ordered that a hundred men of each boat crew man each of the five hundred vessels, all armed and young men standing ready.

**2.78.8**  "When Bharata has been satisfied here regarding Rāma, then this army, bearing good omens, will cross the Gaṅgā today."

**2.78.9**  Having said this, the lord of the Niṣādas, Guha, took gifts of fish, meat, and honey and approached Bharata.

**2.78.10**  Seeing him approaching, the resplendent son of the charioteer, Sumantra, knowing courtesy, announced him to Bharata in a respectful manner.

**2.78.11**  "This headman, surrounded by a thousand kinsmen, is knowledgeable about the Daṇḍaka forest, is an elder, and is a friend of your brother."

**2.78.12**  "Therefore, O Kākutstha, let Guha the lord of the Niṣādas see you — without doubt he knows where Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa are."

**2.78.13**  Having heard this auspicious word from Sumantra, Bharata quickly said: "Let Guha come and see me."

**2.78.14**  Having received permission, Guha, greatly delighted, surrounded by his kinsmen, came before Bharata and, bowing humbly, spoke these words.

**2.78.15**  "This is a district without a palace, and we ourselves are of humble estate. We offer everything we have to you; dwell among us in our fisherman clan."

**2.78.16**  "There are roots and fruits gathered by the Niṣādas, fresh meat and dried meat, and great abundance of forest produce both coarse and fine."

**2.78.17**  "I hope that your army, well-fed, will pass this night here; honored with various desired things, you will depart with your army tomorrow."

**2.79.1**  Thus addressed, Bharata, of great wisdom, replied to Guha, the lord of the Niṣādas, with words bearing reasoned meaning.

**2.79.2**  "Your wish is indeed noble, O friend of my elder, that you alone desire to honour such an army of mine."

**2.79.3**  Having said this, the glorious Bharata, of great splendour, again spoke excellent words to Guha, lord of the Niṣādas.

**2.79.4**  "By which route shall I go to the āśrama of Bharadvāja, O Guha? This region along the Gaṅgā is very dense and difficult to cross."

**2.79.5**  Hearing those words of the wise prince, Guha, who ranged the forest depths, spoke with joined palms.

**2.79.6**  "Skilled archers among our fishermen shall accompany you, O prince of great renown, and I too shall accompany you."

**2.79.7**  "Surely you do not march with ill intent toward Rāma, whose deeds are faultless? This great army of yours raises a suspicion in me."

**2.79.8**  To him speaking thus, Bharata, pure as the sky, replied to Guha with gentle words.

**2.79.9**  "May such a dreadful time never come! You ought not to suspect me — for that Rāghava is my elder brother, equal to a father for me."

**2.79.10**  "I go to bring back Kākutstha who dwells in the forest. You should entertain no other thought, O Guha — I speak truth to you."

**2.79.11**  He, with face beaming with joy on hearing Bharata's words, spoke again to Bharata, filled with delight.

**2.79.12**  "Blessed are you! I see none on earth equal to you, who willingly wish to renounce a kingdom that has come to you without effort."

**2.79.13**  "Undying fame shall surely follow you through the worlds, you who desire to bring back Rāma from his affliction."

**2.79.14**  While Guha was conversing thus with Bharata, the sun lost its lustre and night drew on.

**2.79.15**  Having encamped the army, gratified by Guha, the glorious one returned to his bed together with Śatrughna.

**2.79.16**  Such grief, born entirely of longing for Rāma, beset the great-souled Bharata — one undeserving of it, ever mindful of righteousness.

**2.79.17**  Grief burned the Rāghava with an inner fire, scorching him as a hidden flame scorches a tree consumed in a forest fire.

**2.79.18**  Sweat born of the fire of grief poured from every limb, as the Himālaya, heated by the sun's rays, pours forth its snow-melt.

**2.79.19**  With brooding meditation as its cliffs, sighs as its minerals, a thicket of dejection as its trees, and the fatigue of grief as its lofty peaks —

**2.79.20**  — its living creatures the boundless stupefactions, its bamboos the anguish of affliction — by such a great mountain of sorrow was the son of Kaikeyī overwhelmed.

**2.79.21**  The great-souled Bharata, who had come together with Guha, collected in mind yet deeply despondent — Guha then comforted that Bharata once more concerning his elder brother.

**2.80.1**  Then Guha, who ranged the forest depths, described to the immeasurable Bharata the true conduct of the great-souled Lakṣmaṇa.

**2.80.2**  "I spoke to Lakṣmaṇa, who was keeping vigil, endowed with virtues, bearing a choice bow and quiver, wholly intent on guarding his brother."

**2.80.3**  "'This comfortable bed has been prepared for you, dear one; rest and sleep upon it at ease, O joy of the Rāghava clan.'"

**2.80.4**  "'All these people are accustomed to hardship; you are fit for comfort, O righteous one. We shall keep watch for his protection.'"

**2.80.5**  "'There is none on earth dearer to me than Rāma. I am not carried away by eagerness — even if I speak this, it is not untruth before you.'"

**2.80.6**  "'Through his grace I hope for great renown in this world, abundant attainment of righteousness, and wealth entire.'"

**2.80.7**  "'Therefore I shall guard Rāma, dear friend, as he sleeps with Sītā, bow in hand, together with all my own kinsmen.'"

**2.80.8**  "'Nothing is unknown to me who always roam this forest; we could withstand even a fourfold army in battle.'"

**2.80.9**  "Thus spoken to by the great-souled Lakṣmaṇa, ever mindful of righteousness, all of us were persuaded."

**2.80.10**  "'How can sleep be obtained, or life, or pleasures, while the son of Daśaratha lies on the ground with Sītā?'"

**2.80.11**  "'He who cannot be withstood in battle by all the gods and asuras together — see him, O Guha, lying on the grass with Sītā!'"

**2.80.12**  "'This one son of Daśaratha, possessed of fitting marks, was obtained through great austerity and manifold hardship.'"

**2.80.13**  "'With him banished, the king will not endure long; the earth will swiftly become a widow.'"

**2.80.14**  "'The women, exhausted and weeping with loud lament, have surely fallen silent — the royal palace is devoid of sound today.'"

**2.80.15**  "'I do not expect that Kausalyā, or the king, or my own mother, will all survive this night.'"

**2.80.16**  "'My mother might survive, sustained by care for Śatrughna; but Kausalyā, who bore a hero, being afflicted, will perish.'"

**2.80.17**  "'It is all over, utterly over — without attaining his heart's desire, without installing Rāma on the throne, my father will perish.'"

**2.80.18**  "'Those whose purposes have been fulfilled will, when that time arrives, perform all the funeral rites and cremate the king.'"

**2.80.19**  "'With its pleasant squares and courtyards, its well-laid broad highways, its palaces and mansions, adorned with all manner of gems —'

**2.80.20**  "'— thronged with elephants, horses, and chariots, resonant with the sounds of musical instruments, filled with every auspiciousness, crowded with joyful and prosperous people —'

**2.80.21**  "'— full of groves and pleasure gardens, graced with assemblies and festivals — in my father's royal city they will roam happily.'"

**2.80.22**  "'May we also, together with the one true to his vow and in good health, enter it joyfully when this time has passed.'"

**2.80.23**  "While the great-souled prince stood there lamenting thus, the night passed away."

**2.80.24**  "At dawn, when the sun shone clear, after having both of them form their matted locks, I ferried them across with ease at this bank of the Bhāgīrathī."

**2.80.25**  "The two mighty ones, scorchers of foes, wearing matted locks and bark garments, bearing choice quivers, bows, and swords, resembling bull elephants of the herd, went forth scanning the way with Sītā."

**2.81.1**  Having heard Guha's most painful words, Bharata fell into a stupor right there, at the very spot where the painful account was heard.

**2.81.2**  Tender-limbed, of great inner strength, lion-shouldered, long-armed, with eyes broad as a lotus, youthful and pleasant to behold —

**2.81.3**  — having regained consciousness for but a moment, then utterly despondent, he fell suddenly, like an elephant struck to the heart with a goad.

**2.81.4**  Śatrughna, who stood close beside him, embracing Bharata in that state, wept aloud, beside himself and emaciated by grief.

**2.81.5**  Then all the mothers of Bharata rushed forward — thin from fasting, wretched, worn out by the misfortune of their husband.

**2.81.6**  They gathered weeping around him as he lay fallen on the ground; and Kausalyā, following after, despondent, embraced him.

**2.81.7**  The devoted, sorrow-worn woman held him as a cow holds her calf, and weeping, consumed by grief, questioned Bharata.

**2.81.8**  "Does not the affliction of your sons oppress your body, dear child? The life of this royal family depends on you today."

**2.81.9**  "Seeing you, my son, I live — with Rāma and his brother gone, with King Daśaratha departed, you alone are our protector today."

**2.81.10**  "Have you heard something untoward regarding Lakṣmaṇa, dear child? Or regarding the only son of her who has gone to the forest with his wife?"

**2.81.11**  He, of great renown, having composed himself for a moment though still weeping, consoled Kausalyā and then spoke these words to Guha.

**2.81.12**  "Where did my brother pass the night? Where were Sītā and Lakṣmaṇa? On what bed did he sleep? What did he eat? Tell me, Guha."

**2.81.13**  Thus questioned, Guha, lord of the Niṣādas, told Bharata what he had done when Rāma came as a beloved and honoured guest.

**2.81.14**  "Food of various kinds, delicacies, and diverse fruits were abundantly brought by me for Rāma's meal."

**2.81.15**  "Rāma of true valour declined all of it; indeed he did not accept it, bearing in mind the duty of a kṣatriya."

**2.81.16**  "'For us it is not fitting to receive, O friend; it is always our part to give' — thus were we counselled by that great-souled one, O king."

**2.81.17**  "Having drunk water fetched by Lakṣmaṇa, the great and glorious Rāghava observed a fast that night together with Sītā."

**2.81.18**  "Then Lakṣmaṇa also performed his rite with the remaining water; those three, with restrained speech, attended the twilight devotions with full concentration."

**2.81.19**  "After that, Saumitrī made an auspicious bed, having himself quickly brought kuśa grass for the sake of Rāghava."

**2.81.20**  "Rāma lay down on that bed together with Sītā; and Lakṣmaṇa, having washed their feet, withdrew."

**2.81.21**  "This is that iṅgudī tree's root, and this is that very grass on which Rāma and Sītā both lay that night."

**2.81.22**  "Having fastened his finger-guard and arm-guard, with his two quivers full of arrows, the scorcher of foes Lakṣmaṇa strung his great bow and stood the whole night through, going around him alone on all sides."

**2.81.23**  "And I too, bearing a choice bow and arrows, took my stand there where Lakṣmaṇa stood, guarding him who resembles great Indra, together with my kinsmen who were vigilant and had taken up their bows."

**2.82.1**  Having heard all of this in detail, Bharata, together with his ministers, came to the foot of the iṅgudī tree and beheld that bed of Rāma.

**2.82.2**  He said to all his mothers: "Here on the ground that great-souled one spent the night sleeping; this grass has been pressed down by his body."

**2.82.3**  "Rāma, born of the illustrious Daśaratha, himself supremely illustrious, high-born, and wise, does not deserve to sleep upon the bare earth."

**2.82.4**  "How does this tiger among men, who once lay on beds heaped with the finest spreads and covered with deer-skins above, now sleep upon the ground?"

**2.82.5**  "Always in the upper chambers of palaces, in mansions and in pavilions adorned with gold, silver, and jewels, furnished with the finest coverings —"

**2.82.6**  "— in chambers beautiful with heaped flowers, fragrant with sandal and aguru, gleaming like white clouds, and echoing with the calls of flocks of parrots —"

**2.82.7**  "— ever wakened by the sounds of singing and music, by the jingling of fine ornaments, and by the noble beating of mṛdaṅgas —"

**2.82.8**  "— that scourge of foes, honoured in due time by bards, by many sūtas and māgadhas with fitting songs and hymns of praise —"

**2.82.9**  "This is hard to believe in the world; it does not seem true to me. My mind is confused; I think this must be a dream."

**2.82.10**  "Surely there is no divinity mightier than Time, since Rāma, son of Daśaratha, is brought to sleep upon the earth in this manner."

**2.82.11**  "And Sītā, the lovely daughter of the king of Videha, beloved daughter-in-law of Daśaratha, has lain upon the ground here."

**2.82.12**  "This is my brother's bed; here on this hard and rough ground he turned about, and all this grass has been pressed flat by his limbs."

**2.82.13**  "I think Sītā slept here wearing her ornaments, for here and there one can see scattered drops of gold still clinging to this spot."

**2.82.14**  "Clearly an upper garment of Sītā was spread here then, for these silk threads are still clinging and gleaming in this place."

**2.82.15**  "I think that the bed beside her husband was pleasant enough that the tender, chaste, and gentle Maithilī did not perceive the hardship."

**2.82.16**  "Born into a family of sovereigns of the whole earth, delight of all the world, beloved of all the world — he has abandoned his kingdom, his dearest and most excellent possession."

**2.82.17**  "How does Rāghava — dark as a blue lotus, red-eyed, lovely to behold, and worthy of every comfort — lie here upon the earth, who deserves no hardship at all?"

**2.82.18**  "The Vaidehi has truly achieved her purpose, having followed her husband into the forest; we are all left in uncertainty, bereft of that great-souled one."

**2.82.19**  "With Daśaratha gone to heaven and Rāma retired to the forest, the earth appears to me like a rudderless ship, utterly empty."

**2.82.20**  "No one, even in mind, craves this earth, which is protected by the power of his arms even while he dwells in the forest."

**2.82.21**  "The royal capital is without guard at its enclosures, with horses and elephants unrestrained, with its city gates left open, and unprotected."

**2.82.22**  "Enemies do not covet it, with its joyless army, diminished, in an unstable state, and unprotected — just as they would not eat food laced with poison."

**2.82.23**  "From this day forward I shall sleep on the ground or on grass, living constantly on fruits and roots, wearing matted hair and bark-cloth garments."

**2.82.24**  "For his sake I shall dwell contentedly in the forest for the remaining time, taking upon myself that vow — it shall not prove false for him."

**2.82.25**  "Śatrughna shall dwell here with me for my brother's benefit, while the noble one together with Lakṣmaṇa shall protect Ayodhyā."

**2.82.26**  "May the twice-born install Kākutstha on the throne in Ayodhyā — may the gods grant that this wish of mine be fulfilled."

**2.82.27**  "If, when I propitiate him myself with bowed head in many ways, he still will not relent, then I shall dwell with Rāghava in the forest forever — dwelling there, he has no right to disregard me."

**2.83.1**  Having spent the night right there on the bank of the Gaṅgā, that Rāghava Bharata rose at dawn and spoke thus to Śatrughna:

**2.83.2**  "Rise, Śatrughna — why do you still sleep? Bring quickly the lord of the Niṣādas, Guha — good fortune to you — he shall ferry the army across."

**2.83.3**  "I am awake, not sleeping — I am thinking of our noble brother all the while" — thus spoke Śatrughna, urged by his brother.

**2.83.4**  While those two lions among men were conversing thus with each other, Guha arrived at the proper time, with hands folded, and addressed Bharata:

**2.83.5**  "Did you pass the night comfortably on the river-bank, O Kākutstha? And is all well with your army?"

**2.83.6**  Hearing those words of Guha, spoken out of affection, Bharata, who was fully under Rāma's sway, spoke in return:

**2.83.7**  "Our night was comfortable, O king, and you have honoured us well. Now let your boatmen ferry us across the Gaṅgā with many boats."

**2.83.8**  Then Guha, hastened by hearing Bharata's command, re-entered the town and spoke to those kinsmen of his:

**2.83.9**  "Rise up, awaken — may good fortune always attend you! Draw the boats forth; we shall ferry the army across."

**2.83.10**  Thus commanded, they rose up quickly at the king's order and assembled five hundred boats from all sides.

**2.83.11**  Other boats, recognizable by auspicious marks, large and carrying great loads, fine vessels adorned with flags, fitted with sails, and firmly constructed, gleamed there.

**2.83.12**  Then Guha brought forward a fine boat recognizable by auspicious marks, covered with white blankets, resounding with the noise of bells, and auspicious in every way.

**2.83.13**  Bharata boarded that boat, and the mighty Śatrughna, and Kausalyā, Sumitrā, and the other royal wives.

**2.83.14**  The purohita went first, then the brāhmaṇas who served as preceptors; immediately after them the royal women, and then the carts and the market-wagons.

**2.83.15**  The noise of those who were lighting the camp-fires, of those plunging into the ford, and of those loading the goods and equipment rose up and touched the heavens.

**2.83.16**  Those flag-bearing boats, manned by the boatmen themselves, carrying the people who had boarded them, then shot swiftly across.

**2.83.17**  Some boats were filled with women, others with horses; and some there carried vehicles with their teams and great stores of wealth.

**2.83.18**  Having reached the far bank and disembarked the people, they returned; and the kinsmen of the boatmen busied themselves with various tasks.

**2.83.19**  The elephants, bearing their victory-banners and urged on by their mahouts, shone as they swam across like mountains adorned with flags.

**2.83.20**  Some others boarded boats to cross; yet others crossed on rafts; still others crossed using pots and water-jars as floats; and others crossed by the strength of their arms alone.

**2.83.21**  That sacred army, ferried across the Gaṅgā by the boatmen themselves, set out in the auspicious hour of Maitra toward the excellent forest of Prayāga.

**2.83.22**  Having reassured the army and settled it in camp as was fitting, the great-souled Bharata, accompanied by his priests, set forth to visit Bharadvāja, the foremost of seers.

**2.84.1**  Catching sight of the hermitage of Bharadvāja from a distance of a krośa, that bull among men halted his entire army and went forward with his ministers alone.

**2.84.2**  For that knower of dharma went on foot, having laid aside his weapons and equipment, dressed in two garments of fine linen, placing the purohita before him.

**2.84.3**  Then, approaching the presence of Bharadvāja, that Rāghava left his ministers behind and followed after the purohita.

**2.84.4**  Then, seeing Vasiṣṭha, the great ascetic Bharadvāja quickly rose from his seat, instructing his disciples with the word "arghya — the water of welcome."

**2.84.5**  Meeting with Vasiṣṭha and greeted reverentially by Bharata, that greatly resplendent sage recognized him as the son of Daśaratha.

**2.84.6**  Having offered arghya and water for the feet to both, and fruits afterward, that knower of dharma inquired in due order about the welfare of their family.

**2.84.7**  He asked after Ayodhyā, after the army, the treasury, the allies, and the ministers; knowing what had befallen Daśaratha, he did not mention the king.

**2.84.8**  Vasiṣṭha and Bharata in turn asked him about his well-being — about his body, his sacred fires, the trees, his disciples, the deer, and the birds.

**2.84.9**  Affirming that all was well, the great ascetic Bharadvāja then addressed Bharata thus, bound as he was by his affection for Rāghava:

**2.84.10**  "What business brings you here, you who are the ruler of a kingdom? Tell me everything — for my mind does not feel at ease."

**2.84.11**  "She bore one who destroys enemies and increases the joy of Kausalyā — he who has been sent long into the forest along with his wife and brother."

**2.84.12**  "He of great renown was ordered by a father swayed by a woman to live in the forest for fourteen years."

**2.84.13**  "Do you perhaps wish to do harm to that sinless one? Do you desire to enjoy undisputed sovereignty, yours and that of his younger brother?"

**2.84.14**  Thus addressed, Bharata replied to Bharadvāja, his eyes overflowing with tears of grief, his voice faltering:

**2.84.15**  "I am destroyed if even the venerable one thinks of me in this way. Let him not suspect any fault in me — please do not instruct me thus."

**2.84.16**  "What my mother said in my absence is not something I wished for; I am not pleased by it, and I do not accept that word of hers."

**2.84.17**  "But I have come here as a supplicant to that tiger among men, to bring him back to Ayodhyā and to prostrate myself at his feet."

**2.84.18**  "Knowing me to be thus disposed, you ought to show me your grace. Tell me, O venerable one — where is Rāma, the lord of the earth, now?"

**2.84.19**  Bharadvāja, with kindness, spoke these words to Bharata: "In you, O tiger among men, born of the Rāghava lineage, this is fitting — reverence toward one's guru, self-restraint, and the following of the virtuous."

**2.84.20**  "I knew this already, that it was established in your heart — I asked you merely to confirm it, to magnify greatly your glory."

**2.84.21**  "Your brother dwells on the great mountain Citrakūṭa; you shall go to that place tomorrow — tonight stay here with your ministers. Grant me this wish, O most wise one, O you who are skilled in the objects of desire."

**2.84.22**  Then that noble-visaged Bharata, of distinguished bearing, spoke the word "So be it," and the son of the lord of men resolved in his mind to spend the night in that great hermitage.

**2.85.1**  And that sage, knowing that Bharata had resolved to stay, thereupon invited Bharata, the son of Kaikeyī, with hospitable rites.

**2.85.2**  But Bharata said to him: "Has not this already been done by you — the water for washing feet, the guest-offering, and such hospitality as is fitting in the forest?"

**2.85.3**  Then Bharadvāja replied to Bharata with something of a smile: "I know that you are full of affection and would be pleased with whatever little is offered.

**2.85.4**  But I wish to provide a meal for this army of yours, as befitting my affection — you are worthy of it, O bull among men.

**2.85.5**  And why have you come here having left the army behind at a distance? Why did you not come with the full force, O bull among men?"

**2.85.6**  Bharata replied with joined palms to that ascetic endowed with tapas: "I did not come with the army, revered sir, out of reverence for your sacred presence.

**2.85.7**  The choicest horses, men, and mighty intoxicated elephants, covering the great earth, follow me, revered sir.

**2.85.8**  That they might not harm the trees, the water, the ground, the hermitages, or the leaf-huts — for that reason I came alone."

**2.85.9**  Ordered by the great seer, "Let the army be brought here," Bharata caused the army to advance accordingly.

**2.85.10**  Then, having entered the fire-hall, sipped water, and dried himself, for the purpose of performing the rites of hospitality, he invoked Viśvakarman.

**2.85.11**  "I invoke Viśvakarman and also Tvaṣṭṛ — I wish to perform the hospitality; let all be arranged for me therein.

**2.85.12**  Let all the rivers that flow eastward and those that flow westward, on the earth and in the sky, come together here today from every direction.

**2.85.13**  Let some flow with maireya wine, others with well-brewed surā, and yet others with cool water resembling sugarcane juice.

**2.85.14**  I invoke the divine gandharvas — Viśvāvasu, Hāhā, and Hūhū — and likewise the apsaras goddesses and all the gandharvī women.

**2.85.15**  Ghṛtācī, Viśvācī, Miśrakeśī, Alambusā — those radiant ones who attend upon Śakra, and those who attend upon Brahmā — I invoke all of them together with Tumburu, along with their retinues.

**2.85.16**  That divine forest among the Kurus, adorned like garments and ornaments with leaves, yielding ever-lasting divine fruits for its women — that forest belonging to Kubera — let it be here.

**2.85.17**  Let the blessed Soma ordain for me here the finest food — foods to be chewed, foods to be eaten, to be sucked, and to be licked, in great variety.

**2.85.18**  And varied garlands freshly fallen from the trees, wines and other drinks, and meats of many kinds."

**2.85.19**  Thus composed in meditation and possessed of unequalled splendour, that sage, empowered by his asceticism, spoke with a voice modulated in pitch and intonation.

**2.85.20**  As he meditated with his mind, facing east with joined palms, all those deities came separately, one by one.

**2.85.21**  Then a wind, touching the Malaya and Dardura mountains, blew fitly — cooling, pleasant to the soul, gentle and auspicious, dispelling heat.

**2.85.22**  Then divine showers of flowers descended from the clouds, and the sound of the celestial kettle-drums was heard in all directions.

**2.85.23**  Excellent breezes blew; the hosts of apsarases danced; the divine gandharvas sang; the vīṇās gave forth their notes.

**2.85.24**  That sound, uttered and refined, even and possessed of perfect rhythm, entered heaven, earth, and the ears of all living beings.

**2.85.25**  When that divine sound, delightful to the ears of men, had ceased, the army of Bharata beheld the arrangement made by Viśvakarman.

**2.85.26**  The ground became level for five yojanas in every direction, covered with dense meadows of grass resembling blue vaidūrya gems.

**2.85.27**  In that place grew bilva, kapittha, jackfruit, bījapūraka, āmalakī, and mango trees, adorned with fruits.

**2.85.28**  And from the northern Kurus came a forest yielding divine enjoyments, along with a divine river surrounded by many trees along its banks.

**2.85.29**  Bright buildings with four halls, stables for elephants and horses, clusters of mansions and palaces, and beautiful gateways appeared.

**2.85.30**  A royal palace resembling a white cloud, with a beautiful gateway, decorated with white garlands, and sprinkled with divine fragrances appeared.

**2.85.31**  It was square and spacious, furnished with couches, seats, and vehicles, stocked with all divine flavours, with divine food and garments.

**2.85.32**  All foods were prepared therein; the vessels were washed and spotless; all seats were arranged; it was splendid, with excellent beds well spread.

**2.85.33**  The mighty-armed Bharata, son of Kaikeyī, permitted by the great seer, entered that mansion filled with jewels.

**2.85.34**  All the ministers and the priest followed him, and they were filled with joy on seeing the arrangement of that palace.

**2.85.35**  There Bharata, together with his ministers, approached the divine royal throne, the fan, and the royal parasol, in the manner of a king.

**2.85.36**  He honoured the throne, bowing to it in the name of Rāma, and then, taking up the yak-tail fan, sat on a minister's seat.

**2.85.37**  All the counsellors and the priest sat in order of precedence; then the general and the overseer sat down after them.

**2.85.38**  Then in a moment, by the command of Bharadvāja, rivers of rice-pudding came and presented themselves before Bharata.

**2.85.39**  On both banks of those rivers, smeared with white clay, stood pleasant and divine rest-houses, born of the grace of the brahman sage.

**2.85.40**  And in that very moment, twenty thousand women adorned with divine ornaments came, sent by Brahmā.

**2.85.41**  Twenty thousand women, resplendent with gold, gems, pearls, and coral, came, sent by Kubera.

**2.85.42**  Twenty thousand apsarases came from Nandana — those by whose touch a man appears as if beside himself with rapture.

**2.85.43**  Nārada, Tumburu, Gopa, Parvata, and Sūryavarcas — these kings of the gandharvas sang before Bharata.

**2.85.44**  Alambusā, Miśrakeśī, Puṇḍarīkā, and Vāmanā danced before Bharata by the command of Bharadvāja.

**2.85.45**  The garlands that exist among the gods and in the Caitraratha forest were seen at Prayāga by the command of Bharadvāja.

**2.85.46**  By the power of Bharadvāja, bilva trees became mṛdaṅga drummers, śamī trees became players of the time-beaters, bibhītaka trees became players of stringed instruments, and aśvattha trees became dancers.

**2.85.47**  Then sarala, tāla, tilaka, and naktamāla trees, filled with delight, rushed together there, having become hunchbacks and dwarfs.

**2.85.48**  Śiṃśapā, āmalakī, jambū, and the other creepers of the forest, having assumed the forms of beautiful women, dwelt in the hermitage of Bharadvāja.

**2.85.49**  "Let those who drink wine drink surā! Let the hungry eat rice-pudding! And let them eat the finest meats to their heart's desire!"

**2.85.50**  On the beautiful riverbanks, groups of seven and eight women bathed and anointed each man, one by one.

**2.85.51**  The women with lovely eyes rushed forward, massaging and wiping each other, and those beautiful women plied the men with drink.

**2.85.52**  They fed the horses, elephants, donkeys, camels, and the offspring of Surabhī with sugarcane and sweet treacle; and the mighty warriors of the Ikṣvāku line urged on their mounts.

**2.85.53**  No horse-keeper knew his own horse; no elephant-driver knew his elephant. That army appeared there intoxicated, heedless, and merry.

**2.85.54**  Satisfied with all their desires, smeared with red sandalwood, and attended by the throngs of apsarases, the soldiers cried out:

**2.85.55**  "We will not go back to Ayodhyā! Nor will we go to the Daṇḍaka forest! May Bharata fare well! And may Rāma find happiness!"

**2.85.56**  Thus the infantry soldiers, the elephant-riders, horsemen, and grooms — otherwise without a lord — on receiving that bounty, uttered these words.

**2.85.57**  Those thousands of men who had followed Bharata shouted with great joy and said, "This is heaven itself!"

**2.85.58**  Even after they had eaten their fill of that food equal to nectar, on seeing those divine delicacies they felt the desire to eat again.

**2.85.59**  The servants, slave-girls, young wives, and all the fighting men were exceedingly satisfied, and all were clad in fresh garments.

**2.85.60**  The elephants, donkeys, camels, cows, horses, deer, and birds were all well-fed there; and no one tended to another's animal by mistake.

**2.85.61**  Not a single man was seen there without white garments, nor hungry, nor dirty, nor with hair dishevelled with dust.

**2.85.62**  And with goat and boar meats cooked to perfection, with soups prepared with the essence of fruits, fragrant and savoury,

**2.85.63**  bearing flower-banners, filled all around with white food — thousands of iron vessels were seen there with amazement by the men.

**2.85.64**  On the outskirts of the forest there appeared wells of rice-pudding, wish-fulfilling cows, and trees dripping with honey.

**2.85.65**  Tanks were filled with maireya wine, surrounded by heaps of choice meat, and with roasting-pans of roadside peacock and chicken.

**2.85.66**  Thousands of golden vessels appeared, along with cooking-pots, jars, and barley-gruel bowls filled with well-prepared curds, of the fragrant white kapittha fruit in its prime.

**2.85.67**  Some lakes were filled with sweet mango juice, others with white curds; and at the end appeared heaps of rice-pudding and piles of sugar.

**2.85.68**  At the bathing-ghats of the rivers, the men saw various unguents, powders, astringent preparations, and bathing requisites stored in vessels.

**2.85.69**  Bright, fresh tooth-cleaning twigs in heaps, and white sandal-wood pastes stood in their containers.

**2.85.70**  There too were polished mirrors, heaps of garments, and thousands of pairs of wooden sandals and shoes.

**2.85.71**  Collyrium applicators, combs, brushes, parasols, bows, varied pieces of armour, couches, and seats as well.

**2.85.72**  Watering-ponds filled for donkeys, camels, elephants, and horses; and beautiful pools to bathe in, adorned with blue lotuses and water-lilies.

**2.85.73**  And soft heaps of barley, the colour of blue vaidūrya gems, for the feeding of the animals — all these they saw there everywhere.

**2.85.74**  Those men were struck with wonder at that marvellous scene, like unto a dream, on seeing the such hospitality rendered to Bharata by the great sage.

**2.85.75**  Thus, as they revelled as if in the Nandana grove of the gods, that night passed away in the lovely hermitage of Bharadvāja.

**2.85.76**  Then those rivers, the gandharvas, and all those beautiful women departed in the way they had come, after taking leave of Bharadvāja.

**2.85.77**  Just so those men remained intoxicated and stupefied with wine, sprinkled with divine aguru and sandalwood; just so those various divine and choice garlands lay scattered and trampled by men.

**2.86.1**  Then Bharata, having spent that night with his retinue, having been thus hospitably treated, approached Bharadvāja of his own accord.

**2.86.2**  The sage Bharadvāja, having performed the agnihotra oblation, looked upon that tiger among men as he came with joined palms and addressed Bharata.

**2.86.3**  "Did you pass a comfortable night here in our domain? Is your whole company complete? Tell me, O sinless one, how was the hospitality?"

**2.86.4**  Bharata, having joined his palms and bowed down, spoke to the sage of supreme splendour who had come forth from the hermitage.

**2.86.5**  "I have spent the night in comfort, revered sir, with my entire force and cavalry. I and my ministers have been powerfully satisfied with all our desires by you.

**2.86.6**  Free from fatigue and distress, provided with excellent food and excellent lodgings, all of us — including even the servants — have slept in great comfort.

**2.86.7**  I take leave of you, revered sir, O best of seers, as I set out towards my brother's presence — look upon me with a kindly eye.

**2.86.8**  Tell me, O knower of dharma, the hermitage of that righteous great-souled one — which is the path, and how far is it? Tell me."

**2.86.9**  Thus questioned, the greatly brilliant and greatly ascetic Bharadvāja replied to Bharata, who was eager to behold his brother.

**2.86.10**  "O Bharata, at two and a half yojanas from here, in the uninhabited forest, is Mount Citrakūṭa, pleasant with its caves and groves.

**2.86.11**  On its northern side flows the river Mandākinī, overhung with flowering trees, with beautiful and flowering groves.

**2.86.12**  Adjacent to that river is Mount Citrakūṭa, and beyond it is the leaf-hut — there, dear one, those two certainly dwell.

**2.86.13**  Lead your army, filled with elephants, horses, and chariots, by the southern path, keeping it to your left and right, O commander of forces, O fortunate one — then you will see Rāghava."

**2.86.14**  On hearing of the departure of that king of kings, the women — those who were worthy of conveyances — left their vehicles and gathered around the brahman sage.

**2.86.15**  Trembling, thin, and wretched, Kausalyā, together with the queens and Sumantrī, clasped the feet of the sage with both hands.

**2.86.16**  Kaikeyī, whose desire was unfulfilled, condemned by all the world, grasped his feet with shame.

**2.86.17**  Having circumambulated that blessed great sage, she stood at a little distance near Bharata himself, dejected in mind.

**2.86.18**  Then Bharadvāja of firm vows asked Bharata: "I wish to know in detail about your mothers, O Rāghava."

**2.86.19**  Thus addressed by Bharadvāja, the righteous Bharata, skilled in speech, with joined palms, spoke these words.

**2.86.20**  "She whom you see, revered sir — wretched, emaciated by grief and scanty food — she is the chief queen of my father, worshipful as a goddess.

**2.86.21**  This is Kausalyā, who bore Rāma, that tiger among men who walks with a lion's gait — as Aditi bore the Sustainer Viṣṇu.

**2.86.22**  She who clings to her left arm and stands there heavy-hearted, like a branch of the karṇikāra with withered flowers in the heart of the forest —

**2.86.23**  her two sons, O queen, are the princes of divine appearance — both Lakṣmaṇa and Śatrughna, heroes of true valour.

**2.86.24**  On account of whom those two tigers among men departed from here losing their life in the forest, and the king Daśaratha, bereft of his son, departed to heaven —

**2.86.25**  know that this woman — Kaikeyī, craving for power, ignoble though bearing a noble form, cruel and fixed in sin — is my mother, from whom I see springing all this great calamity upon myself."

**2.86.26**  Having said this, that tiger among men, with a voice choked with tears, his eyes red, sighed repeatedly like an angry serpent.

**2.86.27**  Then the great sage Bharadvāja, of great wisdom, replied to Bharata as he spoke thus, with these meaningful words.

**2.86.28**  "Kaikeyī must not be regarded as guilty by you, O Bharata — for this exile of Rāma will prove to be productive of happiness in the end.

**2.86.29**  Having bowed to him, having circumambulated him, and taken his leave, Bharata, his purpose fulfilled, urged the army: "Let the army be made ready!"

**2.86.30**  Then, having yoked the divine horses to the chariots adorned with gold, men of many kinds mounted them in great numbers, ready for the march.

**2.86.31**  Young she-elephants and bull-elephants, with golden girths, bearing banners, set out trumpeting like rain-clouds at the end of summer.

**2.86.32**  Varied vehicles, both large and small, of great worth, moved forward; and the infantry marched on foot.

**2.86.33**  Then the women with Kausalyā at their head, eager to behold Rāma, proceeded joyfully in the finest vehicles.

**2.86.34**  And the splendid Bharata, with his retinue, mounted a beautiful palanquin harnessed and gleaming like the young sun, and set forth.

**2.86.35**  That great army, teeming with elephants, horses, and chariots, having set out and turned toward the southern direction, rising like a great cloud, crossed through forests frequented by deer and birds.

**2.86.36**  That army of Bharata, with its rejoicing elephants, horses, and warriors, frightening the herds of deer and flocks of birds, as it plunged into that great forest, shone magnificently there.

**2.87.1**  Harassed by that great and advancing army with its banners, the leaders of elephant herds, maddened, fled away together with their herds.

**2.87.2**  Bears, herds of spotted deer, and ruruantelopes were seen on all sides in the forest glades, on the mountains, and near the rivers.

**2.87.3**  That righteous son of Daśaratha set out with gladness, surrounded by the great, tumultuous, four-divisioned army.

**2.87.4**  The army of the great-souled Bharata, resembling the flood of the ocean, covered the earth as clouds cover the sky in the rainy season.

**2.87.5**  Covered by the multitudes of horses and by swift-moving elephants, the earth was invisible for a long time during that period.

**2.87.6**  Having travelled a great distance, with his mounts greatly tired, the splendid Bharata spoke to Vasiṣṭha, the best of counsellors.

**2.87.7**  "The landscape that appears to the eye and what I have heard described — evidently we have arrived at the region that Bharadvāja indicated.

**2.87.8**  This is Mount Citrakūṭa, and there the river Mandākinī; and that forest visible from afar, resembling a dark cloud, gleams before us.

**2.87.9**  The pleasant ridges of Mount Citrakūṭa are now being trampled by my elephants, which are themselves like mountains.

**2.87.10**  These trees on the mountain ridges are shedding their flowers like dark rain-clouds releasing water at the end of summer heat.

**2.87.11**  Look, O Śatrughna, at this mountain haunted by kinnaras, covered all around by horses — like the ocean filled with sea-monsters.

**2.87.12**  These herds of deer, driven forward at swift speed, scatter in every direction like clouds tossed by the wind in the sky in autumn.

**2.87.13**  These trees place fragrant flower-crowns on their crests with their cloud-gleaming canopies, like the men of the south with their garlands.

**2.87.14**  This forest, though it once appeared fierce and silent, now seems to me like Ayodhyā thronged with people.

**2.87.15**  The dust raised by hooves stands covering the sky; the wind carries it swiftly, as if doing me a favour.

**2.87.16**  Look, O Śatrughna, at these chariots yoked with horses, manned by chief charioteers, rushing swiftly through the forest.

**2.87.17**  Look at these lovely peacocks, frightened by the commotion, entering this mountain that is the abode of birds.

**2.87.18**  This region appears to me exceedingly beautiful; this abode of ascetics clearly appears like the path to heaven.

**2.87.19**  Many deer together with does, and spotted antelopes in the forest, of pleasing form, are seen like paintings adorned with flowers.

**2.87.20**  "Let the soldiers halt and search the forest, so that those two tigers among men, Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa, may be seen."

**2.87.21**  Hearing Bharata's words, armed men, heroes all, entered that forest, and there they saw smoke.

**2.87.22**  Observing the tip of the smoke, they came back and said to Bharata: "Fire does not exist in an uninhabited place — evidently the Rāghavas are right here."

**2.87.23**  "Or, if those two tiger-princes, scorchers of foes, are not here, then there are clearly other ascetics dwelling here who are like Rāma."

**2.87.24**  Hearing that well-considered speech of theirs, Bharata, crusher of the power of enemies, addressed all those soldiers.

**2.87.25**  "Let all of you halt here — no one is to go ahead. I alone shall go forward, and Sumantra, and the preceptor as well."

**2.87.26**  Thus instructed, all of them halted there on every side, while Bharata fixed his gaze on the place where the tip of the smoke arose.

**2.87.27**  That army which had been halted by Bharata, even as it looked toward the tip of the smoke ahead, before long became joyful, knowing that the meeting with beloved Rāma was at hand.

**2.88.1**  Having dwelt long on that mountain, he who delighted in hills and forests, wishing to please the Videhan princess and also to charm his own heart—

**2.88.2**  —then Daśaratha's son, radiant as an immortal, pointed out the variegated Citrakūṭa to his wife, even as Puraṃdara shows Śacī the wonders of heaven.

**2.88.3**  "Neither the loss of the kingdom, dear one, nor the separation from friends distresses my mind, for I have seen this delightful mountain."

**2.88.4**  "Behold this mountain, dear one, thronged with hosts of many birds, adorned with mineral-streaked peaks that seem to pierce the sky."

**2.88.5**  "Some regions gleam like silver, some resemble blood-red ochre, some are yellow-madder-coloured, and some shine with the brilliance of precious gems."

**2.88.6**  "Some parts blaze like Puṣya's star, like the sun, like Ketuka flowers; some gleam with a liquid luminescence—the slopes of this king of mountains shine forth, adorned with mineral ores."

**2.88.7**  "This mountain shines, surrounded by herds of various deer, leopards, bears, and troops of trees—harmless and teeming with multitudes of birds."

**2.88.8**  "With mango, rose-apple, Asana, Lodhra, Priyāla, jackfruit, Dhava, Aṅkola, Bhavya, Tiniśa, Bilva, Tinduka, and bamboo—"

**2.88.9**  "—with Kāśmarya, Ariṣṭa, Varaṇa, Madhūka, Tilaka, Badarī, Āmalaka, Nīpa, Vetra, Dhanvana, and Bījaka trees—"

**2.88.10**  "—bearing flowers, laden with fruits, providing shade, and delightful—crowded with these and others, this mountain flourishes in its glory."

**2.88.11**  "On the charming mountain plateaus, dear one, behold these Kinnaras who delight the heart with desire—noble beings sporting in pairs."

**2.88.12**  "Behold the swords hung from branches, the fine garments, and the charming pleasure-spots of Vidyādhara women."

**2.88.13**  "With cascading waterfalls, with springs breaking forth, with rivulets flowing here and there, this mountain shines like an elephant streaming with ichor."

**2.88.14**  "The breeze from the caves, bearing the fragrance born of many flowers, gratifying the sense of smell as it draws near—what person would it not fill with joy?"

**2.88.15**  "If I were to dwell here many autumns together with you, blameless one, and with Lakṣmaṇa, sorrow would not consume me."

**2.88.16**  "In this peak abounding in flowers and fruits, charming, thronged with various flocks of birds, in this summit of marvellous hues, I have found delight, fair one."

**2.88.17**  "Through this forest-dwelling I have obtained a twofold fruit: discharge of my debt of duty to my father, and the fulfilment of Bharata's wish."

**2.88.18**  "Are you content, Vaidehī, dwelling with me on Citrakūṭa, beholding the varied expressions of those restrained in mind, word, and deed?"

**2.88.19**  "This very life is called nectar by foremost royal sages; my great ancestors proclaimed that the forest-dwelling serves the highest purpose of existence."

**2.88.20**  "The boulders of the mountain shine on all sides in their hundreds—vast, manifold, of manifold colours: blue, yellow, white, and red."

**2.88.21**  "At night the luminous herbs of this king of mountains shine by their own radiance like tongues of fire, blazing in their glory by the thousands."

**2.88.22**  "Some regions resemble a palace, some look like pleasure-gardens, and some single great rocks gleam on this mountain, fair one."

**2.88.23**  "Citrakūṭa rises as if having split open the earth; that summit of Citrakūṭa appears auspicious in every direction."

**2.88.24**  "Behold the beds spread out for lovers, covered with Kuṣṭha, Puṃnāga, Tagara, Bhūrja leaves, and upper coverings of lotus petals—couch-like and spread out."

**2.88.25**  "Garlands of lotuses, pressed and shot through by bows, are seen scattered by lovers—behold, dear one, the many kinds of fruit lying about as well."

**2.88.26**  "That mountain Citrakūṭa, abounding in roots, fruits, and water, surpasses even Vasvaukasāra's lotus-lake and the northern Kurus in its abundance."

**2.88.27**  "Sporting through this season, dear Sītā, together with you and with Lakṣmaṇa, I shall attain that contentment which augments the dharma of our lineage—standing on the path of the good, by my own supreme vows."

**2.89.1**  Then, descending from the mountain, the lord of Kosala showed the Maithilī princess the charming river Mandākinī with its auspicious waters.

**2.89.2**  And Rāma, lotus-eyed, spoke to that daughter of the Videha king, she of noble waist and face fair as a lovely moon:

**2.89.3**  "Behold the river Mandākinī—her sandy banks variegated, charming, frequented by geese and cranes, adorned with flowers."

**2.89.4**  "Encircled by diverse trees growing along her banks—flowering and fruit-bearing—she shines on all sides like the lotus-pool of the king of kings."

**2.89.5**  "The fords, their waters now muddied by herds of deer that have drunk there, are charming and arouse delight in me."

**2.89.6**  "At the appointed hour, sages wearing matted locks and antelope-skin, their upper garments of bark-cloth, plunge into the river Mandākinī, dear one."

**2.89.7**  "Other ascetics with sharp vows stand with arms raised, worshipping the sun with discipline, wide-eyed one."

**2.89.8**  "The mountain seems to dance, its peaks tossed by the wind, its trees scattering leaves and flowers all around the river."

**2.89.9**  "How gem-bright are her waters! How rich in sandbanks! How thronged with Siddhas! Behold the river Mandākinī!"

**2.89.10**  "Behold the masses of blossoms blown off and spread out by the wind; behold others floating, borne along in the middle of the stream."

**2.89.11**  "The cakravāka birds, whose calls are most sweet, cry out auspicious sounds as they mount those flowers—how charming, auspicious one!"

**2.89.12**  "The sight of Citrakūṭa and of the Mandākinī, radiant one—I deem it greater than dwelling in the city, and greater even than the sight of you alone."

**2.89.13**  "Come, sport with me in these ever-stirred waters, alongside the Siddhas whose impurities have been shaken off, endowed with austerity, self-restraint, and tranquillity."

**2.89.14**  "Plunge into this Mandākinī as into a friend's embrace, Sītā—submerging the lotuses and water-lilies, fair one."

**2.89.15**  "Consider the wild beasts like the townspeople, dear one; consider the mountain like Ayodhyā; consider this river always as the Sarayū."

**2.89.16**  "And virtuous-souled Lakṣmaṇa stands ever at my command; you too are devoted to me, Vaidehī—both of you fill me with joy."

**2.89.17**  "Bathing at the three sacred hours, subsisting on honey, roots, and fruits—dwelling with you now, I do not yearn for Ayodhyā, nor for the kingdom."

**2.89.18**  "There is no one who, seeing this charming river—stirred by herds of elephants, her waters drunk by elephants, lions, and monkeys, adorned with trees bearing abundant blossoms—would not feel his step gone easy and be content."

**2.89.19**  Speaking thus many gathered words to his beloved companion, directed toward the river, he—the increaser of the Raghu lineage—roamed through that charming Citrakūṭa, radiant as collyrium to the eyes.

**2.90.1**  While he dwelt there, as Bharata approached, the dust and the sound of his army rose up, touching the sky.

**2.90.2**  In that moment, terrified by the great noise, the maddened bull-elephants, tormented, fled in all directions with their herds.

**2.90.3**  Rāghava heard that sound arising from the army, and he watched as all those bull-elephants scattered and fled.

**2.90.4**  Seeing them scatter in flight and hearing that sound, Rāma spoke to Saumitri, the blazing-vigoured Lakṣmaṇa:

**2.90.5**  "Look here, Lakṣmaṇa—Sumitrā is blessed to have such a son as you! A tumultuous sound is heard, deep as terrible thunder."

**2.90.6**  "Is a king or a royal prince ranging the forest in the hunt, or is it some other wild beast, O Saumitri? You must ascertain the truth of all this—discover it quickly, as it really is."

**2.90.7**  Lakṣmaṇa, swift to act, climbed a flowering Śāla tree, and scanning all directions, he gazed to the east.

**2.90.8**  Looking northward, he discerned a great army—crowded with chariots, horses, and elephants, joined with alert foot-soldiers.

**2.90.9**  He reported to Rāma that army filled with horses and elephants, adorned with chariot-banners, and spoke these words:

**2.90.10**  "Let the noble one extinguish the fire; let Sītā take shelter in the cave; string your bow, and take up your arrows and armour as well."

**2.90.11**  Rāma, that tiger among men, replied to Lakṣmaṇa: "Observe the insignia, Saumitri—whose army do you think this is?"

**2.90.12**  Thus addressed by Rāma, Lakṣmaṇa spoke his words—enraged, as though wishing to burn that army like an angry fire.

**2.90.13**  "Desiring the secure kingdom, and clearly having now obtained his consecration, Bharata, Kaikeyī's son, is coming to kill us both."

**2.90.14**  "That very great and resplendent tree shines forth—the Kovidāra banner with its upraised trunk blazes on his chariot."

**2.90.15**  "Some mounted on swift horses ride at will; others, glittering and exultant, the riders mounted on elephants, shine forth."

**2.90.16**  "With bows in hand, let us two take to the mountain, O hero—or let us stand right here, armoured and weapons raised, so that he of the Kovidāra banner may come under our power in battle."

**2.90.17**  "I shall look upon that Bharata, on account of whom such great calamity has befallen you, Rāghava, and Sītā, and me alike."

**2.90.18**  "This enemy Bharata, on whose account you, Rāghava, have been deprived of the eternal kingdom—he is fit to be slain by me, O hero."

**2.90.19**  "I see no fault in slaying Bharata, Rāghava—for in abandoning those who have first done harm, no unrighteousness is ordained. Once he is slain, rule the entire earth."

**2.90.20**  "Today let Kaikeyī, who craved the kingdom, see her son slain in battle by me—struck down with deep agony, like a tree shattered by an elephant."

**2.90.21**  "And I shall slay Kaikeyī together with her partisans and kinsmen. Let the earth today be freed from this great pollution."

**2.90.22**  "Today I shall release this pent-up wrath and the dishonour shown to the giver of honour upon the enemy's army, like fire upon dry grass."

**2.90.23**  "Today I shall drench this forest of Citrakūṭa with blood, piercing the bodies of enemies with my sharp arrows."

**2.90.24**  "Let the jackals and wild beasts drag away the elephants and horses whose hearts have been pierced by my arrows, and likewise the men I have slain."

**2.90.25**  "Having slain Bharata together with his army in this great forest, I shall have discharged my debt to my arrows and my bow—of this there is no doubt."

**2.91.1**  Then Rāma, having gently calmed Lakṣmaṇa the son of Sumitrā who was enraged and overcome with fury, spoke these words.

**2.91.2**  What use here is a bow, or a sword with its shield, now that Bharata himself—that great archer, that supremely wise one—has come in person?

**2.91.3**  It is only fitting that Bharata wishes to see us now; he would not commit even in his mind any harm against us.

**2.91.4**  Has Bharata ever done anything displeasing to you before? What manner of fear is it that you now suspect in Bharata?

**2.91.5**  You must not speak any harsh or unpleasant word to Bharata; for if harm were done to Bharata, I myself would be the one spoken ill of.

**2.91.6**  How could sons slay their own father in any adversity? Or how could a brother slay his brother, O son of Sumitrā—a brother who is one's own very life?

**2.91.7**  If you speak these words on account of the kingdom, then upon meeting Bharata I shall say to him: let the kingdom be given to him.

**2.91.8**  For Bharata, O Lakṣmaṇa, when truly told by me 'give him the kingdom,' will respond with nothing but 'so be it.'

**2.91.9**  Thus addressed by his brother of righteous character, who was ever devoted to his welfare, Lakṣmaṇa seemed to shrink into his own body with shame.

**2.91.10**  Seeing Lakṣmaṇa abashed, the Rāghava spoke again: "I think that mighty-armed one comes here to see us."

**2.91.11**  Reflecting on our forest-dwelling, he will take us back to the city—or at least this daughter of Videha who is accustomed to every comfort.

**2.91.12**  Those two noble horses shine forth, charming, swift as the wind, O hero—the finest of steeds.

**2.91.13**  That very large elephant named Śatruṃjaya—the aged and wise one belonging to our father—trembles at the head of the army.

**2.91.14**  Then Lakṣmaṇa, that conqueror of battles, having descended from the treetop of the sāla tree, stood at Rāma's side with palms joined in reverence.

**2.91.15**  Then, directed by Bharata so that there be no overcrowding, they arranged the camp of the army all around that mountain.

**2.91.16**  The army of the Ikṣvākus, crowded with elephants, horses, and chariots, encamped on the flank of the mountain, covering a space of one and a half yojanas.

**2.91.17**  That army at Citrakūṭa, led by Bharata with righteousness foremost and pride cast aside, shone forth, guided by the wise one for the purpose of propitiating the son of Raghu.

**2.92.1**  Having settled the army, that lord, finest among those who travel on foot, desired to go on foot to meet Kākutstha in the manner becoming toward an elder.

**2.92.2**  As soon as the army had settled in its proper position in an orderly manner, Bharata spoke these words to his brother Śatrughna.

**2.92.3**  "Quickly, O gentle one, together with these groups of men and hunters, you should search this forest all around."

**2.92.4**  "Until I see Rāma, or mighty Lakṣmaṇa, or the illustrious Vaidehī, there will be no peace for me."

**2.92.5**  "Until I behold my brother's auspicious face, moon-like, with eyes like lotus petals, there will be no peace for me."

**2.92.6**  "Until I bear on my head my brother's feet—marked with royal auspicious signs—there will be no peace for me."

**2.92.7**  "Until he who deserves kingship is established in the ancestral kingdom, drenched with the waters of consecration, there will be no peace for me."

**2.92.8**  "Fulfilled in purpose is that illustrious daughter of Janaka, Vaidehī, who follows her husband, lord of the earth bounded by the ocean."

**2.92.9**  "Blessed is that Citrakūṭa, that mountain resembling the king of mountains, in which Kākutstha dwells like Kubera in the Nandana grove."

**2.92.10**  "That inaccessible forest, frequented by wild beasts, has accomplished its purpose, for in it resides Rāma of great splendor, finest among those who bear weapons."

**2.92.11**  Having spoken thus, Bharata of great splendor, that bull among men, entered the great forest on foot alone.

**2.92.12**  He, the foremost among speakers, passed through the midst of those thickets of trees grown on the mountain slopes with their flower-laden tips.

**2.92.13**  He approached a flowering sāla tree on Mount Citrakūṭa and saw the pillar of smoke rising from the fire that belonged to Rāma's hermitage.

**2.92.14**  Seeing it, the illustrious Bharata rejoiced together with his kinsmen, knowing "Rāma is here"—as if he had crossed to the far shore of the waters.

**2.92.15**  Having spotted Rāma's sacred hermitage on Mount Citrakūṭa, that great-souled one, after settling the army once more, swiftly went forward together with Guha.

**2.93.1**  Once the army had encamped, the eager Bharata then went to see his brother, pointing the way to Śatrughna.

**2.93.2**  Having instructed the seer Vasiṣṭha—"Bring my mothers quickly"—he who was devoted to his elders hastened on ahead.

**2.93.3**  Sumantra too followed close behind Śatrughna, for his longing to behold Rāma was just as great as Bharata's own.

**2.93.4**  While walking, the illustrious Bharata then beheld his brother's leaf-hut standing in that ascetics' abode, and also the cottage.

**2.93.5**  Before that shelter Bharata then saw broken pieces of firewood and flowers that had been gathered.

**2.93.6**  He also saw in that forest large heaps gathered of dried dung of deer and buffalo, stored against the cold.

**2.93.7**  Even while walking, the mighty-armed and radiant Bharata spoke joyfully to Śatrughna and to all those ministers.

**2.93.8**  "I believe we have reached the place that Bharadvāja described; I think the river Mandākinī is not too far from here."

**2.93.9**  "These strips of bark cloth tied high up—this must be a marked path made by Lakṣmaṇa who desired to travel in the dark hours."

**2.93.10**  "And here, on the mountain's flank, are the tracks of swift elephants with prominent tusks, that have roamed about trumpeting at one another."

**2.93.11**  "That smoke which the ascetics in the forest ever seek to kindle—look, there it rises densely along the well-worn path."

**2.93.12**  "Here I shall joyfully behold that tiger among men, that noble one who always honours his elders—Rāghava, like a great seer."

**2.93.13**  Then, having walked a short while, that Rāghava reached Citrakūṭa and came to the Mandākinī, and spoke these words to the people.

**2.93.14**  "That tiger among men who delights in the seat of heroes dwells on the ground; that lord of men has come to a desolate place—cursed be my birth and my very life!"

**2.93.15**  "On my account has the lord of the world, great in glory, fallen into calamity; relinquishing every pleasure, Rāghava dwells in the forest."

**2.93.16**  "Condemned thus by all the world, today, seeking to appease them, I shall fall again and again at the feet of Rāma and Sītā."

**2.93.17**  So lamenting in that forest, the son of Daśaratha then saw the great, holy, and beautiful leaf-cottage.

**2.93.18**  Covered with the many leaves of sāla, tāla, and aśvakarṇa trees, broad and spread with soft kuśa grass like a sacrificial altar at a ceremony.

**2.93.19**  Adorned with bows resembling Indra's rainbow, massive and heavy, with golden backs, of great substance, capable of afflicting enemies.

**2.93.20**  Graced by terrible arrows in the quivers, resembling the rays of the sun, it shone like Bhogavatī adorned with serpents of blazing visages.

**2.93.21**  Illumined by two great silver-hilted swords, and beautified also by two shields variegated with golden studs.

**2.93.22**  With bright finger-guards of iguana hide hung up, adorned with gold, impregnable to hosts of enemies—like a lion's cave to deer.

**2.93.23**  Bharata saw there in Rāma's dwelling a broad and sacred sacrificial altar with its fire blazing, with water flowing toward the northeast.

**2.93.24**  And having looked about for a moment, Bharata saw his elder—Rāma—seated in the cottage, wearing a coiled mass of matted locks.

**2.93.25**  He saw Rāma seated there, wearing a black antelope hide and garments of bark and cloth, luminous on every side like fire.

**2.93.26**  With lion's shoulders, mighty arms, eyes resembling the white lotus, righteous in conduct—the lord of the earth bounded by the ocean.

**2.93.27**  Seated, the mighty-armed, like the eternal Brahmā, on a ground strewn with darbha grass, together with Sītā and Lakṣmaṇa.

**2.93.28**  Seeing him, the illustrious Bharata, righteous in soul, the son of Kaikeyī, overwhelmed by grief and bewilderment, rushed forward.

**2.93.29**  And seeing him, he lamented in distress; with a voice choked by tears, unable to contain himself through any steadiness, he spoke these words.

**2.93.30**  "He who should be attended in the assembly by his subjects—that very elder brother of mine now sits attended by forest deer."

**2.93.31**  "That great-souled one who was accustomed to thousands of fine garments—he here wears only an antelope hide, practicing righteousness."

**2.93.32**  "He who once wore various splendid and beautiful flowers—how does this Rāghava now endure this burden of matted locks?"

**2.93.33**  "He whose accumulation of merit was joined through sacrifices performed as prescribed—that same one now pursues righteousness through the mortification born of bodily hardship."

**2.93.34**  "His body was anointed with precious sandalwood—how is this noble one's body now served by nothing but grime?"

**2.93.35**  "Rāma, who was born for happiness, has fallen into this suffering on my account. Cursed be the life of cruel me, condemned by all the world!"

**2.93.36**  Lamenting thus in his wretchedness, his lotus-face drenched in perspiration, Bharata fell weeping—before he could even reach Rāma's feet.

**2.93.37**  The mighty prince Bharata, tormented by grief, cried out once pitifully—"O noble one!"—and then could say nothing more.

**2.93.38**  His throat stopped up with tears, gazing upon the illustrious Rāma, he cried out only "O noble one!" and after that was unable to speak at all.

**2.93.39**  Śatrughna too, weeping, bowed at Rāma's feet. Rāma, embracing both of them, himself shed tears.

**2.93.40**  Then the two princes in that forest were joined by Sumantra and by Guha—as the sun and moon in the sky are joined by Venus and Bṛhaspati.

**2.93.41**  Seeing those princes gathered there in the great forest, mighty as leaders of elephant herds, the forest-dwellers too all shed tears, casting aside their joy.

**2.94.1**  Rāma the Rāghava, having smelt him on the crown of his head and embraced him, placed Bharata on his lap and then, composed, began to question him.

**2.94.2**  "Where then was your father, dear boy, that you have come to the forest? For as long as he lives, you have no right to come to the forest."

**2.94.3**  "Alas, after a long time I see Bharata arrived from afar, bearing a grim aspect here in this forest — why, dear boy, have you come to the forest?"

**2.94.4**  "Is King Daśaratha well — he of firm vows, performer of Rājasūya and Aśvamedha sacrifices, resolute in righteousness?"

**2.94.5**  "Is that learned brahmin, ever devoted to dharma, of great splendour, the preceptor of the Ikṣvākus — is he still duly honoured, dear boy?"

**2.94.6**  "Dear boy, is Kausalyā well, and Sumitrā the mother of good children — is the noble queen Kaikeyī also happy?"

**2.94.7**  "Is your family priest — a son of noble lineage, endowed with humility, widely learned, free from envy, a supervisor rather than a critic — duly honoured?"

**2.94.8**  "Is there one assigned to your sacred fires who knows the prescribed rites, is intelligent and upright, and always informs you in good time of what has been offered and what is yet to be offered?"

**2.94.9**  "Do you honour Sudhanvan as your instructor, dear boy — he who is endowed with excellent mastery of the bow and weapons and is learned in the science of polity?"

**2.94.10**  "Have you appointed as your ministers, dear boy, those who are your equals in spirit, who are brave, learned, with senses mastered, of noble birth, and skilled in reading signs and gestures?"

**2.94.11**  "For counsel is the root of victory for kings, O Rāghava, when it is well guarded by ministers who are keepers of secrets and versed in the śāstras."

**2.94.12**  "Do you not fall under the sway of sleep? Do you wake at the proper time? Do you, in the latter portions of the night, reflect on matters requiring skillful management?"

**2.94.13**  "Do you deliberate not alone, yet also not with too many? Does the counsel you have deliberated not run loose throughout the kingdom?"

**2.94.14**  "Having determined upon a course of action whose foundation is light but whose fruit is great, do you promptly set about executing it, without delay, O Rāghava?"

**2.94.15**  "Do only those who have done well — or at least those who have given the semblance of doing well — know all your affairs that are not to be transacted?"

**2.94.16**  "Is the counsel deliberated by you or by your ministers not inferred by those who are unnamed, through logic or other means, dear boy?"

**2.94.17**  "Do you desire one wise man over a thousand fools? For a wise man in difficult situations can bring about the highest good."

**2.94.18**  "Even if a king keeps thousands or indeed tens of thousands of fools in attendance, there is no help to be had from them."

**2.94.19**  "Even a single minister who is intelligent, brave, capable, and discerning can bring a king — or even one who is king in name only — to great prosperity."

**2.94.20**  "Are your servants employed in tasks befitting their grade — the foremost in foremost duties, the middling in middling ones, and the lowest in the lowest?"

**2.94.21**  "Do you appoint to the highest offices those ministers who have been tested by the fourfold temptations, who served your father and grandfather, who are pure, and who are the best?"

**2.94.22**  "Do officiating priests not disregard you as they would a fallen man, as women disdain one greedy to receive gifts and driven by desire?"

**2.94.23**  "A physician skilled in stratagem, one devoted to corrupting servants, and a brave man hungry for dominion — he who does not slay these three deserves to be slain."

**2.94.24**  "Have you appointed as your commander a man who is bold and brave, steadfast, wise, pure, of noble birth, loyal, and capable?"

**2.94.25**  "Are your principal warriors — powerful, skilled in battle, whose deeds have been witnessed, and of proven valour — honoured and esteemed by you?"

**2.94.26**  "Do you give your army their rations and pay, proper and befitting, when the time comes due, without delay?"

**2.94.27**  "For when the time for rations and pay is overstepped, retainers grow angry at their master and become corrupted — this is reckoned a very great calamity."

**2.94.28**  "Are all your principal men of noble birth devoted to you? Are they resolved to lay down their lives in your cause?"

**2.94.29**  "Have you appointed as your envoy, O Bharata, one who is learned in the ways of the country, astute, resourceful, who speaks according to instruction, and is a man of wisdom?"

**2.94.30**  "Do you know by spies, in threes, undetected, the eighteen vulnerable points in the enemy's realm, and the fifteen in your own?"

**2.94.31**  "Do you always deal firmly with enemies who have been expelled and who have returned, without despising them as weak, O slayer of foes?"

**2.94.32**  "Do you not frequent, dear boy, those brāhmaṇas who follow materialist philosophy? For these are childish men, skilled only in mischief, who fancy themselves wise."

**2.94.33**  "Though the foremost scriptures of dharma exist, these dull-witted men, having gotten hold of logic, speak fruitless words."

**2.94.34**  "Formerly occupied by the heroes of our ancestors, true to her name, with firm gates, filled with elephants, horses, and chariots —"

**2.94.35**  "— surrounded always by brāhmaṇas, kṣatriyas, and vaiśyas devoted to their own duties, with senses controlled, of great enterprise, and by thousands of ministers —"

**2.94.36**  "— encircled by palaces of varied forms, filled with physicians — do you protect Ayodhyā, prosperous and flourishing?"

**2.94.37**  "Frequented by hundreds of sacred trees, thronged with well-settled inhabitants, adorned with shrines, drinking-halls, and tanks —"
