**2.94.38**  "— whose men and women are joyful, beautified by festivals and assemblies, with well-tilled fields, rich in cattle, and free from acts of violence —"

**2.94.39**  "— not dependent on rain alone for water, charming, free from beasts of prey — does your flourishing realm dwell in happiness, O Rāghava?"

**2.94.40**  "Are all those who live by farming and cattle-keeping dear to you? Dear boy, it is by the people's livelihood that prosperity grows."

**2.94.41**  "Have you provided for their sustenance through measures of protection and relief? For all who dwell in the realm must by dharma be protected by the king."

**2.94.42**  "Do you conciliate women? Are they well guarded? Do you not trust them excessively? Do you not disclose secrets to them?"

**2.94.43**  "Is the elephant forest protected, and do you ensure the elephants are satisfied? Do you show yourself daily adorned to the people, rising early each morning on the royal highway?"

**2.94.44**  "Are all your fortresses filled with wealth, grain, weapons, water, engines of war, and also with craftsmen and archers?"

**2.94.45**  "Is your income large and your expenditure smaller? Does your treasury not go to those unworthy of it, O Rāghava?"

**2.94.46**  "Does your expenditure go toward the purposes of the gods, of the ancestors, toward brāhmaṇas and guests, toward warriors, and toward the company of friends?"

**2.94.47**  "Does a noble, pure-souled man, falsely accused of theft, go unquestioned by those skilled in the śāstras and remain unbound out of greed — remaining spotless?"

**2.94.48**  "When a thief is caught, questioned, and duly examined in due course with cause established — is he not released out of greed for money, O bull among men?"

**2.94.49**  "In a case of calamity affecting alike the wealthy and the destitute, O Rāghava, do your widely-learned ministers decide the matter without partiality?"

**2.94.50**  "The tears that fall from those falsely accused, O Rāghava, they destroy the sons and cattle of him who punishes out of partiality."

**2.94.51**  "Do you cherish the elderly, children, and chief physicians, O Rāghava, by these three means — gifts, thought, and word?"

**2.94.52**  "Do you pay homage to teachers, elders, ascetics, guests of the gods, all sacred shrines, and the accomplished brāhmaṇas?"

**2.94.53**  "Do you not obstruct dharma by means of wealth, nor wealth by means of dharma, nor both of them by pleasure born of desire and passion?"

**2.94.54**  "Having apportioned wealth, dharma, and pleasure each to its proper time, do you attend to all three, O Bharata, best of the victorious, you who know the right time?"

**2.94.55**  "Do the brāhmaṇas who are versed in all the meanings of the śāstras wish you well, O greatly wise one, together with the citizens and the people of the realm?"

**2.94.56**  "Impiety, falsehood, anger, negligence, procrastination, avoidance of those with wisdom, laziness, indulgence in the five sensory addictions —"

**2.94.57**  "— deliberating on affairs alone, taking counsel with those ignorant of consequences, failure to begin what has been resolved, and failure to guard secret counsel —"

**2.94.58**  "— neglect of auspicious rites, and failure to rise in greeting on all occasions — do you shun these fourteen faults of kings?"

**2.94.59**  "Do you not eat food prepared with fine flavours alone by yourself, O Rāghava? Do you give a share to your friends who are deserving?"

**2.95.1**  Having heard Rāma's words, Bharata replied: "What shall the dharma of kingship do for me who am bereft of dharma?"

**2.95.2**  "This is the eternal dharma ever established among us, O bull among men: while the eldest son lives, O king, a younger son cannot become king."

**2.95.3**  "Come back to Ayodhyā in her prosperity together with me, O Rāghava, and consecrate yourself as king for the welfare of this dynasty."

**2.95.4**  "They call a king human, yet in my estimation he is regarded as divine — he whose conduct is conjoined with dharma and artha is said to be more than human."

**2.95.5**  "While I was in the Kekaya country and you had resorted to the forest, the king — that sacrificer, honoured by the righteous — departed to heaven, O noble one."

**2.95.6**  "Rise up, O tiger among men; let the water-offering be made to father. Śatrughna and I have already performed ours."

**2.95.7**  "That which is given by one who is beloved is said to be inexhaustible in the world of the ancestors, O Rāghava, and you indeed are beloved of your father."

**2.95.8**  Hearing those piteous words spoken by Bharata that were connected with his father's death, the Rāghava lost consciousness.

**2.95.9**  Struck by the thunderbolt of those unwelcome words spoken by Bharata, that scorcher of foes Rāma threw up both his arms and fell to the ground like a tree in full bloom felled in the forest by an axe.

**2.95.10**  So Rāma, lord of the earth, lay fallen on the ground, like an elephant exhausted by the battering of a riverbank and lying asleep.

**2.95.11**  His brothers, weeping, together with the Vaidehi, sprinkled water on that great bowman who was worn thin by grief on every side.

**2.95.12**  Regaining consciousness, shedding tears from both his eyes, the Kākutstha began to speak piteously at length.

**2.95.13**  "What now is to be done for that great-souled man by me, ill-born as I am, who died of grief for me and was not even cremated by me?"

**2.95.14**  "Ah, Bharata has achieved his purpose — the king was honoured by you, O sinless one, and by Śatrughna in all the funerary rites."

**2.95.15**  "Even though my forest-dwelling is over, I cannot bring myself to go to Ayodhyā — bereft of its sovereign, leaderless, scattered in attention."

**2.95.16**  "When my period of forest-dwelling is over, O scorcher of foes, who will govern me again in Ayodhyā, now that father has gone to another world?"

**2.95.17**  "Those comforting words which father used to speak to me long ago, seeing my good conduct — whence shall I hear those words, so pleasing to the ear?"

**2.95.18**  Having spoken thus to Bharata, the Rāghava, scorched by grief, approached his wife whose face resembled the full moon and spoke to her.

**2.95.19**  "Sītā, your father-in-law is dead. You are bereft of your father, Lakṣmaṇa. Bharata brings word of sorrow: the lord of the earth has gone to heaven."

**2.95.20**  Having consoled Sītā, daughter of Janaka, who was weeping, Rāma — himself afflicted with grief — spoke these words of sorrow to Lakṣmaṇa there.

**2.95.21**  "Bring the piṇyāka paste of iṅgudi fruit, and fetch an upper garment of bark-cloth; for the sake of the water-offering for our great-souled father, I shall go."

**2.95.22**  "Let Sītā go ahead; you follow closely beside her; I shall go last — for this path is most terrible."

**2.95.23**  Then Suman­tra, their constant companion who had known their souls, who was of great wisdom, gentle, self-controlled, tranquil, and of firm devotion to Rāma —

**2.95.24**  — Sumantra, together with those princes, having comforted Rāghava, led him down, supporting him, to the auspicious river Mandākinī.

**2.95.25**  Those glorious ones then, with difficulty, approached the Mandākinī, beautiful river always attended by flowering groves, at a good ford.

**2.95.26**  Having reached its swift current at an auspicious and unmuddied ford, they offered the water to the king, saying, "Let this be for him."

**2.95.27**  The lord of the earth, cupping his hands filled with water, facing toward the south, weeping, spoke these words:

**2.95.28**  "O tiger among kings, may this pure, inexhaustible water, given by me today, reach you who have gone to the world of the ancestors."

**2.95.29**  Then the glorious Rāghava, ascending from the bank of the Mandākinī, performed together with his brothers the oblation of food for his father.

**2.95.30**  Rāma, deeply afflicted with grief, placed the piṇyāka paste mixed with jujube and iṅgudi upon a spread of darbha grass, and weeping, spoke these words:

**2.95.31**  "Eat this with pleasure, O great king — for what we eat is what we offer. Whatever food a man eats, his deities receive that same food."

**2.95.32**  Then by the same path, ascending from the riverbank, that tiger among men climbed the lovely ridge of the mountain.

**2.95.33**  Then the lord of the earth, reaching the doorway of the leaf-hut, took both Bharata and Lakṣmaṇa by the hands.

**2.95.34**  From the sound of those brothers weeping together with the Vaidehi, an echo arose on the mountain, like the roaring of lions.

**2.95.35**  Understanding the tumultuous sound, Bharata's soldiers were startled and said: "Surely Bharata has met with Rāma" — for that great sound was of those very men lamenting their dead father.

**2.95.36**  Then, abandoning their encampments, all of them with one mind rushed toward that sound, running as fast as their places allowed.

**2.95.37**  Some went on horses, some on elephants, some on well-adorned chariots; others, the tender-limbed, went on foot alone.

**2.95.38**  All the people, desiring to see Rāma — though he had been away only briefly, as if he had been long absent — rushed at once to the hermitage.

**2.95.39**  Hastening eagerly to witness the brothers' reunion, they went in vehicles of every kind, crowded with the sounds of hooves and wheels.

**2.95.40**  The earth, struck by the many vehicles with their hooves and wheels, gave forth a tumultuous sound, like the sky at the gathering of clouds.

**2.95.41**  Frightened by that sound, the elephants surrounded by their females, marking the air with their scent, departed to another forest.

**2.95.42**  Wild boars, deer, lions, buffaloes, bears and monkeys, tigers, elk, and gayal — all were frightened, along with spotted deer.

**2.95.43**  Cakravāka birds, water-cocks, geese, kāraṇḍava ducks, grebes, male cuckoos, and herons — bewildered — fled in all directions.

**2.95.44**  The sky filled with birds frightened by that sound; the earth was covered with human beings — both gleamed brilliantly at that time.

**2.95.45**  Beholding those men with eyes full of tears and deeply afflicted, he who knew dharma embraced them like a father and like a mother.

**2.95.46**  There he embraced some men, and some men greeted him in return; then that prince, having duly met all of them together with their companions and kinsmen, honoured them each according to their desert.

**2.95.47**  Then the sound of those great souls weeping, reverberating across the earth and through the sky, filling the caves of the mountains and all directions, was heard continually like the roar of a kettle drum.

**2.96.1**  Vasiṣṭha, placing the wives of Daśaratha before him, advanced toward that place, thirsting for the sight of Rāma.

**2.96.2**  The royal wives, going slowly toward the Mandākinī, saw there that ford which had been frequented by Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa.

**2.96.3**  Kausalyā, her face withered and filled with tears, spoke pitiably to Sumitrā and to the other royal women:

**2.96.4**  "This is the only ford in the forest — the one bathing place — of those who, though untiring in their deeds, are now left without a lord, stripped of their kingdom."

**2.96.5**  "From here, O Sumitrā, your son the son of Sumitrā — tireless — always fetches water himself for the sake of my son."

**2.96.6**  The wide-eyed queen saw on the ground, on darbha grass with tips pointing south, the piṇyāka paste of iṅgudi placed there as an offering to his father.

**2.96.7**  Beholding that offering placed on the ground in grief for his father by Rāma, Queen Kausalyā spoke to all the wives of Daśaratha:

**2.96.8**  "Behold — this is what has been duly given by the Rāghava to his father: the offering of the great-souled Rāghava, lord of the Ikṣvākus."

**2.96.9**  "For that great-souled king who was equal to the gods, who had enjoyed every pleasure — this, I think, is not a fitting meal."

**2.96.10**  "He who ruled the earth bounded by four seas, who was like Mahendra on earth — how is it that that lord of the earth now subsists on iṅgudi paste?"

**2.96.11**  "Nothing in the world appears more sorrowful to me than this — that prosperous Rāma should offer his father nothing but the expressed juice of iṅgudi."

**2.96.12**  "Having seen the iṅgudi paste given by Rāma as an offering to his father, how does my heart not burst into a thousand pieces from grief?"

**2.96.13**  Her co-wives then consoled her in her distress and went on, and they saw Rāma in the hermitage — like an immortal fallen from heaven.

**2.96.14**  Beholding Rāma abandoned by every luxury, the mothers, afflicted and worn thin by grief, shed tears with audible weeping.

**2.96.15**  Rāma, that tiger among men, of firm vows, rose and clasped the auspicious feet of all those mothers.

**2.96.16**  With their soft-touching, gentle-fingered, auspicious hands, those wide-eyed women wiped the dust from Rāma's back.

**2.96.17**  The son of Sumitrā too, gazing upon all those mothers with distress, greeted them devoutly and gently, following right after Rāma.

**2.96.18**  Just as they had behaved toward Rāma, so all the women conducted themselves toward him — toward Lakṣmaṇa of auspicious marks, born of Daśaratha.

**2.96.19**  Sītā too, grief-stricken, clasped the feet of those mothers-in-law, her eyes filled with tears, and stood before them.

**2.96.20**  Embracing her who was afflicted with grief, as a mother would embrace a daughter, Kausalyā spoke to the emaciated, forlorn Sītā grown thin by forest-dwelling:

**2.96.21**  "Daughter of the king of Videha, daughter-in-law of Daśaratha, wife of Rāma — how have you come to suffer so in this solitary forest?"

**2.96.22**  "Like a lotus scorched by the sun's heat, like a blue lotus withered, like gold obscured by dust, like the moon afflicted by clouds —"

**2.96.23**  "— beholding your face, O Vaidehi, grief burns me like fire consuming a dry abode, blazing fiercely in my heart, born from the kindling-sticks of calamity."

**2.96.24**  While his mother spoke thus in her distress, the elder brother of Bharata, the Rāghava, approached and clasped the feet of Vasiṣṭha.

**2.96.25**  Of that family priest who was equal to fire, that Bṛhaspati — grasping the feet of him whose splendour was fully ripened — the Rāghava sat down together with him, as Indra the lord of immortals would sit with Bṛhaspati.

**2.96.26**  Then righteous Bharata sat down behind, together with ministers, the foremost men of the city, soldiers, and the people who were most knowing in dharma, in the presence of his elder brother.

**2.96.27**  Having sat down, that valiant Bharata gazed at the Rāghava blazing with splendour even in his ascetic garb, and with hands folded in reverence — as Mahendra, purified, regards Prajāpati.

**2.96.28**  "What words will Bharata speak today to the Rāghava, having bowed to him and duly honoured him?" — such, in truth, was the keen and supreme curiosity of all the noble people gathered there at that moment.

**2.96.29**  Rāma the Rāghava, Lakṣmaṇa steadfast in truth, great-souled, and righteous Bharata — surrounded by their friends — shone like the three sacrificial fires accompanied by their priests at a Vedic rite.

**2.97.1**  Then Rāma, having comforted that brother who revered his elders, began, together with his brother Lakṣmaṇa, to question him.

**2.97.2**  "What is this that I wish to hear from your own lips—why have you come to this region dressed in bark-cloth, matted locks, and a black-antelope hide?"

**2.97.3**  "For what reason have you, wearing a black-antelope skin and matted hair, abandoned the kingdom and entered this forest? You should tell me everything."

**2.97.4**  Thus addressed by the great-souled Kākutstha, the son of Kaikeyī, clasping his hands firmly in reverence once more, spoke these words:

**2.97.5**  "The noble father, the long-armed one, abandoning virtue and performing a most terrible deed, has gone to heaven, tormented by grief for his son."

**2.97.6**  "Commanded by a woman—Kaikeyī, my own mother, O scorcher of foes—he committed this great sin that destroys one's own renown."

**2.97.7**  "She, having failed to reap the fruit of the kingdom, widowed and emaciated by grief, my mother will fall into a most dreadful hell."

**2.97.8**  "You should show grace to me who am your slave. Consecrate yourself today with the kingdom, as Maghavān [Indra] was consecrated."

**2.97.9**  "All these subjects, and those mothers who are now widowed, have come to your presence; you should show them your grace."

**2.97.10**  "It is both fitting by succession and fitting in yourself, O giver of honour—take the kingdom by dharma and fulfil the wishes of your friends."

**2.97.11**  "Let the whole earth be without widowhood, with you as its lord, as an autumnal night is [made full] by the unstained moon."

**2.97.12**  "Implored with bowed head by me, together with these ministers, you should show grace to a brother, a pupil, a slave."

**2.97.13**  "This entire council of ministers, our father's perpetual [retinue], is honoured, O tiger among men; I dare not transgress them."

**2.97.14**  Having spoken thus, Bharata, the long-armed son of Kaikeyī, with tears in his eyes, grasped Rāma's feet with his head [bowed] once more.

**2.97.15**  Embracing that brother Bharata, who was sighing again and again like a rutting elephant, Rāma spoke thus:

**2.97.16**  "How could a person like you—well-born, endowed with virtue, splendid, faithful to your vows—commit a sin for the sake of a kingdom?"

**2.97.17**  "I see not even the slightest fault in you, O slayer of enemies; nor indeed should you reproach your mother out of childish impulse."

**2.97.18**  "O knower of dharma, O best of those who bear dharma, the reverence owed to a mother honoured by the world is equal to that owed to a father."

**2.97.19**  "Being told by both of these two who are devoted to dharma—mother and father—'Go to the forest, O Rāghava,' how could I do otherwise?"

**2.97.20**  "The kingdom of Ayodhyā, honoured by the world, is to be received by you; the Daṇḍaka forest is to be dwelt in by me, clothed in bark."

**2.97.21**  "Having thus made this division and given these commands before the assembly of the world, the great-spirited, mighty king Daśaratha went to heaven."

**2.97.22**  "He, the dharma-souled king, teacher of the world, is the authority for you; you ought to enjoy the portion given by your father as is proper."

**2.97.23**  "For fourteen years, gentle one, dwelling in the Daṇḍaka forest, I shall enjoy the portion given to me by the great-souled father."

**2.97.24**  "What the great-souled father, honoured among men, comparable to the lord of the gods, said to me—that alone I consider to be the highest good of the soul, not the imperishable lordship over all the worlds."

**2.98.1**  Then, for those lions among men surrounded by their bands of friends, grieving all the while, the night passed in sorrow.

**2.98.2**  When the night had brightened into a fair dawn, those brothers, accompanied by their friends, having offered oblations and performed japa at the Mandākinī, approached Rāma.

**2.98.3**  They sat in silence; no one said anything. But Bharata, in the midst of his friends, spoke a reply to Rāma's words:

**2.98.4**  "My mother has been appeased; this kingdom was given to me. I give it back to you yourself—enjoy the kingdom free of thorns."

**2.98.5**  "Like a dam broken by the great force of water at the onset of rains, this great realm is impossible for anyone but you to hold."

**2.98.6**  "I have no power to follow your pace, O lord of the earth—as a donkey cannot match the pace of a horse, nor a common bird match Tārkṣya [Garuḍa]."

**2.98.7**  "O Rāma, easy indeed is the life of one who is always depended upon by others; but hard is the life of one who depends upon others."

**2.98.8**  "Just as a tree planted and nurtured by a man, having grown with a sturdy trunk into a great tree, becomes too tall for that same small man to climb—

**2.98.9**  "—and when that tree, having bloomed, yields him no fruit, he does not enjoy the delight for whose sake he caused it to flourish—

**2.98.10**  "—such is this simile, O long-armed one; you ought to understand its meaning: if you, the bull among men, the lord, do not govern us your servants, then indeed—"

**2.98.11**  "O great king, let the guilds and chief men everywhere behold you, standing in the kingdom blazing like the sun, O subduer of enemies."

**2.98.12**  "In your retinue, O Kākutstha, let intoxicated elephants trumpet; let the women of the inner apartments rejoice with great composure."

**2.98.13**  Having heard Bharata's words, the townspeople and various other folk thought them good and joined in imploring Rāma.

**2.98.14**  Seeing that illustrious one thus afflicted and lamenting, Rāma, self-mastered and resolved in spirit, comforted Bharata.

**2.98.15**  "This person is not lord of himself; he has no power to act at will. Fate drags him this way and that."

**2.98.16**  "All accumulations end in dissolution; all that rises ends in a fall; all unions end in separation; life ends in death."

**2.98.17**  "As for ripe fruits there is no fear other than falling, so for a man who is born there is no fear other than death."

**2.98.18**  "Just as a house with firm pillars, having grown old, crumbles down, so men crumble, having fallen under the power of old age and death."

**2.98.19**  "The days and nights pass for all living beings here, swiftly consuming their life-spans, as the sun's rays consume water in summer."

**2.98.20**  "Grieve for yourself—why do you grieve for another? Your life-span diminishes whether you stand still or move on."

**2.98.21**  "Death walks together with one; death sits down together; having gone a very long road, death turns back together with one."

**2.98.22**  "Wrinkles have come upon the limbs, and the hair has turned white; the man, worn out by old age—what great deed can he now accomplish?"

**2.98.23**  "Men rejoice at the sun's rising and rejoice at the sun's setting, yet they do not perceive the waning of their own life."

**2.98.24**  "They delight upon seeing the new, ever-fresh turn of seasons arrive here; yet with the revolution of the seasons, the life-breath of living beings is eroded."

**2.98.25**  "As two pieces of driftwood come together on the great ocean, and then, at the appointed time, drift apart again—

**2.98.26**  "—so wives and sons and kinsmen and possessions come together and scatter apart; their separation is certain."

**2.98.27**  "Here no living being abides as one might wish; therefore there is no power in lamenting for one who has departed."

**2.98.28**  "Just as someone standing on the road might say to a caravan setting out, 'I too shall come after you'—

**2.98.29**  "—so this road, trodden by those who went before us, the road of fathers and grandfathers, is certain. Having entered upon it—how should one grieve over what admits of no deviation?"

**2.98.30**  "When age is falling away like a stream that does not turn back, the self must be employed in what is good; creatures are said to be partakers of happiness."

**2.98.31**  "Our father, that dharma-souled lord of the earth, having performed all the auspicious sacrifices with full dakṣiṇā offerings and shaken off his sins, has gone to heaven."

**2.98.32**  "Through properly supporting his servants, through protecting his subjects, and through righteous acquisition of wealth, our father has gone to the three heavens."

**2.98.33**  "Having worshipped with many kinds of sacrifice, having obtained abundant enjoyments, and having attained the utmost span of life, the lord of the earth has gone to heaven."

**2.98.34**  "Our father, having abandoned the worn-out human body, has attained divine prosperity—that which courses through the world of Brahmā."

**2.98.35**  "No wise person of such qualities as his ought to grieve for him—and still less someone like you, who is learned and endowed with greater intelligence."

**2.98.36**  "These manifold griefs, lamentations, and weeping are indeed to be avoided by the steadfast and wise man in all circumstances."

**2.98.37**  "Therefore be at peace, do not grieve; go and dwell in that city. You have been commanded thus by the self-controlled father, O best of those who speak."

**2.98.38**  "And where I too have been commanded by that same one of meritorious deeds, there I shall carry out the command of the noble father."

**2.98.39**  "It is not right for me to abandon his command, O subduer of enemies; and it must always be honoured by you as well—he is truly our kinsman, he is our father."

**2.98.40**  When Rāma had spoken thus and fell silent, his meaningful words concluded, the righteous Bharata spoke a remarkable, righteous speech:

**2.98.41**  "Who in this world could be like you, O subduer of enemies? Neither sorrow disturbs you, nor does joy elate you."

**2.98.42**  "You are honoured by the elders, and you seek out their counsel in matters of doubt. In death as in life, in adversity as in prosperity—you are the same."

**2.98.43**  "One who has attained such wisdom—by what could he be tormented? Having encountered adversity thus, he ought not to be dejected."

**2.98.44**  "You are of a nature comparable to an immortal, O great-souled one, true to your vows, all-knowing, all-seeing, and wise, O Rāghava."

**2.98.45**  "Unbearable suffering ought not to befall you who are endowed with such qualities and who are skilled in understanding rise and fall."

**2.98.46**  "The sinful act committed by my petty mother on my account in my absence—that unwanted deed: may you show grace to me regarding it."

**2.98.47**  "I am bound by the bond of dharma, and therefore I do not slay this mother of mine here with severe punishment, though she who committed the sin deserves punishment."

**2.98.48**  "How could I, born of Daśaratha of pure lineage and deeds, knowing dharma, perform a most unrighteous and reprehensible act?"

**2.98.49**  "I would not censure our father in any assembly—he was an elder, a man of sacred rites, aged, a departed king, a father, a deity."

**2.98.50**  "O knower of dharma, O one who understands dharma—who, desiring to please a woman, would perform such a sinful deed devoid of dharma and righteous purpose?"

**2.98.51**  "The ancient saying is that creatures become bewildered at the time of death; through the king's acting thus, that saying has been made manifest in the world."

**2.98.52**  "Having regard for a good purpose, may you reverse and undo what our father transgressed out of anger, delusion, and rashness."

**2.98.53**  "A son who regards his father's transgression as a proper thing—that is considered an offspring in the eyes of the world; otherwise the opposite is true."

**2.98.54**  "May you be that offspring; do not ratify the misdeed of your father—that act, censured by the wise in this world."

**2.98.55**  "May you deliver Kaikeyī, and me, and our father, and our friends and kinsmen, and all the townspeople and country-dwellers—may you deliver all this."

**2.98.56**  "What place does the forest have, and what place does kṣatriya duty have? What place do matted locks have, and what place does governance have? You ought not to perform this contradictory action."

**2.98.57**  "But if you wish to practise dharma arising from hardship, then endure hardship by protecting the four varṇas through dharma."

**2.98.58**  "Those who know dharma declare the householder's stage to be the best among the four stages of life, O knower of dharma—how can you abandon it?"

**2.98.59**  "I am a child compared to you in learning, in position, and in birth. How shall I protect the earth while you stand by?"

**2.98.60**  "I am a child of inferior intelligence and virtue, inferior in position; and being without you, I dare not maintain myself."

**2.98.61**  "Govern this entire, undivided, thorn-free paternal kingdom, O knower of dharma, by your own dharma, together with your kinsmen."

**2.98.62**  "Let the officiating priests, including Vasiṣṭha, skilled in sacred formulas, consecrate you here, O knower of dharma, together with your kinsmen, with full ritual."

**2.98.63**  "Consecrated by us, go to Ayodhyā to govern it; having swiftly conquered the worlds, as Vāsava [Indra] with the Maruts."

**2.98.64**  "Discharging the three debts, thoroughly burning away enemies, satisfying friends with desires—you alone govern, and govern me here."

**2.98.65**  "Today, O noble one, let your friends rejoice at your consecration; today let your enemies, afraid, guard the ten directions."

**2.98.66**  "Wiping away the reproach of my mother, O bull among men, protect yourself and our father today from sin."

**2.98.67**  "With bowed head I beseech you: show compassion to me and to all kinsmen, as the great lord [Śiva] shows it to all beings."

**2.98.68**  "Or else, if you shall go from here to the forest, turning your back on all this, I too shall go together with you."

**2.98.69**  Even so, Rāma, that lord of the earth, though earnestly implored by Bharata prostrating himself, did not form any intention of returning; steadfast in spirit, he stood firm in the command of his father.

**2.98.70**  Beholding that extraordinary steadfastness in Rāghava, the people at once felt joy, yet being afflicted felt sorrow—sorrow that he would not go to Ayodhyā, yet joy upon seeing his firm resolution.

**2.98.71**  The officiating priests and the beloved leaders of the townspeople's companies, and the mothers, senseless with tears, praised Bharata as he spoke thus, and together bowed to Rāma and joined in imploring him.

**2.99.1**  Then the elder brother of Lakṣmaṇa, the illustrious one greatly honoured in the midst of his kinsmen, replied to Bharata who was speaking thus once more:

**2.99.2**  "This speech that you have delivered is fitting for one born a son of Daśaratha, the best of kings, upon Kaikeyī."

**2.99.3**  "Long ago, brother, our father, when taking your mother as wife, promised your maternal grandfather an unequalled bride-price of a kingdom."

**2.99.4**  "And in the war of gods and demons, the lord king, greatly pleased and gratified, granted your mother a boon."

**2.99.5**  "Then your illustrious mother, having kept that boon in reserve, asked the best of men for two boons—she of excellent complexion."

**2.99.6**  "The kingdom for you, O tiger among men, and my banishment—and the king, bound by his word, granted her that boon accordingly."

**2.99.7**  "By that father, O bull among men, I too have been commanded here—as the gift of the boon—to dwell in the forest for fourteen years."

**2.99.8**  "So I have come to this solitary forest, accompanied by Lakṣmaṇa and Sītā, unrivalled in this, standing firm in the true word of my father."

**2.99.9**  "You likewise ought to make the king, that lord of kings who was true to his word, [come true in his word] swiftly through your consecration."

**2.99.10**  "Free the king, the lord, from debt on my behalf, O Bharata; save our father, O knower of dharma, and honour our mother."

**2.99.11**  "For it is heard, dear one, that long ago a glorious sacred text was sung by the sacrificer Gaya himself at Gayā, concerning the fathers:

**2.99.12**  "'Because a son delivers his father from the hell called Puṃ, therefore he is called putra [son]; or because a son protects the fathers.'"

**2.99.13**  "One should desire many sons, virtuous and learned; among them assembled together, may at least one go to Gayā."

**2.99.14**  "Thus all the royal seers were convinced, O delight of the king's line; therefore save our father from hell, O best of men, O lord."

**2.99.15**  "Go to Ayodhyā, Bharata, please the subjects, O hero, together with Śatrughna and all the twice-born."

**2.99.16**  "And I too shall enter the Daṇḍaka forest without delay, O king, accompanied by these two—Vaidehī and Lakṣmaṇa."

**2.99.17**  "You, Bharata, be king of men yourself; I too shall be king of the forest deer. Go to the best of cities today with joy; and I too, filled with joy, shall enter the Daṇḍakas."

**2.99.18**  "Let the royal parasol, warding off the sun's rays, make cool shade upon your head, O Bharata; and I too shall happily take refuge in that surpassing cool shade of the forest trees."

**2.99.19**  "Śatrughna of auspicious intelligence is your helper; Saumitri is known as my chief companion. We four, noble sons, O Bharata, serve the king who stood firm in truth—do not despair."

**2.100.1**  While Rāma was consoling Bharata, Jābāli, the most excellent of brāhmaṇas, spoke to Rāma who was learned in dharma — these words that were devoid of dharma:

**2.100.2**  "Well then, O Rāghava, let not this purposeless resolve be yours — this resolve that belongs to a common man, not to one of noble understanding and austere mind.

**2.100.3**  Who is any man's kinsman? What does one obtain from another? A being is born alone and perishes alone.

**2.100.4**  Therefore, O Rāma, the man who clings to 'mother' and 'father' should be reckoned as if deranged — there is truly no one who belongs to anyone.

**2.100.5**  Just as a man travelling to another village lodges somewhere for a night and abandons that lodging to continue his journey the next day —

**2.100.6**  So too, O Kākutstha, a father, mother, home, and wealth are for men merely a transient lodging — the wise do not cling to them.

**2.100.7**  Abandoning the paternal kingdom, O foremost of men, he is not worthy to tread this painful, difficult, and thorny wrong path.

**2.100.8**  Consecrate yourself king in the prosperous city of Ayodhyā — the city awaits you now, wearing a single braid of mourning.

**2.100.9**  Enjoying the great royal pleasures befitting a king's son, sport in Ayodhyā as Śakra sports in heaven.

**2.100.10**  Daśaratha was nothing to you, nor were you anything to him — he was one king, you are another. Therefore do as I say.

**2.100.11**  That king has gone where all must go — such is the course of mortals. But you are afflicting yourself in vain.

**2.100.12**  Those who are devoted to artha and dharma — those I pity, not the others. For they attain suffering here and destruction in the next world.

**2.100.13**  People have spread the notion that the aṣṭakā rite is sacred to the ancestors — look at the waste of food! For what will a dead man eat?

**2.100.14**  If what is eaten here by one person could pass to another's body, one might give śrāddha to a traveller — but that food would not reach him on the road.

**2.100.15**  These texts saying 'sacrifice, give, consecrate yourself, perform austerities, renounce' were composed by clever men to entice people to give.

**2.100.16**  Make up your mind, O great intellect, that there is no other world. Rely on what is perceptible; put the imperceptible behind you.

**2.100.17**  Heeding the counsel of the wise, which illuminates all the world, accept the kingdom, appeased by Bharata's entreaty."

**2.101.1**  Having heard Jābāli's words, Rāma — foremost among men of truthful nature — replied with great reason, employing his own unclouded judgement:

**2.101.2**  "What you have said here with the intent of doing me good — that which is improper yet looks proper, that which is unwholesome yet seems wholesome —

**2.101.3**  A man who is without limits, given to wicked conduct, and seen to have broken character — he wins no esteem among the virtuous.

**2.101.4**  Whether a man is of good lineage or bad, a hero or a braggart — it is character alone that declares him pure or impure.

**2.101.5**  An ignoble man may appear noble; one fallen from purity may appear pure; a man without good marks may look auspicious; a man of bad character may seem virtuous.

**2.101.6**  If I were to embrace this adharma dressed in the garb of dharma — this confusion of the world's order, stripped of proper rite and conducive of no good —

**2.101.7**  What discerning man, skilled in distinguishing right action from wrong, would think highly of me in the world — me, who had become wicked and a corrupter of the world?

**2.101.8**  Whose conduct could I follow? How would I attain heaven, living by this mode of life and having forfeited my vow?

**2.101.9**  This entire world conducts itself by imitating those who act as they wish. As kings behave, so do their subjects.

**2.101.10**  Truth and freedom from cruelty — this is the eternal conduct of kings. Therefore kingship is grounded in truth, and the world is established in truth.

**2.101.11**  The sages and the gods have held truth alone to be supreme. In this world, one who speaks truth attains the highest abode.

**2.101.12**  Men recoil from a liar just as from a serpent. Truth is the supreme dharma in the world; it is said to be the very root of heaven.

**2.101.13**  Truth alone is the sovereign of the world; upon truth does Lakṣmī herself rest. All things are rooted in truth; there is no station higher than truth.

**2.101.14**  Gifts given, sacrifices performed, oblations offered, austerities endured, and the Vedas — all are established in truth. Therefore one should be devoted to truth.

**2.101.15**  One man sustains an entire world; one man upholds a family. One man sinks into hell; one man is exalted in heaven.

**2.101.16**  Why then should I not honour my father's command? — I, who am pledged to truth, who ratified that pledge by truth itself with truth.

**2.101.17**  Neither from greed, nor from delusion, nor from ignorance shrouded in darkness will I break the embankment of truth — I who have pledged truth to my father.

**2.101.18**  We have heard it said that neither gods nor ancestors accept the offerings of one who has bound himself to falsehood, who is fickle, and whose mind is unsteady.

**2.101.19**  This dharma — truth — I myself perceive as turning toward the inner self. That burden trodden by virtuous men is to be embraced for its own sake.

**2.101.20**  I will not abandon the kṣatriya dharma to take up adharma that merely resembles dharma — that which is practised by the base, the cruel, the greedy, and the wicked.

**2.101.21**  One commits sin with the body, having deliberated with the mind, and speaks untruth with the tongue — these three are the sinful deeds of the three faculties.

**2.101.22**  The earth, fame, glory, and Lakṣmī seek out a man of truth; even those dwelling in heaven follow him. Therefore one should adhere to truth alone.

**2.101.23**  For what you said to me, urging me with seemingly reasoned words — 'do this good thing' — would have been the most ignoble course.

**2.101.24**  How could I, having vowed this forest-dwelling to my father, do Bharata's bidding by abandoning my father's word?

**2.101.25**  That vow was firmly made by me in my father's presence, and at that moment Queen Kaikeyī was filled with joy.

**2.101.26**  Living thus in the forest, pure, eating with restraint, satisfying the ancestors and gods with holy roots, flowers, and fruits —

**2.101.27**  — I, with the five senses fully content, set forth on life's journey without deceit, full of faith, discerning what ought and ought not to be done.

**2.101.28**  Having attained this field of action, one must perform whatever auspicious action is to be done. Fire, wind, and the moon share in the fruits of one's deeds.

**2.101.29**  The king of the gods ascended to heaven by performing a hundred sacrifices; and the great seers attained heaven by undertaking fierce austerities.

**2.101.30**  Truth, dharma, valour, compassion for all beings, gentle speech, and the honouring of brāhmaṇas, gods, and guests — the virtuous declare these to be the path to the three heavens.

**2.101.31**  Those sages who delight in dharma, who associate with the virtuous, who are full of lustre, foremost in the virtue of giving, non-violent, and free from stain — they are worshipped as the foremost in the world.

**2.102.1**  Perceiving that Rāma was angered, Vasiṣṭha replied: "Jābāli too understands the comings and goings of this world — he spoke these words only wishing to turn you back."

**2.102.2**  "Hear from me, O lord of the world, the origin of this world. All was water in the beginning; from it the earth was fashioned. Then Brahmā, the self-born, arose together with the gods.

**2.102.3**  He then became a boar and lifted up the earth; and with his sons of purified souls he created the entire world.

**2.102.4**  Brahmā, born of space, is eternal, constant, and imperishable. From him was born Marīci; from Marīci, Kaśyapa was born as a son.

**2.102.5**  From Kaśyapa was born Vivasvān; from Vivasvān came Manu, known as Vaivasvata. He was the first Prajāpati; from Manu came Ikṣvāku as a son.

**2.102.6**  Know that Ikṣvāku, to whom this prosperous earth was first given by Manu, was the primordial king in Ayodhyā.

**2.102.7**  The glorious son of Ikṣvāku was known as Kukṣi; from Kukṣi was born the heroic son Vikukṣi.

**2.102.8**  From Vikukṣi came the greatly lustrous and valiant son Bāṇa; from Bāṇa came the long-armed Anaraṇya of great renown.

**2.102.9**  During the reign of the great king Anaraṇya, O best of the virtuous, there was neither erratic rainfall nor famine, nor any thief whatsoever.

**2.102.10**  From Anaraṇya came King Pṛthu of mighty arms; from that great king Pṛthu came Triśaṅku — who, heroic by virtue of his truthful word, ascended to heaven in his own body.

**2.102.11**  Triśaṅku's son was the greatly renowned Dhundhumāra; from Dhundhumāra was born the greatly lustrous Yuvanāśva.

**2.102.12**  From Yuvanāśva was born the glorious son Māndhātā; from the greatly lustrous Māndhātā came Susaṃdhi.

**2.102.13**  Susaṃdhi had two sons: Dhruvasaṃdhi and Prasenajit. From the glorious Dhruvasaṃdhi came Bharata, destroyer of enemies.

**2.102.14**  From the long-armed Bharata was born one named Asita, against whom arose these rival kings as enemies: the Haihayas, the Tālajaṅghas, the Śūras, and the Śaśabindus.

**2.102.15**  Having arrayed against all of them in battle, the king was driven into exile; he dwelt as an ascetic, content on a beautiful mountain. Both his wives were pregnant, so tradition relates.

**2.102.16**  A Bhārgava named Cyavana dwelt on the Himālayas. Kālindī approached that sage and saluted him.

**2.102.17**  The brahmin blessed her, she who was hoping for the boon of a son's birth. Then that queen returned home and gave birth to a son.

**2.102.18**  But by her co-wife, poison had been given to her, intending to destroy the foetus. Born together with that poison, he became known as Sagara.

**2.102.19**  That king, named Sagara, had the ocean dug; he performed sacrifice with intensity, terrifying these subjects.

**2.102.20**  Sagara's son, so we have heard, was Asamañjas — a doer of wicked deeds, banished by his father while still living.

**2.102.21**  The valiant son of Asamañjas was Aṃśumān; the son of Aṃśumān was Dilīpa; from Dilīpa came Bhagīratha.

**2.102.22**  From Bhagīratha came Kakutstha, from whom the Kākutsthas are remembered; the son of Kakutstha was Raghu, from whom the Rāghavas take their name.

**2.102.23**  The lustrous son of Raghu, grown great and given to devouring men, was known on earth as Kalmāṣapāda or Saudāsa.

**2.102.24**  Kalmāṣapāda's son was known as Śaṅkhaṇa; possessing that valour, Sahasena came to ruin.

**2.102.25**  The son of Śaṅkhaṇa was the heroic and glorious Sudarśana; from Sudarśana came Agnivarṇa; from Agnivarṣa came Śīghraga.

**2.102.26**  The son of Śīghraga was Maru; the son of Maru was Praśuśruka; the son of Praśuśruka was the greatly radiant Ambarīṣa.

**2.102.27**  Ambarīṣa's son was Nahuṣa, of true valour; and the son of Nahuṣa was Nābhāga, supremely righteous.

**2.102.28**  Nābhāga had two sons: Aja and Suvrata; and the righteous king Daśaratha was the son of Aja.

**2.102.29**  You are his eldest heir, O Rāma, widely proclaimed as such. Therefore receive your own kingdom and look after the world, O king.

**2.102.30**  For among all the Ikṣvākus the firstborn becomes king; when the elder is living, no younger son is consecrated in the kingdom — the eldest is installed.

**2.102.31**  Therefore, O man of great glory, you should not today abandon the eternal family dharma of the Rāghavas and of yourself — rule this earth, rich in jewels and rich in kingdoms, as your father did."

**2.103.1**  Then Vasiṣṭha, the royal chaplain, having spoken to Rāma, spoke again further words joined with righteousness.

**2.103.2**  For a man born into this world there are three teachers, O Kākutstha — the preceptor, the father, and the mother, O Rāghava.

**2.103.3**  The father begets a man, O bull among men, while the preceptor bestows wisdom — therefore he is called a guru.

**2.103.4**  I am your father's preceptor and yours as well, O scorcher of foes; by acting on my words you shall not transgress the path of the righteous.

**2.103.5**  These assemblies and guilds are gathered here before you; by practicing righteousness among them, dear child, you shall not transgress the path of the good.

**2.103.6**  You ought not to disobey your aged and virtuous mother; by acting on her word you shall not transgress the path of the righteous.

**2.103.7**  By acting on the words of Bharata who beseeches you, O Rāghava, you shall not transgress yourself, O you of valor in truth and righteousness.

**2.103.8**  Thus sweetly addressed by his guru, Rāghava himself, that bull among men, replied to Vasiṣṭha who was seated before him.

**2.103.9**  What mother and father constantly do for a child — what has been done by mother and by father — that is not easily requited.

**2.103.10**  By giving according to their ability, by bathing and clothing, by constant affectionate speech, and by fostering growth —

**2.103.11**  For king Daśaratha who begot me is my father; what I have been commanded by him shall not prove false.

**2.103.12**  Thus addressed by Rāma, Bharata, most noble but deeply distressed, immediately spoke to the charioteer.

**2.103.13**  "Spread kuśa grass for me quickly on this bare ground, charioteer — I shall lie down here facing my elder brother until he relents toward me."

**2.103.14**  "Without food, without seeking shelter, without wealth, like a twice-born ascetic, I shall lie before this hermitage until he returns."

**2.103.15**  Sorrowful, seeing Sumatra looking toward Rāma, he had the kuśa grass spread and himself laid it out on the ground.

**2.103.16**  Then Rāma of great splendor, the foremost of royal sages, spoke to him: "Why, dear Bharata, do you lie down here facing me in this manner?"

**2.103.17**  "A brahmin may lie on one side to press men by this rite, but the practice of lying prostrate before the consecrated is not proper for them."

**2.103.18**  "Rise, O tiger among men; abandon this harsh vow; return hence quickly to Ayodhyā, foremost of cities, O Rāghava."

**2.103.19**  But Bharata, remaining seated, looked all around at the townspeople and country folk and said: "Why do you not instruct my elder brother?"

**2.103.20**  The townspeople and country folk said to that great-souled one: "We acknowledge the Kākutstha speaks rightly and well."

**2.103.21**  "This illustrious one himself abides in his father's word; therefore we are not able to turn him away from it forthwith."

**2.103.22**  Understanding the words of those people, Rāma spoke: "Hear thus the counsel of friends who see through the eye of righteousness."

**2.103.23**  "Having heard both these things and considered rightly, O Rāghava, rise, O mighty-armed one, and touch water with me."

**2.103.24**  Then, having risen and touched water, Bharata spoke these words: "Let the assemblies, ministers, and guilds listen to me."

**2.103.25**  "I did not beg my father for the kingdom, nor do I instruct my mother; I acknowledge my elder brother Rāghava as supremely versed in righteousness."

**2.103.26**  "But if the forest must indeed be dwelt in and the father's command must be fulfilled, then I myself will live in the forest for fourteen years."

**2.103.27**  The righteous-souled Rāma, astonished by his brother's truthful speech, looked at the townspeople and country folk and spoke.

**2.103.28**  "What was pledged, what was entrusted, what was granted by my father while he lived — neither I nor Bharata is able to annul that."

**2.103.29**  "I should not resort to deceit in my forest dwelling, which would be contemptible; what was said by Kaikeyī was fitting, and my father did what was well done."

**2.103.30**  "I know Bharata to be forbearing and devoted to the honor of his elders; all good resides in this great-souled one who is steadfast in truth."

**2.103.31**  "Returned from the forest, I shall be the supreme lord of the earth together with this dharma-natured brother."

**2.103.32**  "The king was implored by Kaikeyī; I have fulfilled that command — by this I shall free that lord of the earth, my father, from untruth."

**2.104.1**  The great sages assembled there, struck with wonder, watched the hair-raising meeting of those two brothers of incomparable splendor.

**2.104.2**  The invisible hosts of sages and perfected beings, supreme seers, praised those two great-souled Kākutstha brothers.

**2.104.3**  "Blessed is he whose two sons are both learned in righteousness and heroic in virtue; having heard the exchange of both, we are filled with longing."

**2.104.4**  Then the assembled sages who desired the slaying of Daśagrīva quickly spoke these words to Bharata, that tiger among kings.

**2.104.5**  "Born of a noble lineage, O greatly wise one, of great conduct and great fame — you should accept Rāma's word, if you regard your father."

**2.104.6**  "We wish this Rāma to be forever free of debt to his father; and through freedom from debt to Kaikeyī, Daśaratha has gone to heaven."

**2.104.7**  Having spoken these words, the gandharvas together with the sages and all the royal sages departed each to his own destination.

**2.104.8**  Gladdened by those auspicious words, Rāma of auspicious aspect, his face lit with joy, paid honor to those sages.

**2.104.9**  But Bharata, his body slack, his voice faltering, with palms joined, spoke again these words to Rāghava.

**2.104.10**  "Considering the duty of a king and the succession of the family's dharma, you ought to fulfill the entreaty of me and of my mother, O Kākutstha."

**2.104.11**  "I alone do not venture to protect this vast kingdom, nor to delight the townspeople and countryfolk who are devoted to you."

**2.104.12**  "Our kinsmen, warriors, allies, and friends await you alone, as farmers await the rain cloud."

**2.104.13**  "Accept and establish this kingdom, O greatly wise one; you are capable, O Kākutstha, of protecting the world."

**2.104.14**  Having spoken thus, Bharata fell then at his brother's feet, earnestly entreating Rāma with these affectionate words.

**2.104.15**  Taking his brother upon his lap, Rāma himself spoke, dark-complexioned, lotus-petal-eyed, his voice like that of a lordly swan.

**2.104.16**  "This resolve that has come to you is your own and born of proper training, by which you venture so greatly, dear one, to protect the earth."

**2.104.17**  "Together with ministers, friends, and wise counselors, deliberating on all matters, have even the greatest affairs carried out."

**2.104.18**  "Lakṣmī might depart from the moon, or the Himālaya might give up its snow, or the ocean might overflow its shore — I shall not break my father's vow."

**2.104.19**  "Whether done out of desire or greed by your mother for your sake, dear one, you should not take that to heart; treat her as a mother."

**2.104.20**  As he spoke thus, Bharata addressed the son of Kausalyā, who was resplendent as the sun, whose appearance was like the crescent moon.

**2.104.21**  "Have these golden-adorned sandals mounted upon your feet; these will ensure the welfare and security of all the world."

**2.104.22**  Then that tiger among men of very great splendor mounted the sandals and then dismounted them, and presented them to the great-souled Bharata.

**2.104.23**  The valorous Bharata, knower of dharma, reverently received those well-adorned sandals, circumambulated Rāghava, and then placed them on the head of the finest elephant.

**2.104.24**  Then, having honored in due order the people, the elders, ministers and nobles, and his two younger brothers, he who advances the Rāghava lineage dismissed them, himself standing firm in his own dharma, immovable as the Himālaya.

**2.104.25**  His mothers, their throats choked with tears, could not address him out of grief; but he, having saluted all the mothers, weeping, entered his own hut.

**2.105.1**  Then Bharata, having placed the sandals upon his head, joyfully mounted his chariot accompanied by Śatrughna.

**2.105.2**  Vasiṣṭha, Vāmadeva, and Jābāli of firm vows went ahead, all the ministers honored with sacred formulas.

**2.105.3**  Facing eastward, they went along the lovely river Mandākinī, circumambulating the great mountain Citrakūṭa.

**2.105.4**  Beholding thousands of beautiful and various minerals and ores, Bharata then proceeded along its flanks with his army.

**2.105.5**  Not far from Citrakūṭa, Bharata then saw the hermitage where the sage Bharadvāja had made his dwelling.

**2.105.6**  That wise delight of his lineage, having reached Bharadvāja's hermitage, descended from his chariot and venerated his feet.

**2.105.7**  Then Bharadvāja, pleased, spoke to Bharata: "Has the purpose been accomplished, dear child — have you met with Rāma?"

**2.105.8**  Thus addressed by the wise Bharadvāja, Bharata, who cherished righteousness, replied to Bharadvāja.

**2.105.9**  "He of firm valor, Rāghava, when entreated by the guru and by me, being greatly pleased, spoke these words to Vasiṣṭha:"

**2.105.10**  "'I shall truly uphold that very vow of my father — the vow my father made — for fourteen years.'"

**2.105.11**  Thus addressed, the greatly wise Vasiṣṭha, versed in speech, replied with a great utterance to the speech-skilled Rāghava.

**2.105.12**  "'Present with joy these golden-adorned sandals, O greatly wise one; they shall ensure your welfare and security in Ayodhyā.'"

**2.105.13**  "Thus addressed by Vasiṣṭha, Rāghava stood facing east and gave those golden sandals to me for my kingdom."

**2.105.14**  "'I have turned back, having been permitted by Rāma of very great soul; I go to Ayodhyā itself, bearing these auspicious sandals.'"

**2.105.15**  Having heard this auspicious speech of the great-souled Bharata, the sage Bharadvāja pronounced a still more auspicious speech.

**2.105.16**  "This is no wonder, O tiger among men, O best of those of noble character and conduct, that nobility should rest in you as water flows to a hollow."

**2.105.17**  "Immortal is that long-armed father of yours, Daśaratha, whose son you are — such a righteous soul, devoted to dharma."

**2.105.18**  Having been spoken to by that great-souled sage, Bharata with palms joined and clasping his feet began to take leave of him.

**2.105.19**  Then, having circumambulated Bharadvāja again and again, the illustrious Bharata departed for Ayodhyā together with his ministers.

**2.105.20**  That vast army following Bharata, with vehicles, carts, horses, and elephants, turned back once more.

**2.105.21**  Then, having crossed the divine river Yamunā garlanded with waves, they all beheld once again the river Gaṅgā with her auspicious waters.

**2.105.22**  Having crossed that lovely river full of delightful water, he entered, together with his kinsmen and army, the beautiful town of Śṛṅgaverapura.

**2.105.23**  From Śṛṅgaverapura, Bharata then saw Ayodhyā again; scorched with grief, he spoke these words to his charioteer.

**2.105.24**  "Charioteer, see — Ayodhyā is destroyed, it does not shine; it is without beauty, without joy, wretched, its sounds muffled and silent."

**2.106.1**  The lord Bharata of great renown quickly entered Ayodhyā riding in a chariot whose sound was deep and resonant.

**2.106.2**  He found it as a dark, lightless night, haunted by cats and owls, its people and elephants cowering — struck through with gloom.

**2.106.3**  Like Rohiṇī, the beloved wife of the enemy of Rāhu — though radiant with splendor — oppressed and afflicted by a planet that had risen against her.

**2.106.4**  Like a mountain river grown lean — its waters warm and turbid, its birds tormented by the summer heat, its fish and crocodiles sunk low.

**2.106.5**  Like a golden flame, smokeless, that had risen from a sacrificial fire, anointed with oblation, and then subsided into extinction.

**2.106.6**  Like an army in great battle that has lost its armor, its elephants, horses, chariots, and banners shattered, its foremost heroes slain.

**2.106.7**  Like a wave of the ocean that had arisen foaming and roaring, then fallen silent — the wind that stirred it now stilled.

**2.106.8**  Like a sacrificial altar grown silent after the soma-pressing is complete, abandoned by all the ritual implements and the handsome officiating priests.

**2.106.9**  Like a leading cow standing distressed in the middle of the cowpen, no longer grazing fresh grass, yearning — abandoned by the bull.

**2.106.10**  Like a fresh pearl necklace bereft of its lustrous gems — those excellent, radiant stones with their lovely, captivating gleam.

**2.106.11**  Like a star that has suddenly left its place and fallen from the sky upon the exhaustion of its merit — its brilliance and expanse contracted.

**2.106.12**  Like a forest creeper that at the end of spring was decked in flowers and humming with intoxicated bees, then scorched and wilted by a swiftly spreading forest fire.

**2.106.13**  Like a sky veiled by rain-clouds — its moon and stars hidden — with all its merchant quarters bewildered and every market and shop shut down.

**2.106.14**  Like a drinking hall in the open air, fallen into ruin — its finest wines exhausted, littered with broken vessels, its revelry slain, uncleaned.

**2.106.15**  Like a watering station that has fallen and collapsed — its floor broken and sunken, covered with shattered vessels, its water fully used up.

**2.106.16**  Like a bowstring — broad, extended, fitted with its cord for the swift archer — cut through by arrows and fallen from the weapon to the ground.

**2.106.17**  Like a young filly, weak, ridden suddenly by a battle-fierce horseman, then dropped and abandoned — her load cast aside.

**2.106.18**  Like the light of the sun hidden in the deep of the rainy season, its disc entered by rain-clouds, concealed by dark-blue storm clouds.

**2.106.19**  But Bharata, the glorious son of Daśaratha, seated in his chariot, spoke these words to the charioteer who was driving the finest of chariots.

**2.106.20**  "Why indeed is the deep, resounding noise of song and music that Ayodhyā used to have no longer heard today as before?"

**2.106.21**  "The heady fragrance of wine, the rich scent of garlands, and the perfume of burned aguru incense — none of these waft in any direction."

**2.106.22**  "The sound of the finest vehicles, the smooth neigh of horses, the trumpeting of lordly elephants in rut, the great rumble of chariots — none of this is now heard in this city, since Rāma has been exiled."

**2.106.23**  "The broad avenues of Ayodhyā no longer shine, graced by young men in elegant dress who walk with heads held high and move swiftly about."

**2.106.24**  Speaking thus in many ways, he entered his father's dwelling — bereft of that lord of men, like a mountain cave bereft of its lion.

**2.107.1**  Then Bharata, firm in his vows and consumed by grief, having left the mothers in Ayodhyā, spoke these words to his elders.

**2.107.2**  "I shall go to Nandigrāma. I take leave of all of you today. There I will endure all this sorrow without the son of Raghu."

**2.107.3**  "For the king has gone to heaven, and my guru dwells in the forest. I wait for Rāma to return for the kingdom — he of great glory is the true king."

**2.107.4**  Having heard this noble speech of the great-souled Bharata, all the ministers and the family priest Vasiṣṭha replied.

**2.107.5**  "What you have spoken, Bharata, is fitting and praiseworthy — a word born of brotherly affection, suited to you alone."

**2.107.6**  "You are ever devoted to your kinsmen, steadfast in brotherly love, treading the path of the noble — what man would not approve of you?"

**2.107.7**  Having heard the agreeable and welcome words of the ministers, he spoke to his charioteer: "Let my chariot be yoked."

**2.107.8**  With a joyful countenance, he paid obeisance to all the mothers, and the glorious one mounted the chariot accompanied by Śatrughna.

**2.107.9**  Having swiftly mounted the chariot, both Śatrughna and Bharata set out in great joy, surrounded by ministers and the family priest.

**2.107.10**  Ahead of the city, all the twice-born with Vasiṣṭha at their head proceeded facing east, toward where Nandigrāma lay.

**2.107.11**  That army, dense with elephants, horses, and chariots, set out unbidden, and all the citizens of the city went forth as Bharata departed.

**2.107.12**  That righteous-souled Bharata, devoted to his brother, seated in the chariot and bearing Rāma's sandals upon his head, quickly proceeded to Nandigrāma.

**2.107.13**  Then Bharata swiftly entered Nandigrāma, quickly descended from the chariot, and spoke these words to his elders.

**2.107.14**  "This kingdom has been given to me by my brother himself as a trust. These golden-adorned sandals, which bear responsibility for welfare and protection, I will guard this kingdom in anticipation of the son of Raghu's return."

**2.107.15**  "Quickly and by my own hand I shall place these sandals back upon the feet of the son of Raghu and behold Rāma's two feet together with his sandals."

**2.107.16**  "Then, my burden set down, reunited with the son of Raghu, having rendered the kingdom to my guru, I shall take up the mode of life proper to a disciple."

**2.107.17**  "And having returned these excellent sandals and this kingdom and Ayodhyā itself as a trust to the son of Raghu, I shall be cleansed of sin."

**2.107.18**  "When the scion of Kākutstha is consecrated and the people rejoice with delight, my happiness and my glory will be four times greater than a kingdom."

**2.107.19**  Thus lamenting, the wretched and grief-stricken Bharata of great renown administered the kingdom from Nandigrāma together with his ministers.

**2.107.20**  The lord Bharata, that hero wearing bark garments and matted locks, clad in the guise of a sage, then resided in Nandigrāma together with his army.

**2.107.21**  Longing for Rāma's return, devoted to his brother, obedient to his brother's word, Bharata then fulfilled the vow he had taken.

**2.107.22**  Having consecrated the sandals, Bharata then resided in Nandigrāma, referring all governance to those sandals.

**2.108.1**  After Bharata had returned, Rāma, dwelling in the forest of austerities, began to perceive anxiety and agitation among the ascetics.

**2.108.2**  Those ascetics who dwelt in the hermitage in front of Citrakūṭa, engaged in their practices and taking refuge with Rāma — he observed them to be restless.

**2.108.3**  Pointing at Rāma with their eyes and with furrowed brows, anxious, they whispered to one another and exchanged hushed conversations among themselves.

**2.108.4**  Perceiving their restlessness, Rāma became anxious in his own heart, and with joined palms he then spoke to the seer, the master of the hermitage community.

**2.108.5**  "Blessed one, has something previously done by me been seen as a fault, by reason of which the ascetics are disturbed?"

**2.108.6**  "Has some act of my younger brother Lakṣmaṇa, done through carelessness, been seen by the seers as unbecoming of him?"

**2.108.7**  "Does Sītā, devoted to service toward me, who attends to you all, conduct herself with the behavior proper to a woman, as is fitting?"

**2.108.8**  Then the seer, aged with old age and grown ancient through austerity, spoke as though trembling to Rāma, who surpasses all in compassion for living beings.

**2.108.9**  "How could there be any fault, dear one, in the daughter of Videha — she of auspicious nature and auspicious disposition — toward the ascetics especially?"

**2.108.10**  "This is happening to the ascetics on account of you — it is the rākṣasas. Terrified because of them, they whisper these tales to one another."

**2.108.11**  "A certain rākṣasa named Khara, younger brother of Rāvaṇa, dwelling in Janasthāna, has been uprooting and harrying all the ascetics."

**2.108.12**  "Insolent, boastful of his victories, cruel, a devourer of men, arrogant, and wicked — he does not, dear one, tolerate you."

**2.108.13**  "From the very time that you, dear one, have been residing in this hermitage, from that time the rākṣasas have been harassing the ascetics."

**2.108.14**  "For they manifest themselves in loathsome, cruel, terrifying, and various hideous and misshapen forms that are painful to behold."

**2.108.15**  "Having defiled the ascetics with inauspicious and impure matter, those ignoble ones standing in front quickly strike down others."

**2.108.16**  "And those feeble-minded ones, hiding unseen in various hermitage sites, take delight in destroying the ascetics there."

**2.108.17**  "They hurl away the ritual ladles and vessels, douse the sacred fires with water, and crush the water-pots when the oblation rite is at hand."

**2.108.18**  "Wishing to abandon these hermitages that have been overrun by those evil-souled ones, the seers are urging me today to depart to another region."

**2.108.19**  "Before those wicked ones inflict bodily harm upon the ascetics, Rāma, we will abandon this hermitage."

**2.108.20**  "There is a forest rich in roots and fruits, wondrous and not far from here; I will once again, together with my community, take refuge in that ancient hermitage."

**2.108.21**  "Moreover, Khara perpetrates wrong against you yourself, dear one. Come away from here with us, if your mind so inclines."

**2.108.22**  "He who dwells with his wife is always beset by uncertainty, O scion of Raghu; even for one who is capable and virtuous, residence here is now painful."

**2.108.23**  The prince Rāma was unable to restrain with any words of reply that ascetic, who had spoken thus and was already eager to depart.

**2.108.24**  Welcoming Rāma, taking leave of him, and consoling the son of Raghu, the master of the hermitage community departed, abandoning the hermitage together with his groups.

**2.108.25**  Rāma, having escorted the band of seers away from that region and dissuading the master of the hermitage community from following further, saluted the seer properly; approved by those joyful ones and instructed in his purpose, he proceeded to his own blessed dwelling to reside there.

**2.108.26**  The lord, that son of Raghu, did not abandon even for a moment the hermitage now deprived of its seers; for the ascetics, holding fast to the virtues proper to the conduct of seers, constantly followed after the son of Raghu.

**2.109.1**  When the ascetics had departed, Rāghava, reflecting upon many reasons, did not find it agreeable to remain there any longer.

**2.109.2**  "Here Bharata saw me, and the mothers with all the townspeople — that memory follows me always, as I grieve for them continually."

**2.109.3**  The encampment of that great-souled one's army, with the dung of horses and elephants, had greatly trampled and polluted the ground of this hermitage.

**2.109.4**  Having deliberated thus — "therefore let us go elsewhere" — Rāghava set out, accompanied by the daughter of Videha and by Lakṣmaṇa.

**2.109.5**  Reaching the hermitage of Atri, that greatly renowned one paid homage to him; and the blessed Atri in turn received him as a son.

**2.109.6**  Having himself directed the hospitality so that everything was fittingly honoured for him, Atri also comforted the highly blessed Saumitrī and Sītā.

**2.109.7**  Addressing his aged wife who had come to meet them, and having honoured her well, that knower of dharma, who was devoted to the welfare of all beings, entreated her to comfort Sītā.

**2.109.8**  The foremost among sages said: "Receive the daughter of Videha — Anasūyā, the greatly blessed female ascetic, the one who walks in dharma."

**2.109.9**  He declared to Rāma concerning that female ascetic who walks in dharma: "When the world was scorched without respite by ten years of drought,

**2.109.10**  she, endowed with fierce austerity and adorned with religious vows, created roots and fruits and caused the Jāhnavī to flow.

**2.109.11**  By her the great austerity of ten thousand years was practised, dear one, and through the vows of Anasūyā all obstacles were destroyed.

**2.109.12**  She, acting swiftly for the sake of the gods' purpose, compressed ten nights into one night — she is like a mother to you, O sinless one.

**2.109.13**  Let the daughter of Videha approach this venerable, illustrious one, who is worthy of reverence by all beings and who is ever free from anger."

**2.109.14**  When the sage spoke thus, Rāghava replied "So be it" and then addressed these excellent words to Sītā, who knows dharma:

**2.109.15**  "Princess, you have heard what this sage has said. For your own good, go quickly to that female ascetic.

**2.109.16**  She who is known in the world as Anasūyā by virtue of her deeds — go quickly to that female ascetic, who is worthy of approach."

**2.109.17**  Hearing these words of Rāghava, who wished her well, the daughter of Mithilā, Sītā, approached that wife of Atri, the knower of dharma.

**2.109.18**  She was frail, wrinkled, and old, with hair whitened by age, her limbs trembling constantly like a plantain tree in the wind.

**2.109.19**  Sītā, undisturbed, paid reverence to that greatly blessed Anasūyā, the devoted wife, and announced her own name.

**2.109.20**  And having paid homage, the daughter of Videha, with palms joined in a cupped gesture, delightedly enquired after the well-being of that blameless female ascetic.

**2.109.21**  Then, seeing that greatly blessed Sītā, the one who walks in dharma, Anasūyā cheerfully spoke words of comfort: "By good fortune you regard dharma!

**2.109.22**  O Sītā, O noble lady, by good fortune you follow Rāma into the forest, giving up your kinsfolk, your pride, and your prosperity, though he is confined there.

**2.109.23**  Whether dwelling in a city or in the forest, whether wicked or inauspicious — women for whom the husband is dear, their worlds are of great prosperity.

**2.109.24**  Whether ill-natured, given over to desire, or destitute of wealth — the husband is the supreme deity for women of noble nature, O noble lady.

**2.109.25**  When I reflect, I see no kinsman more distinguished than this, O daughter of Videha — one fit in every way, like austerity performed that bears imperishable fruit.

**2.109.26**  But wicked women do not understand this distinction between virtue and fault — those who behave guided by their hearts' desires and idle talk, though their lord is the husband.

**2.109.27**  Women who are indeed of such a kind, having fallen under the sway of improper conduct, acquire infamy and fall from dharma, O daughter of Mithilā.

**2.109.28**  But women endowed with virtues like yours, who perceive the higher and lower worlds, will move in heaven just as those who have performed meritorious deeds."

**2.110.1**  Thus addressed by Anasūyā, the daughter of Videha, honouring her words without jealousy, began to speak gently in reply:

**2.110.2**  "What you say to me, noble lady, is no wonder — for this too is known to me: how the husband is the teacher for a woman.

**2.110.3**  Even if this husband of mine were devoid of proper conduct, O noble lady, he would nonetheless have to be served by me without duplicity.

**2.110.4**  How much more so one who is praiseworthy for his virtues, compassionate, master of his senses, steadfast in affection, righteous in spirit, dutiful to his mothers, and beloved of his father.

**2.110.5**  The same conduct that the mighty Rāma observes toward Kausalyā, he observes likewise toward the other queens and royal women.

**2.110.6**  Even toward women seen only once, that hero dear to kings, the knower of dharma, laying aside pride, conducts himself as toward a mother.

**2.110.7**  And coming to this lonely and dreadful forest, I hold within my heart what was collected and placed there by my mother-in-law.

**2.110.8**  And the words with which my mother instructed me formerly at the time of the giving of my hand, in the presence of the sacred fire — those words too are firmly held in my mind.

**2.110.9**  All of that has been renewed by your words, O one who walks in dharma. For a woman there is no austerity prescribed other than devoted service to her husband.

**2.110.10**  Sāvitrī is honoured in heaven for having rendered devoted service to her husband, and in like manner you have gone to heaven through devoted service to your husband.

**2.110.11**  She is the foremost among all women, and this goddess too in heaven — Rohiṇī is not seen even for a moment without the moon.

**2.110.12**  And thus such eminent women, firm in their vow to their husbands, are honoured in the world of the gods by their own meritorious deeds."

**2.110.13**  Then Anasūyā, greatly delighted upon hearing Sītā's words, smelled her on the head and spoke, gladdening the daughter of Mithilā:

**2.110.14**  "I possess great austerity acquired through various religious vows; relying on the power thereof, O Sītā, O one of pure observance, I desire to grant you a boon.

**2.110.15**  Your speech, O daughter of Mithilā, is fitting and proper; I am pleased — what fitting thing shall I do for you? Tell me" — thus Sītā spoke to her who was endowed with the power of austerity.

**2.110.16**  That knower of dharma, thus addressed by her, became even more pleased, and said: "Ah, O Sītā, I shall indeed make your delight bear fruit!

**2.110.17**  This excellent divine garland, this garment and ornaments, and this fragrant ointment, O daughter of Videha, this precious unguent —

**2.110.18**  given by me, this shall adorn your limbs, O Sītā; suited to you, unstained, it will always remain so forever.

**2.110.19**  Anointed with the divine fragrant ointment, O daughter of Janaka, you will delight your husband as Śrī delights the eternal Viṣṇu."

**2.110.20**  The daughter of Mithilā accepted the garment, the fragrant ointment, the ornaments, and the garlands — that incomparable gift of affection.

**2.110.21**  Having received that gift of affection, the illustrious Sītā, steadfast, with hands joined and cupped together, sat beside that one whose wealth is austerity.

**2.110.22**  Thus, with Sītā seated beside her, Anasūyā of firm vow began to ask a pleasing question:

**2.110.23**  "I have heard the story, O Sītā, that you were won at the svayaṃvara by this illustrious Rāghava.

**2.110.24**  I wish to hear that story in full, O daughter of Mithilā. You ought to tell me the whole of it as you experienced it."

**2.110.25**  Thus addressed, Sītā said to that one who walks in dharma, "Listen then," and narrated the story:

**2.110.26**  "The heroic Janaka by name, lord of Mithilā, a knower of dharma, devoted to the duties of the warrior order, rules the earth justly.

**2.110.27**  As he, plough in hand, was tilling the field-plot, I, they say, arose, cleaving the earth — the daughter of the king.

**2.110.28**  Seeing me, the king — intent on the throwing of clods — with all my limbs covered in dust, Janaka was astonished.

**2.110.29**  Being childless, he took me with affection onto his own lap, saying "This is my daughter" — and his love fell upon me.

**2.110.30**  And from the sky an incomparable, superhuman voice spoke: "Indeed so, O king — by dharma this is your daughter."

**2.110.31**  Then my father, the righteous lord of Mithilā, greatly rejoiced, and that lord of men, having obtained me, attained great prosperity.

**2.110.32**  He entrusted me to his elder queen as one beloved, through her meritorious deeds, and I was honoured by her with tender maternal affection.

**2.110.33**  But seeing that my marriageable age, easily attained through union with a husband, was at hand, my father fell into anxiety, helpless as a pauper at the loss of wealth.

**2.110.34**  The father of a maiden is subject to insult in the world from those of equal or inferior rank — even if he be equal to Indra on earth.

**2.110.35**  Seeing that insult as not far off and perceiving it within himself, the king, having entered the ocean of anxiety, could not reach the farther shore, like one without a boat.

**2.110.36**  For knowing me to be womb-unborn, that guardian of the earth, reflecting and reflecting, could not find a husband for me who was equal and fitting.

**2.110.37**  This thought arose in that wise one, as he deliberated without cease: 'I shall conduct a svayaṃvara for my daughter.'

**2.110.38**  At a great sacrifice, the great-souled Varuṇa had then given to him, with affection, an excellent bow and two inexhaustible quivers of arrows.

**2.110.39**  It was immovable by men, and even by great effort on account of its heaviness kings were unable to bend it even in their dreams.

**2.110.40**  Having obtained that bow, my truth-speaking father declared, first summoning the lords of men and kings together:

**2.110.41**  'The man who lifts this bow and strings it — his wife my daughter shall become, without doubt.'

**2.110.42**  And seeing that most excellent bow, mountainlike in its heaviness, the kings paid homage and departed, unable to lift it.

**2.110.43**  But after a very long time, this Rāghava of great splendour came to behold the sacrifice, accompanied by Viśvāmitra.

**2.110.44**  Rāma of truly heroic prowess came with his brother Lakṣmaṇa; and the righteous Viśvāmitra was fittingly honoured by my father.

**2.110.45**  He spoke to my father there: 'These two sons of Daśaratha, Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa, are desirous of beholding the bow.' Addressed thus by that brahmin, my father caused the bow to be brought forth.

**2.110.46**  In a mere instant that mighty one bent it, and with a swift motion strung the bowstring — that mighty one fully drew it.

**2.110.47**  As he drew it forcefully, the bow broke in two in the middle; its sound was dreadful, like that of a falling thunderbolt.

**2.110.48**  Then I was there being offered in bestowal to Rāma by my father, true to his word, who took up the excellent vessel of water.

**2.110.49**  But Rāghava did not accept me as I was being given, not having learned the wish of his father, the lord and master of Ayodhyā.

**2.110.50**  Then, having invited the aged king Daśaratha as father-in-law, I was given by my father to Rāma, the knower of the self.

**2.110.51**  And my younger sister, the virtuous and beautiful Ūrmilā, was also given by my father himself to Lakṣmaṇa as wife.

**2.110.52**  Thus was I given to Rāma at that svayaṃvara, and by dharma I am devoted to that husband, the best among the mighty."

**2.111.1**  Anasūyā, the knower of dharma, having heard that great story, embraced the daughter of Mithilā with both arms and smelled her on the head.

**2.111.2**  "Your speech has been clear of syllable and word, wonderful, sweet — the whole of how the svayaṃvara transpired has been heard by me.

**2.111.3**  I indeed delight greatly in your story, O sweet-spoken one; the glorious sun has set, having brought on the auspicious night.

**2.111.4**  The birds that scatter abroad each day in search of food — at twilight, having settled for sleep, their calls are heard.

**2.111.5**  These sages too, wet from their ablutions, having purified their fruits, come together, their bark garments drenched with water.

**2.111.6**  From the oblation-fires of the sages, after the ritual offerings have been made, smoke the colour of a dove's wing is seen, lifted by the wind.

**2.111.7**  The trees, sparse-leafed yet dense in their mass on all sides, even at a distance make the directions invisible.

**2.111.8**  Creatures of the night's essence move about on all sides; and these animals of the ascetic grove lie down at the banks of the sacrificial altar.

**2.111.9**  Night has set in, O Sītā, adorned with constellations; the moon, wrapped in its own moonlight, is seen having risen in the sky.

**2.111.10**  Go — I give you leave; be the companion of Rāma. I have been fully satisfied by you as you spoke so sweetly.

**2.111.11**  And do adorn yourself now before my very eyes, O daughter of Mithilā — bring me joy, dear child, shining with the divine ornaments."

**2.111.12**  Then Sītā, having adorned herself, she who is like a daughter of the gods, bowed her head to Anasūyā and went toward Rāma.

**2.111.13**  The best among the eloquent, Rāghava, beheld Sītā thus adorned and rejoiced at the gift of affection from the female ascetic.

**2.111.14**  Then the daughter of Mithilā, Sītā, reported to Rāma all the gift of affection from the female ascetic — the garment, ornaments, and garlands.

**2.111.15**  Rāma and the great warrior Lakṣmaṇa were overjoyed upon seeing the honour rendered to the daughter of Mithilā — something exceedingly rare among humans.

**2.111.16**  Then, pleased, that moon-faced joy of the Raghu line passed that auspicious night there, honoured by the siddha ascetics.

**2.111.17**  When that night had passed and the sacred fires had been anointed and the offerings made, those two tigers among men took leave of the ascetics who ranged the forest.

**2.111.18**  Those forest-ranging ascetics, walkers in dharma, spoke to them of the passage through that forest, which was beset by rākṣasas.

**2.111.19**  "This is the path of the great sages who fetch fruits in the forest; by this path, O Rāghava, it is fitting for you to enter the difficult forest."

**2.111.20**  Thus, blessed by those ascetic brahmins with joined palms who had performed the rite of benediction, that scorcher of foes, Rāghava, with his wife and Lakṣmaṇa, entered the forest like the sun entering a mass of clouds.
